He will burn in a fire of blazing flame, and his wife, the carrier of firewood, around her neck a rope of twisted palm fiber* (al-Masad 111). A chapter of the Quran was given over to a single man's rejection, sealing his ruin while he yet lived. Abū Lahab lived on for years a disbeliever, the prophecy standing over him unbroken until his death confirmed it.

To grasp the Meccan years one must grasp the shape of the message that was descending, for it was not yet a code of law but an overturning of the way a human being saw the world. The early Meccan chapters were short and thunderous and built for the memory and the tongue, and they struck at three things together. They struck at the gods, declaring that there was no deity but God, one, without partner or daughter or rival, the creator the idolaters themselves admitted and then buried under their brokers. They struck at the denial of the reckoning, hammering on the resurrection and the Day with an urgency that left no room for the comfortable Meccan certainty that death was the end and wealth was the measure. And they struck at the moral order of the city, at the hoarding of wealth and the neglect of the orphan and the contempt for the weak. The recitation swore by the declining day that man was in loss, *by time, indeed mankind is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds and counsel one another to truth and counsel one another to patience* (al-ʿAṣr 103). It warned the one who piled up wealth and counted it, thinking it would make him immortal (al-Humazah 104:1-3). It described the Day when the earth would convulse and give up its burdens and every atom of good and evil would be seen, *so whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it* (al-Zalzalah 99:7-8). It painted the splitting of the sky and the scattering of the stars and the seas set boiling, and asked the heedless man what had deceived him about his generous Lord (al-Infiṭār 82:1-6). This was the substance of the offense. It was not merely a new god. It was a new accounting, in which the proud were brought low and the buried daughter was asked for her crime and the hoarded gold became a fire. The men who ran Mecca understood at once that if it were true their whole world was overturned.

And to carry these claims the recitation did not merely assert them. It turned the eye of its hearers upon the world they already lived in, making the whole of creation its evidence. Against a people who admitted that God had made the heavens and the earth and then prayed to stones beside Him, it pointed again and again to the order of the made world as the standing proof of the One who made it, asking them to look at what was before their faces. *Do they not look at the camels, how they are created, and at the sky, how it is raised, and at the mountains, how they are set, and at the earth, how it is spread* (al-Ghāshiyah 88:17-20). It drew the signs from the most ordinary things, from the rain that revives the dead earth and the turning of the night into the day, from the sleep that is a little death and the waking that is a little raising, from the diversity of human tongues and colors, every one of them a sign for a people who would reflect. *And of His signs is that He created you from dust, then at once you are human beings spreading out. And of His signs is that He created for you mates from among yourselves that you may find rest in them, and He placed between you affection and mercy. And of His signs is the creation of the heavens and the earth and the difference of your languages and your colors* (al-Rūm 30:20-22). And it promised that the proof would only grow plainer with time, in the wide world and in the body of the seer himself, until denial had nowhere left to stand. *We will show them Our signs in the horizons and within their own selves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth* (Fuṣṣilat 41:53). The strangest of these signs was the nearest, the one each person carried in his own making and overlooked because it stood too close to be seen, the standing wonder of his own existence. Toward it the recitation kept returning the heedless eye, for the mystery a man chased across the far horizon was breathing in him the whole time.

The message also leveled, and the leveling reached even the Prophet ﷺ himself in a rebuke the recitation did not spare him. One day he was occupied with the powerful men of Quraysh whom he hoped to win, and he turned away from a poor blind man, Ibn Umm Maktūm, who had come seeking knowledge. The correction came down upon the Prophet ﷺ in the third person, as a lasting verse in the Book that all would recite. *He frowned and turned away, because the blind man came to him. And what would make you know, perhaps he would be purified, or be reminded so the reminder would benefit him. As for the one who considers himself self-sufficient, to him you give attention, though it is not upon you that he is not purified. But as for the one who came to you striving, while he fears, from him you are distracted* (ʿAbasa 80:1-10). The revelation that exalted the Prophet ﷺ also corrected him before his whole community, and the honesty of that is itself a sign, for a man fabricating a scripture to glorify himself does not write into it the record of his own error. And when Quraysh, unable to silence him, sought a compromise, offering to worship his God if he would worship theirs in turn, the answer refused every blending. *Say, O disbelievers, I do not worship what you worship, nor are you worshippers of what I worship, nor will I be a worshipper of what you worship, nor will you be worshippers of what I worship. For you your religion, and for me my religion* (al-Kāfirūn 109). There would be no half-measure between the One and the three hundred and sixty.

## The Tortured

Mecca answered the message first with mockery, then with pressure, then with torture, and the torture fell hardest on those the city could harm without fear of their clans, the slaves and the poor and the friendless among the believers. The biographers and the sound reports together preserve the names. Bilāl, the Abyssinian slave, was laid on the burning sand under a great rock by his master and told to renounce the One God, and he answered only, One, One, refusing under the stone to name another, until Abū Bakr bought him and set him free. Abū Bakr spent his wealth buying and freeing the tortured slaves of the believers, and the recitation would later honor exactly such giving that seeks nothing but the face of the Most High (al-Layl 92:18-21, in the reports tied to him). The family of Yāsir bore the worst. Yāsir and his wife Sumayyah and their son ʿAmmār were tortured to force them from the faith. The Prophet ﷺ, the biographers transmit, would pass them under their torment and say, patience, family of Yāsir, your appointed place is Paradise. Sumayyah was killed under torture by Abū Jahl, and she became the first martyr of Islam, an aged woman who would not deny her Lord, the first of all who would die for this faith down the centuries. ʿAmmār, pressed past endurance, was made to utter words of disbelief with his tongue to save his life, and came to the Prophet ﷺ in anguish. The revelation eased him, exempting the one whose tongue is forced while his heart holds firm. *Whoever disbelieves in God after his belief, except one who is compelled while his heart is at rest with faith, but those who open their breasts to disbelief, upon them is wrath from God* (al-Naḥl 16:106). The faith was learning, in blood, the difference between the heart and the tongue, and the Book was marking it down.

## The Crossing to Abyssinia

When the persecution grew unbearable the Prophet ﷺ told the weakest and most exposed of the believers to cross the sea to Abyssinia, to the land of the Christian king the Arabs called the Negus, of whom he said that there ruled a king under whom no one was wronged. A first small party went, and then a larger, some eighty in time, the first migration of Islam, leaving the sanctuary of their birth for the protection of a Christian throne. Quraysh, alarmed that their runaways had found refuge, sent an embassy after them with gifts to demand their return. What followed is preserved in the sīra on the narration of Umm Salamah, who was there, and it is one of the great meetings of the early faith. The Negus summoned the believers and asked them to account for the new religion that had cut them off from their people without bringing them into his. Their spokesman was Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib, the Prophet's cousin, and his answer became a portrait of Islam drawn for a Christian king. We were a people of ignorance, he said, worshipping idols, eating carrion, committing shameful deeds, severing kinship, the strong devouring the weak, until God sent among us a man we knew for his truth and trust and purity, who called us to worship God alone and abandon the stones, to speak truth, keep trust, honor kinship, and cease from bloodshed and the wrongful eating of the orphan's wealth.

The Negus asked whether Jaʿfar had with him anything of what had come to the Prophet ﷺ from God, and Jaʿfar recited to him the opening of the chapter of Mary, the Quranic account of the Christian's own Lord, of Zechariah (AS) and John (AS), and of the virgin and her son. *And mention in the Book Mary, when she withdrew from her family to a place toward the east. Then she took, apart from them, a screen, and We sent to her Our spirit, and it appeared to her as a well-made man* (Maryam 19:16-17). The chapter goes on through the annunciation, the birth beneath the palm, and the infant Jesus (AS) speaking from the cradle in defense of his mother. *He said, indeed I am the servant of God. He has given me the Scripture and made me a prophet, and made me blessed wherever I am, and has enjoined upon me prayer and charity as long as I live, and dutifulness to my mother, and He has not made me arrogant or wretched* (Maryam 19:30-32). The Negus wept until his beard was wet, and his bishops wept, and he said that what Jaʿfar had recited and what Jesus (AS) brought came from the one lamp, and he refused to surrender the believers to Quraysh, returning the embassy's gifts. The first refuge of Islam was granted by a Christian who heard the Quran's account of Christ and recognized the same light, and the believers remained safe under him for years. The episode is sīra in its detailed narration, devotedly preserved, and it carries a truth the later centuries of dispute would obscure, that the recitation honored Jesus (AS) and Mary so highly that a Christian king wept to hear it.

## The Years of the Siege and the Sorrow

Unable to reach the Prophet ﷺ while his clan protected him, Quraysh turned to pressure the whole clan of Hāshim, believers and unbelievers together. They imposed a boycott, a written ban posted at the Kaʿba forbidding any to marry the people of Hāshim, to trade with them, or to deal with them at all, until they surrendered the Prophet ﷺ to be killed. For about three years the clan withdrew into the valley quarter, the shiʿb of Abū Ṭālib, hemmed in and hungry. The children cried with hunger by the biographers' reports, and the believers and the still-unbelieving kin of the Prophet ﷺ endured together for the bond of blood. The boycott broke at last when men of Quraysh, ashamed, moved to annul it, and the document was found, by the tradition's account, eaten away. But the years had worn down the two who had sheltered him most.

In the space of a short time, in the year the tradition calls the Year of Sorrow, the Prophet ﷺ lost both Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib. Khadījah, who had believed first and stood by him through every blow, died after a quarter century of marriage, and with her went the human refuge of his home and the great consolation of his heart. Abū Ṭālib, the uncle who had protected him for forty years against all Quraysh, lay dying. The Prophet ﷺ came to him and pleaded with him at the last to say the word of faith so that he might intercede for him, and Abū Ṭālib, with the chiefs of Quraysh at his bedside shaming him from the religion of his fathers, died upon the way of his fathers. The grief of it reached into the recitation, for the Prophet ﷺ longed to guide the uncle who had sheltered him, and the verse came down to teach him the limit of even a prophet's reach. *Indeed you do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills, and He knows best the rightly guided* (al-Qaṣaṣ 28:56). The guidance of hearts belonged to God alone, and not even the Messenger ﷺ could place faith in a beloved heart that refused it. He had lost his wife and his protector in one season, and the city, sensing him exposed, pressed in harder.

Bereft and besieged, he carried the message out of Mecca to the town of Ṭāʾif in the hills, hoping its people might receive what Mecca refused. They refused it more cruelly, setting their fools and children to pelt him with stones until his feet ran with blood. He withdrew, broken, and made the prayer the biographers preserve as among the most moving he ever uttered, complaining to God alone of his weakness and his helplessness before men, asking into whose care God would entrust him, and declaring that if God were not angry with him he did not care, for God's favor was wide enough for him. And the sound tradition, in Bukhārī on the narration of ʿĀʾishah, preserves what came next. When ʿĀʾishah later asked him whether he had known a day harder than the day of Uḥud, he answered that he had suffered much from Ṭāʾif, and that the angel of the mountains had been sent to him there, offering to crush the people of the two mountains between them if he wished. He refused, saying rather that he hoped God would bring forth from their loins those who would worship God alone and associate nothing with Him. At the lowest point of his life, with his blood on the ground and the power to destroy his persecutors offered to him by an angel, he chose instead to hope for the faith of their grandchildren. The mercy to the worlds had been struck at its limit and answered with mercy.

## The Night Journey

It was in this season of sorrow and rejection, by the strong reckoning of the tradition, that the Prophet ﷺ was given the journey that lifted him above all of it, the night journey and the ascension, the Isrāʾ and the Miʿrāj. The recitation opens its account in a single verse of glory. *Glory to the One who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque, whose surroundings We have blessed, to show him of Our signs. Indeed He is the Hearing, the Seeing* (al-Isrāʾ 17:1). In one night he was carried from the Kaʿba at Mecca to the sanctuary at Jerusalem, the Farthest Mosque, and from there raised through the heavens into the presence of his Lord. The sound collections of Bukhārī and Muslim preserve the ascent in detail, on the narrations of Anas and others, and the deeper meaning of what was seen belongs to a later Book of this work, where the unseen horizon is taken up on its own. Here the event stands in its place in the life. He was carried on a mount the tradition names al-Burāq, and at Jerusalem he led the prophets who had gone before him in prayer, the seal of them praying at their head. Then he was raised through the seven heavens, meeting the prophets stationed in them, Adam in the nearest, and Jesus (AS) and John (AS), and Joseph (AS), and Idrīs (AS), and Aaron (AS), and Moses (AS), and Abraham (AS) at the seventh by the House that the angels circle as men circle the House below. He was taken to the lote tree of the utmost boundary, the sidrat al-muntahā, beyond which none may pass, of which the chapter of the star says *the sight did not swerve, nor did it transgress, he certainly saw of the greatest signs of his Lord* (al-Najm 53:17-18). And there the prayers were ordained, fifty in the day and night, until Moses (AS), by the narration, urged him to return and ask his Lord for relief, knowing the weakness of men. The Prophet ﷺ returned again and again until the fifty were reduced to five in deed and fifty in reward. The five daily prayers, the spine of the believer's day, were brought down not by an angel to the earth but received by the Prophet ﷺ himself in the highest heaven, the one ordinance the tradition holds was given above all the others, in the presence of God. When he returned and told Mecca of the night, the disbelievers mocked him, and some who had believed faltered, but Abū Bakr, hearing it, said that if the Prophet ﷺ had said it then it was true, and earned the name al-Ṣiddīq, the great affirmer of truth. The man rejected by Ṭāʾif and bereaved of his wife and uncle had been raised, in the same season, above the seventh heaven.

## The Pledges at Aqaba

The opening, when it came, came from the north, from the town that would become Medina. The oasis of Yathrib was torn by a long and exhausting feud between its two Arab tribes, the Aws and the Khazraj, and it lived alongside its established Jewish tribes who spoke of a coming prophet. It was a soil prepared in a way Mecca was not. At the season of pilgrimage the Prophet ﷺ would present himself to the tribes camped around Mecca, calling them to God. A small group of men from Khazraj heard him and recognized in him, the reports say, the prophet their Jewish neighbors had foretold, and they believed. They returned to Yathrib and spread the faith, and the next year a delegation came to meet the Prophet ﷺ secretly at a place called ʿAqabah near Mecca. There twelve of them gave him a pledge, the first pledge of ʿAqabah, swearing to worship God alone, to associate nothing with Him, not to steal or commit unlawful sexual intercourse, not to kill their children, not to slander, and to obey him in what was right, a pledge the sound tradition records in nearly the same terms as the later pledges of the women. He sent a teacher back with them to instruct the new community in the recitation, and the faith spread through the houses of Yathrib until there was hardly a house without it.

The following year a far larger group came, over seventy men and two women of the Aws and the Khazraj, and at the same ʿAqabah, in the dark of night, they gave the second pledge. It was a pledge of a different order, for it was a pledge of protection and of war. They swore to receive the Prophet ﷺ into their city and to defend him as they defended their own wives and children, against all comers, knowing that to shelter him was to declare war on all of Arabia. One of them asked aloud what they would have if they kept the pledge and lost everything, and the answer was Paradise. They gave their hands on it. The believers of Mecca, hunted and worn, now had a city that had sworn to die for them. The Prophet ﷺ told them to depart for Yathrib, and quietly, family by family, the Muslims of Mecca began to slip away to the north, until almost none were left but the Prophet ﷺ, Abū Bakr, ʿAlī, and those held back by force or weakness.

## The Migration

Quraysh saw the city emptying of believers and understood that the Prophet ﷺ was about to escape their reach into a fortified town that would shield him, and they resolved to end it before he could go. They gathered in the council house and settled on a plan the recitation itself would describe, that a young man of strength from every clan should strike him together, so that his blood would be spread across all the clans and his own clan of Hāshim could not avenge it against any single house. The Quran records the night and the One who outmatched their scheme. *And when those who disbelieved plotted against you, to imprison you or kill you or drive you out. They plot, and God plots, and God is the best of plotters* (al-Anfāl 8:30). The Prophet ﷺ, warned, had ʿAlī lie in his bed in his cloak to deceive the watchers. ʿAlī, a young man, gave his body to the assassins' watch so the Prophet ﷺ could slip out, and was left also to return the trusts that the very people seeking to kill the Prophet ﷺ had still left in his keeping, for they trusted the Trustworthy with their valuables even as they plotted his death. He went to Abū Bakr, who had prepared two mounts and wept with joy at being chosen as the companion of the road, and the two of them left Mecca and went not north but south, to a cave in the mountain of Thawr, to hide while the search raged.

The hours in the cave of Thawr became one of the most beloved scenes of the faith, and the Quran sealed it into the Book in the verse of the second of two. The pursuers came so close that Abū Bakr, by the sound narration in Bukhārī, could see their feet at the mouth of the cave and feared for the Prophet ﷺ, whispering that if one of them merely looked down at his feet he would see them. And the Prophet ﷺ answered him with the words the recitation would enshrine, what do you think of two whose third is God. The Quran gives the moment its eternal frame. *If you do not aid him, God has already aided him, when those who disbelieved drove him out, the second of two, when they were in the cave, when he said to his companion, do not grieve, indeed God is with us. So God sent down His tranquility upon him and supported him with soldiers you did not see* (al-Tawba 9:40). The tranquility came down, the searchers turned away, and the two emerged after three nights and took the desert tracks toward Yathrib. The tradition would later love to tell of a spider's web spun and doves nesting at the cave's mouth to make it seem long undisturbed, a detail of weaker chain that the community has cherished though it does not rest on the strongest reports, while the core of the scene, the two in the cave, the nearness of God, and the descended tranquility, stands in the Book itself.

On the road a Meccan named Surāqah ibn Mālik, lured by the bounty Quraysh had set on the Prophet's head, gave chase on his horse, and the sound tradition in Bukhārī carries what happened. As he closed the distance his horse's legs sank into the firm ground beneath him, and again when he rose and pursued, until he understood that he was held back, and he called out for safety and turned from hunter to suppliant. The Prophet ﷺ, fleeing for his life with a price on his head, promised the man who had come to kill him that he would one day wear the bracelets of Khosrow, the king of Persia. Years after the Prophet ﷺ had died, when the armies of Islam had broken the very Persia that had once seemed eternal, the bracelets and the regalia of the Persian king were brought to Medina, and Surāqah was called and the bracelets of Khosrow were placed on his arms. The promise made on the road of the Hijra by a hunted fugitive was kept against the treasure of a fallen empire. The thread runs straight back to the two exhausted giants with which this history began, for the God who had foretold Rome's return over Persia in the chapter of the Romans now set a Bedouin pursuer's hands inside the bracelets of the Persian king.

He came at last to the outskirts of Yathrib, to the village of Qubāʾ, and the people of the city, who had been going out each morning to watch the road for him, met him with a joy the tradition remembers in song and welcome. He stayed at Qubāʾ and there laid the foundation of the first mosque of Islam, a mosque the recitation would later honor as founded upon piety from the first day (al-Tawba 9:108). Then he entered the city itself, and let his she-camel choose the place where she would kneel, and bought the ground where she stopped, and there he built his mosque and his household, the heart of what the city would become. Yathrib would take a new name from his coming, Madīnat al-Nabī, the City of the Prophet, Medina. The orphan of Quraysh, who had begun this life fatherless in a barren valley and had spent forty years as the Trustworthy and thirteen as the rejected warner, had crossed the empty road north and arrived in a city that had sworn to defend him with its life. The Meccan night was over. From the date of this crossing the Muslims would one day reckon their years, for the Hijra, the migration, was the hinge on which the faith turned from a persecuted voice into a community with a home. What that community would build, and the wars and the laws and the covenant that would make it, belongs to the next Book.

# III · THE STATESMAN OF MEDINA

*In Mecca he had been a warner with no power but the truth. In Medina he became something the world had not seen, a prophet who was also a head of state, a lawgiver, a judge, a commander, and the architect of a community bound not by blood but by a creed and a written covenant. The ten years between the Hijra and his death compressed into themselves the founding of a polity, the writing of its constitution, the fighting of its wars, and the cleansing of the House for which the whole story had been waiting. This is the Book of the statesman. The recitation that came down across these years came down no longer as warning alone but as law, as battle order, as the constitution of a people learning, often through their own failures, to be the community the Book described.*

## The City of the Prophet

The first work in Medina was not war but building, in stone and in bond. He raised his mosque on the ground his camel had chosen, a simple structure of palm trunks and mud brick. It had a shaded portion where the poorest of the emigrants, the people of the bench, the Ahl al-Ṣuffah, would live and learn, and beside it stood the chambers of his household. The mosque was not only a place of prayer. It was the assembly, the court, the school, and the seat of government of the new community, and from it the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ governed. He instituted the call to prayer, the adhān, after consultation, choosing the human voice to summon the believers rather than a horn or a bell, and Bilāl, who had said only One under the torturing rock, became the first to raise it.

Then he bound the community together by an act of deliberate engineering against the deepest law of Arabia, the law of blood. The emigrants from Mecca, the Muhājirūn, had arrived stripped of their property and their kin, strangers in a strange city. The believers of Medina, the Anṣār, the Helpers, had homes and date-groves and families. The Prophet ﷺ paired them, man with man, in a brotherhood, the muʾākhāh, in which each Helper took an emigrant into his house and his livelihood as though he were kin. The recitation honored the Helpers for a generosity that has few equals in the record of any people. *And those who were settled in the home and in faith before them, they love those who emigrated to them and find no want in their breasts for what they were given, and give preference over themselves even though they are in need. And whoever is protected from the stinginess of his soul, those are the successful* (al-Ḥashr 59:9). The recitation made the bond a matter of law and not only of love, declaring the emigrants and the helpers allies of one another, *indeed those who believed and emigrated and strove with their wealth and their lives in the cause of God, and those who gave shelter and aid, they are allies of one another* (al-Anfāl 8:72). A society was being built whose primary tie was no longer the tribe into which a man was born but the faith he had chosen, and that reordering of the foundations of human loyalty is among the most radical things the Prophet ﷺ did.

Over this community, and over the city as a whole, he set a written constitution, the document the sources preserve as the Ṣaḥīfah and that the historians of law have called the first written constitution of its kind. It bound the believers, emigrant and helper, into a single ummah, a single political community. And it brought the Jewish tribes of Medina into the same covenant as a confederated people, with their religion to themselves, sharing in the defense of the city, contributing to its costs in war, and bound to mutual aid against any who attacked Medina. It abolished the isolated blood feud in favor of collective responsibility under the new order, and it named the final reference for any serious dispute, that whatever the parties differed upon was to be referred to God and to Muhammad ﷺ. A city of feuding tribes and three faiths was given, in a single document, a constitution that made it one polity with one defense and one supreme arbitration, while leaving each community its own law in its own affairs. The man who had joined the clans at the Black Stone with a shared cloak now joined a whole city with a written word.

That a state founded on the recitation should leave the Jews of the city their own religion and their own law was not a concession wrung from weakness but a thing the recitation laid down in principle, for it forbade the forcing of faith in the plainest words it uses anywhere. *There is no compulsion in religion. The right course has become clear from error* (al-Baqarah 2:256). Belief is the turning of the heart, and a heart cannot be turned by the sword, and the recitation knew it, asking the Prophet ﷺ whether he would compel what God had left free. *And had your Lord willed, all who are on the earth would have believed, all of them together. So will you compel the people until they become believers* (Yūnus 10:99). His office was to carry the message and to make it plain, not to drive men into it, the recitation fixing the limit again and again, that he was a reminder and not a tyrant over them. The God who sent him stood in need of no one's faith, rich beyond the worlds, and the worth of a faith lay in its being freely given, the uncoerced yes of a soul that could have said no. So the city the Prophet ﷺ bound into one polity was not bound into one creed. It was given one peace and one defense and one law of the common life, and within it the freedom to believe or to refuse was left where the recitation had placed it, in the keeping of each soul before its Lord.

## The Change of the Qibla

For the first period in Medina the believers had prayed toward Jerusalem, the sanctuary of the earlier prophets, as they had in the last Meccan years. Then, in the second year, came a command that turned the body of the community in a new direction and announced its independence as a people in the line of Abraham (AS). The Prophet ﷺ had been turning his face to the sky in a kind of longing, and the recitation answered the longing by turning the qibla, the direction of prayer, from Jerusalem to the Kaʿba, the House of Abraham (AS) at Mecca. *We have certainly seen the turning of your face toward the heaven, so We will surely turn you to a qibla with which you will be pleased. So turn your face toward the Sacred Mosque, and wherever you are, turn your faces toward it* (al-Baqarah 2:144). The recitation knew the offense it would cause and met it head on, for the change would be seized upon by the doubters and the People of the Book as a sign of confusion. *The foolish among the people will say, what has turned them from the qibla they used to face. Say, to God belongs the east and the west, He guides whom He wills to a straight path* (al-Baqarah 2:142). The turning was a declaration. The new community was not a sect of an older faith facing an older center. It was the restoration of the religion of Abraham (AS), facing the House Abraham (AS) had raised, and the recitation framed the whole episode as a test that separated those who followed the Messenger ﷺ from those who would turn on their heels (2:143).

These same early Medinan years brought the great institutions of the believer's life into their settled form. The fast of the month of Ramadan was ordained, the recitation tying it to the very month in which the recitation had begun to descend. *The month of Ramadan in which was revealed the Quran, a guidance for the people and clear proofs of guidance and the criterion. So whoever among you sights the month, let him fast it* (al-Baqarah 2:185), and the command was given gently, that God intends ease and not hardship (2:185). Fasting was prescribed as it had been prescribed upon those before them, that they might attain God-consciousness (2:183). The charity of the believer, the zakāt, was being fixed as a due and not a favor. The pilgrimage would in time be made obligatory upon those who could reach it. The shape of the worshipping community was set in these years, its day turned five times toward the House, its year marked by the fast of the month of revelation, its wealth purified by a fixed due to the poor, and the recitation that set it came down into the daily life of a city that was living it as it was revealed.

## Badr

In the second year the long cold war with Mecca turned hot, and it turned upon a caravan. A great Meccan caravan under Abū Sufyān was returning from Syria along the route near the coast, and the Prophet ﷺ led out a force of a little more than three hundred to intercept it, lightly equipped, with few horses, expecting an easy seizure of goods rather than a battle. But Abū Sufyān, warned, diverted the caravan to safety and sent to Mecca for help. Mecca, its pride and its trade both stung, marched out near a thousand strong, armored and mounted, to crush the upstart of Medina once and for all. The two forces met at the wells of Badr. The believers, outnumbered more than three to one and stripped of the easy prize they had come for, now faced the full strength of Quraysh, and the recitation records that some of them argued against the fight as though being driven to death with their eyes open (al-Anfāl 8:5-6). The Prophet ﷺ consulted his men, and the emigrants and then the helpers pledged to follow him into the battle though it meant their lives, and he drew them up at the wells and seized the water.

Through the night before the battle and in its hour the Prophet ﷺ stood in his shelter and prayed, and the sound tradition in Muslim preserves the desperation of his supplication. He raised his hands and begged his Lord to grant the help He had promised, saying that if this small band were destroyed, God would not be worshipped on the earth, until Abū Bakr took his cloak and told him his Lord would surely fulfill His promise. And the help came. The recitation describes a reinforcement the eye did not see, the casting of sleep over the believers as a security, the sending down of rain to firm the sand beneath them, and the descent of the angels. *When you sought help of your Lord and He answered you, indeed I will reinforce you with a thousand of the angels, rank upon rank* (al-Anfāl 8:9), and again, *remember when your Lord inspired to the angels, I am with you, so strengthen those who believe* (8:12). The small force broke the great one. The chiefs of Quraysh who had led the persecution fell on the field, Abū Jahl among them, the man the Prophet ﷺ had called the Pharaoh of this community. And the recitation took from the victors the very thing that might have undone them, the pride of having won, declaring that the killing and the victory had not been theirs at all. *And you did not kill them, but God killed them. And you did not throw when you threw, but God threw, that He might test the believers with a good test from Him* (al-Anfāl 8:17). The day was named the Day of the Criterion, the yawm al-furqān, the day God distinguished truth from falsehood (8:41), and the recitation reminded them ever after that God had given them victory at Badr while they were utterly weak, that they might be grateful (Āl ʿImrān 3:123).

The captives taken at Badr produced the first great question of policy the recitation answered, and it answered it as a rebuke. Quraysh sought to ransom their prisoners, and the believers, poor and in need, inclined to take the ransom, and the Prophet ﷺ allowed it. But the recitation came down stern, that a prophet should not hold captives until he had subdued the land, that the believers had looked to the goods of this world while God intended the Hereafter, and that had it not been for a prior decree from God a great punishment would have touched them for what they took (al-Anfāl 8:67-68). Here again the honesty of the revelation marks it, for it records the Prophet ﷺ and his community choosing, and then corrects the choice, in a Book they themselves recited. A fabricated scripture flatters its authors. This one reproved them on the morning after their greatest triumph.

## Uhud

Mecca came back for revenge in the third year, and at the mountain of Uḥud outside Medina the believers learned the bitter underside of Badr, that the help of God is tied to obedience, and that it is withdrawn when obedience fails. Quraysh marched out some three thousand strong under Abū Sufyān, with the women led by Hind bint ʿUtbah beating their drums and crying for vengeance for the fathers and brothers slain at Badr. The Prophet ﷺ drew up his force of about seven hundred with their backs to the mountain, and he set fifty archers on a small hill to guard the army's rear and flank, commanding them not to leave their post whatever happened, whether the Muslims won or lost. The battle opened in the believers' favor, and the Meccan line broke, and the army of Medina fell to gathering the spoils of the fleeing enemy. And the archers on the hill, seeing the victory and fearing to lose their share of the plunder, abandoned their post against the explicit order of the Prophet ﷺ. The Meccan cavalry, under Khālid ibn al-Walīd who had not yet entered Islam, swept around the undefended hill and fell on the believers from behind, and victory turned in an instant to rout.

The slaughter was grievous. Ḥamzah, the Prophet's uncle, the Lion of God who had fought like ten men, was killed and his body mutilated, his liver cut out by Hind in her vengeance. The Prophet ﷺ himself was struck down in the chaos, his face cut, a tooth broken, the rings of his helmet driven into his cheek, and a cry went up that he had been killed. The cry nearly finished the army, for men lost heart and some thought of surrender. But he was alive, and those around him rallied to him and drew him up the mountain to safety, and the line re-formed, and Quraysh, having bloodied them but unable to take the city, withdrew. The recitation devoted a long passage of the chapter of the Family of ʿImrān to the lesson of the day, and it did not spare the believers. *And God had certainly fulfilled His promise to you when you were killing them by His permission, until the moment you lost courage and fell to disputing about the order and disobeyed, after He had shown you what you love. Among you are some who desire this world and among you are some who desire the Hereafter* (Āl ʿImrān 3:152). The defeat was traced to its true cause, the disobedience of the archers and the love of the spoils, and the believers were told plainly that the wound they suffered was answered by a like wound they had given at Badr, and that God was sifting them, marking the true believers from the false (3:140, 3:166-167).

Two consolations were folded into the rebuke, and the community has leaned on both ever since. To those who grieved the dead, the recitation forbade the very word death over the martyrs. *And do not think of those killed in the way of God as dead. Rather they are alive with their Lord, receiving provision, rejoicing in what God has given them of His bounty* (Āl ʿImrān 3:169-170). And to those who had nearly broken when they thought the Prophet ﷺ was slain, the recitation administered a hard and necessary truth, that the faith did not rest on the life of the man who carried it but on the God who sent him. *Muhammad is but a messenger. Messengers have passed away before him. So if he dies or is killed, would you turn back on your heels. And whoever turns back on his heels will never harm God at all, and God will reward the grateful* (Āl ʿImrān 3:144). The verse was spoken to a frightened battlefield. It would be spoken again, with terrible force, on the day the Prophet ﷺ truly died, and it would hold the community together in that hour as it held them at Uḥud. And in the same passage the recitation drew from the wounds of the day a portrait of the Prophet's own character, that it was by a mercy from God that he was gentle with them, for had he been harsh and hard-hearted they would have scattered from around him, and so he was to pardon them and seek forgiveness for them and consult them in the matter (Āl ʿImrān 3:159). The commander whose order had been disobeyed at the cost of his army's blood and his own was commanded to pardon, to forgive, and to keep consulting the very men who had failed him.

## The Treachery of the Tribes

The constitution of Medina had bound the Jewish tribes of the city into one defensive covenant with the believers, and the years of war brought that covenant to a series of breaking points. The account must be given plainly, neither softened into apology nor inflamed into the thing the enemies of the faith have made of it. What is at issue throughout is the breach, in time of existential war, of a signed pact of mutual defense, and the response of a head of state to treason within the walls of a besieged city.

After Badr the tribe of Banū Qaynuqāʿ, by the sources, broke into open defiance and provocation, and after a confrontation they were besieged and then expelled from Medina with their movable property, their lives spared. After Uḥud the tribe of Banū al-Naḍīr was found, by the account the biographers carry, to have conspired in a plot to kill the Prophet ﷺ while he sat by the wall of their settlement, and they too were besieged and expelled, departing with what their camels could carry, their date-groves left behind. The chapter of the Exile, al-Ḥashr, was revealed concerning the expulsion of al-Naḍīr, opening with the gathering of the divine names and naming the event as God's own act. *It is He who expelled those who disbelieved among the People of the Book from their homes at the first gathering. You did not think they would leave, and they thought their fortresses would protect them from God, but God came upon them from where they did not expect* (al-Ḥashr 59:2). These were expulsions, not killings, the penalty of broken covenant and conspiracy in a city at war, and the recitation tied them to the sovereignty of God over the affairs of the community.

The gravest episode came with the third tribe, Banū Qurayẓah, and it came at the worst possible moment, during the siege of the Trench, when the survival of the whole community hung by a thread, and it is taken up in its place below, for it belongs to that siege. It must be said here only that the modern reader will find scholarly discussion of the numbers and details transmitted in the biographical sources, which are sīra-grade in their particulars, while the broad event of the reckoning with Qurayẓah is referenced in the recitation itself, in the chapter of the Confederates. The container, a tribe that turned traitor during a siege that nearly destroyed Medina and was judged for it, stands in the Book. The exact figures and the fine details belong to the biographical tradition and carry its weight, and an honest account holds them at that weight rather than asserting them as certainties or denying them as inventions.

## The Trench

In the fifth year Mecca tried to end the matter with one overwhelming blow, gathering a grand confederation, the Aḥzāb, Quraysh and the tribe of Ghaṭafān and their allies and the expelled chiefs of al-Naḍīr, a host the sources put at some ten thousand, the largest army Arabia had yet assembled, marching on a Medina that could field perhaps three thousand. Against such numbers in the open the believers had no hope, and it was the counsel of a Persian companion, Salmān al-Fārisī, who knew the warcraft of his homeland, that saved the city. He advised the digging of a great trench, a khandaq, across the exposed northern approach to Medina, a thing the Arabs did not know in war, and the Prophet ﷺ took the spade with them and dug alongside the poorest, binding stones to his belly against the hunger, by the sound reports, as they worked. When the confederation arrived they found themselves halted before a ditch their cavalry could not cross, and the great army settled into a siege.

The siege was the severest trial the community had yet endured, and the recitation that came down through it, in the chapter of the Confederates, is among the most psychologically exact passages in the whole Book, for it describes not the battle but the fear. *When they came upon you from above you and from below you, and when the eyes shifted in terror and the hearts reached the throats, and you assumed about God every kind of assumption. There the believers were tested and shaken with a severe shaking* (al-Aḥzāb 33:10-11). The hypocrites of the city, the doubters who had professed faith without holding it, began to slip away with excuses, and some sought to spread despair (33:12-13). And in the midst of it the besieged community learned that Banū Qurayẓah, the one Jewish tribe remaining under the covenant inside the defenses, had been turned by the chiefs of al-Naḍīr and had broken the pact, opening the prospect of an attack from within the walls while the great army pressed from without, the eyes turning aside and the hearts at the throat exactly as the recitation said. The believers held. The recitation records the difference between the two responses to the terror, for when the true believers saw the confederates they said only that this was what God and His Messenger ﷺ had promised them, and it increased them in faith (33:22).

The siege broke not by a charge but by a wind. After weeks in the cold of the desert winter, with their supplies failing and their alliance fraying under the strain and the manoeuvres the Prophet ﷺ set among them to sow distrust, a fierce wind rose against the confederates in the night, tearing up their tents, scattering their fires, and breaking their will, and they struck camp and dispersed in the dark, and the greatest army Arabia had raised melted away without a battle. The recitation named the wind and the unseen hosts as God's own doing. *O you who believe, remember the favor of God upon you when armies came to you and We sent against them a wind and armies you did not see, and God is ever Seeing of what you do* (al-Aḥzāb 33:9), and again, *God turned back those who disbelieved in their rage, they gained no good, and God sufficed the believers in the fighting, and God is ever Powerful and Mighty* (33:25).

With the great army gone, the Prophet ﷺ turned at once to the treason within, to Banū Qurayẓah, who had broken the covenant at the moment of the city's greatest danger. They were besieged in their fortresses and surrendered, and they asked that their fate be decided not by the Prophet ﷺ directly but by an arbiter, and they named Saʿd ibn Muʿādh, the chief of the Aws, who had been their old confederate before Islam, trusting that their former ally would be lenient. Saʿd, gravely wounded in the siege and soon to die of his wound, gave his judgment according to the law by which these very tribes lived, the law of the scripture they held, under which the men who made war and broke faith were put to the sword and the women and children taken captive. The recitation records the reckoning as God's act against those who had supported the confederates from their strongholds. *And He brought down those of the People of the Book who supported them from their fortresses, and cast terror into their hearts, so a group you killed and a group you took captive. And He made you inherit their land and their homes and their property* (al-Aḥzāb 33:26-27). The judgment was severe, and it was the judgment of treason in time of siege, rendered by an arbiter the condemned had themselves chosen, under the law of their own scripture. The honest reader holds the severity and the context together, and does not pretend the one away to spare the other. The community had been an arrow's flight from annihilation, betrayed from within at the hour of its greatest peril, and it answered the betrayal as the law of that world answered betrayal, through an arbitration the traitors had selected.

## Hudaybiyah

In the sixth year the Prophet ﷺ did a thing his enemies could not have predicted. He announced not a campaign but a pilgrimage, and set out for Mecca with some fourteen hundred believers in the dress of pilgrims, unarmed but for the traveler's sword, to perform the lesser pilgrimage at the House from which they had been driven. Quraysh, caught between their dread of being seen to bar the Arabs from the sanctuary and their refusal to let the man they had exiled enter in triumph, blocked the road. The Prophet ﷺ turned aside and halted at a place called Ḥudaybiyah on the edge of the sacred precinct, and there his she-camel Qaṣwāʾ knelt and would not rise, the moment carried in Bukhārī. He said she had not grown stubborn but was restrained by the One who had restrained the elephant from Mecca, and he took it as a sign to make peace rather than force the gate of the sanctuary with blood.

What followed tested the believers harder than any battle. A rumor that the envoy ʿUthmān, sent into Mecca, had been killed brought the Prophet ﷺ to call his people to a pledge beneath a tree, the Pledge of Good Pleasure, the Bayʿat al-Riḍwān, in which they swore to stand with him to the death, and the recitation sealed God's pleasure with them in that hour. *Certainly was God pleased with the believers when they pledged to you under the tree, and He knew what was in their hearts, so He sent down tranquility upon them and rewarded them with a near victory* (al-Fatḥ 48:18), and the pledge to the Prophet ﷺ was made a pledge to God Himself, *indeed those who pledge to you, they are but pledging to God, the hand of God is over their hands* (48:10). Then ʿUthmān returned alive, and a treaty was struck, and its terms looked to the believers like a humiliation. There would be a truce of ten years. The believers would turn back this year without entering Mecca, and come the next. Any Meccan who fled to Medina without his guardian's leave would be returned to Mecca, but any believer who deserted to Mecca would not be returned. ʿUmar, fierce in his faith, came near to breaking under it, asking the Prophet ﷺ how they could accept disgrace in their religion, and the Prophet ﷺ answered only that he was the servant of God and His Messenger ﷺ and would not disobey Him. The believers, heartsick, were slow even to shave their heads and end the pilgrimage state, until the Prophet ﷺ, on the counsel of his wife Umm Salamah, shaved his own head first and they followed.

On the road back the recitation came down and turned the apparent defeat inside out, opening the chapter of the Victory with words that astonished them. *Indeed We have given you a clear victory* (al-Fatḥ 48:1). And so it proved, for the truce that had seemed a surrender opened the roads, and in the two years of peace that followed, men entered Islam in numbers greater than in all the years of war before it, the faith spreading freely now that the swords were sheathed, so that the historians have called Ḥudaybiyah the greatest victory of all, won without a battle, the patience at the kneeling camel vindicated by the floods of the converted. The recitation even confirmed the true dream that had set the Prophet ﷺ on the road, that the believers would indeed enter the Sacred Mosque in security, their heads shaved, without fear, in God's good time (48:27). The statesman had traded a year's pride for a generation's harvest.

## The Letters to the Kings

Secure in the truce, the Prophet ﷺ lifted his eyes past Arabia to the powers of the earth, and sent envoys bearing letters to the kings beyond the peninsula, calling them to the worship of the One God, to Heraclius the emperor of Rome, to Khosrow the king of Persia, to the Negus of Abyssinia, to the Muqawqis the governor of Egypt, and to the Arab rulers of the frontiers. The letters carried the Quranic invitation to common ground with the People of the Book. *Say, O People of the Book, come to a word equal between us and you, that we worship none but God and associate nothing with Him, and that none of us take others as lords besides God* (Āl ʿImrān 3:64). The responses are remembered, and two of them close circles this history opened in its first Book. Heraclius, the emperor who had broken Persia and walked the Cross back to Jerusalem, received the letter and, by the sound and famous report in Bukhārī, summoned the Meccan Abū Sufyān, then in Syria with a caravan, and questioned him closely about the man who claimed prophecy, his lineage, his followers, whether he ever broke his word, whether his followers increased or fell away. From the answers, that he was of noble lineage, that his followers were the weak and the young and ever-increasing, that none turned back from his religion in displeasure, and that he never broke a covenant, Heraclius concluded that this was indeed the prophet whose coming he had read of, and said that if what he heard were true the man would one day rule the ground beneath his own feet, though the emperor's own people would not let him follow. Khosrow of Persia tore the letter in his pride, and when the Prophet ﷺ heard of it he said that God would tear his kingdom to pieces, and within years the kingdom of Persia was torn to pieces exactly so, the empire that had once held Jerusalem and sacked the Roman east undone, its regalia carried in time to Medina, the bracelets of its king placed on the arm of the Bedouin Surāqah as the Prophet ﷺ had promised on the road of the Hijra. The two exhausted giants of the opening Book reappear here at the hinge of the story, the one recognizing the truth and unable to follow it, the other tearing the summons and torn in turn.

## Khaybar

In the seventh year the Prophet ﷺ moved against Khaybar, the great oasis of fortified Jewish settlements to the north that had become a center of hostility, sheltering the expelled chiefs who plotted against Medina. The reduction of its fortresses is remembered for the giving of the banner. On the eve of the assault the Prophet ﷺ said, by the sound report in Bukhārī and Muslim, that he would give the standard the next day to a man who loved God and His Messenger ﷺ and whom God and His Messenger ﷺ loved, and a man through whom God would grant the victory, and the men passed the night wondering who it would be. In the morning he called for ʿAlī, who was suffering in his eyes, and applied his saliva to them so that they were healed at once, and gave him the banner, and by his hand the strongest fortress fell. When Khaybar was taken its people were not expelled but left upon their land as cultivators, surrendering a share of its produce to Medina, an arrangement that became a model, the conquered people of the Book retained on their soil under terms rather than driven from it.

## The Opening of Mecca

In the eighth year Quraysh broke the truce of Ḥudaybiyah, for a tribe allied to them attacked a tribe allied to the Prophet ﷺ, with Qurashi help, shedding blood within the sacred precinct. The Prophet ﷺ, the guarantor of his allies, called the believers to arms in secret and marched on Mecca with ten thousand, the largest force he had ever led, before Quraysh even grasped what was coming. The city that had tortured and exiled him, that had marched against him at Badr and Uḥud and the Trench, lay open before an army it could not resist, and Abū Sufyān, the old commander of the wars against him, came out and submitted and accepted Islam. The Prophet ﷺ, in his mercy, declared that whoever entered Abū Sufyān's house was safe, and whoever shut his own door was safe, and whoever entered the sacred Mosque was safe. The army entered Mecca from its several quarters with almost no blood shed, the conquest of the great prize achieved by overwhelming force held in almost complete restraint.

The Prophet ﷺ went to the House, the Kaʿba his ancestor had raised for the One God and his people had filled with idols, and he circled it and entered upon it, and there he did the thing the whole story had been moving toward since the first verses of Quraysh and the Elephant. The sound report of Ibn Masʿūd in Bukhārī and Muslim records that there were three hundred and sixty idols set around the House, and the Prophet ﷺ went among them striking them down one after another with the stick in his hand, reciting as each fell the verse of the chapter of the Night Journey, *truth has come and falsehood has vanished, indeed falsehood is ever bound to vanish* (al-Isrāʾ 17:81), and another verse, *truth has come, and falsehood can neither begin anything nor repeat it* (Sabaʾ 34:49). The House of Abraham (AS) was cleansed of its tenants and returned to the worship of the One for whom it was built. The key of the Kaʿba, which the Prophet ﷺ might have kept for his own clan, he returned to its hereditary keeper, ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥah, declaring it a trust. And he climbed onto the roof of the House and had Bilāl, the slave who had said only One under the torturing rock, raise the call to prayer over the conquered city from the roof of the Kaʿba itself, the voice of the freed slave proclaiming the oneness of God from the highest point of Mecca.

Then came the moment that defined the conquest, the moment of mercy over vengeance. The Prophet ﷺ stood before the people of Mecca, his lifelong persecutors, who had done to him and his every cruelty, and who now stood at his mercy with their lives in his hand, and he asked them what they thought he would do with them, and they said they hoped for good, for he was a noble brother and the son of a noble brother. And he answered them with the words the Quran gives to the prophet Joseph (AS) forgiving the brothers who had thrown him in the well, *no blame upon you this day, God will forgive you, and He is the most Merciful of the merciful* (Yūsuf 12:92), and he declared them free. There was no slaughter, no settling of the long accounts of twenty years, no reprisal against the families who had buried his companions and broken his teeth. The conqueror of Mecca pardoned Mecca. And the recitation that came down near this culmination did not exult in triumph but turned the Prophet ﷺ toward humility and the nearness of his own death, the short chapter of Divine Help. *When the help of God has come, and the victory, and you see the people entering the religion of God in crowds, then exalt the praise of your Lord and ask His forgiveness, indeed He is ever accepting of repentance* (al-Naṣr 110). At the summit of his earthly success the command was not to celebrate but to praise God and seek forgiveness, and the perceptive among the companions understood that the chapter announcing the people entering the faith in crowds was also announcing that the Prophet's work was nearly done.

There is a pattern in that summit worth pausing upon, for it runs against the deepest grain of power. The recitation traces the first ruin of all to a single posture, the refusal of the one who would not bow because he reckoned himself the better. *I am better than him. You created me from fire and created him from clay* (al-Aʿrāf 7:12). It is the pride that takes its own origin for a warrant to stand above the common law of the creature, the self lifted into a god, and it is the root from which every tyranny grows. And here, at the one moment in his life when the Prophet ﷺ held absolute power over those who had wronged him, the city that had tortured and exiled him lying open and helpless in his hand, the recitation turned him not upward into the pride that had felled the first refuser but downward into the dust. He entered Mecca, the reports say, with his head bowed so low upon his mount that it almost touched the saddle, humbled before God in the very hour of his triumph. He took no revenge. He freed those who had earned death. And the word that came down upon the victory was the command to seek forgiveness. The conqueror at his summit did the opposite of what the fallen one had done at his, bowing where the other had refused to bow, ascribing the whole of it to God where the other had claimed it for himself, and the height the Prophet ﷺ reached was reached, exactly, by the lowering.

## Hunayn and Taif

The conquest of Mecca was not the end of the year's fighting, for the great tribal confederation of Hawāzin and Thaqīf, alarmed at the fall of Mecca, gathered to crush the believers before they could consolidate. The Prophet ﷺ marched out against them with a vast force, twelve thousand now, ten thousand who had taken Mecca and two thousand of the newly submitted Meccans, the largest Muslim army yet. And at the valley of Ḥunayn this great host met its hardest lesson, for some of the men, looking at their own numbers, felt the confidence that comes before a fall, and the recitation names the failing exactly. The enemy, expert archers, had set an ambush in the narrow valley, and at the first onslaught in the dim of dawn the Muslim vanguard broke and the great army turned in rout, the numbers that had pleased them availing nothing. The Prophet ﷺ held his ground on his mule amid the flight, calling the believers back by name, by the bond of the pledge, until the line re-formed around him and turned and broke the enemy. The recitation took the day as a teaching against the pride of numbers. *God has already given you victory in many regions, and on the day of Ḥunayn, when your great number pleased you but availed you nothing at all, and the earth, vast as it is, closed in upon you, and you turned back fleeing. Then God sent down His tranquility upon His Messenger and upon the believers, and sent down soldiers you did not see, and punished those who disbelieved* (al-Tawbah 9:25-26). The victory at Badr had taught them that God gives the few the strength of the many. The near-disaster at Ḥunayn taught them that numbers without God are nothing, and that the tranquility comes down upon the patient, not the proud.

The vast spoils of Ḥunayn produced the last great test of the founding years, a test not of the sword but of the heart, and it fell upon the Anṣār, the Helpers who had given the Prophet ﷺ their city and their lives. In distributing the spoils the Prophet ﷺ gave generously, and especially to the chiefs of Mecca and the tribes newly come to Islam, those the recitation calls the ones whose hearts are to be reconciled, giving the new and the wavering large shares to bind them to the faith, while the Anṣār, who had borne the heat of the years, received little. A murmur arose among the younger Helpers, that the Prophet ﷺ had given to his own people of Quraysh and left them out, and word of it reached him, and he gathered the Anṣār alone and spoke to them words the sound tradition preserves and that the community has never read without emotion. He reminded them that he had come to them rejected and they had sheltered him, poor and they had enriched him, in fear and they had made him safe. Then he asked them whether they were troubled over the goods of this world, the sheep and the camels, by which he sought to bind men newly come to the faith, while he entrusted them, the Anṣār, with Islam itself. Were they not content, he asked, that the people should go back to their homes with the sheep and the camels, while they went back to their homes with the Messenger of God ﷺ. Would it not please them that, while others took the flocks, the Anṣār took the Prophet ﷺ to their houses. And he prayed for the Helpers and the children of the Helpers, until the men wept into their beards and said they were content with God and His Messenger ﷺ as their portion. The spoils were given to others. The Prophet ﷺ was the portion of the Anṣār, and they wept for joy at the trade.

There remained Ṭāʾif, the town of Thaqīf that had stoned the Prophet ﷺ and driven him out in the Year of Sorrow, and now its survivors had fled behind their walls. The Prophet ﷺ laid siege to it, but the walls held, and rather than spend his men against them he lifted the siege and withdrew. When his companions asked him to invoke God against Thaqīf he prayed instead for their guidance, asking God to guide Thaqīf and bring them, and within a short time the people of Ṭāʾif came of their own accord to Medina and entered Islam, the very town that had pelted him with stones submitting at last by its own feet. The prayer he had made years before at the lowest hour of his life, when the angel of the mountains offered to crush them and he had refused, hoping God would bring forth from their loins those who worshipped Him alone, was answered before his eyes. Mecca was open, the House was cleansed, the great confederations were broken, and the man who had crept out of Mecca by night with a price on his head had returned to it as the master of Arabia, and pardoned it. Yet the work was not finished. There remained the far northern frontier and the expedition of Tabūk, the year in which the tribes of all Arabia would come to him in delegation, the final pilgrimage in which the religion would be declared complete, and the death that the chapter of Divine Help had quietly foretold. That completion is the matter of the Book that follows.

# IV · THE COMPLETION

*A work that began in the seam between two empires ended by swallowing them both. The last three years of the Prophet's life carried the message to the edge of the Roman frontier, drew the tribes of all Arabia into the faith in delegation after delegation, and reached its summit in a single pilgrimage and a single verse that declared the religion complete. Then the man who had carried it died, an ordinary human death in the arms of his wife, and the community he had built nearly broke on the shock of it, and was held together by one old friend reciting one verse. Within a few years of his death the people of the barren valley had taken Jerusalem from Rome and Ctesiphon from Persia, and the prophecy with which this history opened, the Romans defeated and then victorious, had given way to a third power neither giant had foreseen. This is the Book of the completion, of the perfected religion, the highest companionship, and the edifice of prophecy sealed with its final brick.*

## The March to Tabuk

In the ninth year word reached Medina that the Romans were massing on the northern frontier of Arabia for a strike against the new power in the Hijaz, and the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ called the believers to the hardest expedition of his life. It was the march to Tabūk on the edge of Syria, in the burning heat of high summer, across a waterless waste, against the greatest military power on the earth. The tradition called it the army of hardship, the jaysh al-ʿusrah, and the recitation used the march as a sieve, separating those whose faith was real from those who had professed it with their tongues. The hypocrites of Medina, who had slipped away at the Trench, now made their excuses to stay behind in the cool of the date-groves rather than march to the heat and the danger, and the chapter of Repentance, Barāʾah, came down in this period heavy with their exposure, rebuking those who said do not go forth in the heat, answering that the fire of Hell is hotter, if they would only understand (al-Tawbah 9:81). The recitation pressed the believers to go forth light or heavy and to strive with their wealth and their lives (9:41), and it stripped the excuses of the hypocrites bare one by one.

Three believers stayed behind not from hypocrisy but from a failure of will they could not afterward forgive themselves, and their story is preserved in one of the longest and most searching narrations in Bukhārī, told by one of the three himself, Kaʿb ibn Mālik. He had meant to prepare and march, and had delayed, and delayed again, until the army was gone and he was left behind with no excuse but his own weakness. When the Prophet ﷺ returned, Kaʿb would not lie to him as the hypocrites lied, but confessed plainly that he had no excuse. The Prophet ﷺ commanded that the three be shunned, and for fifty days no one in Medina spoke to them, until the earth, the recitation says, vast as it was, closed in upon them, and their own souls closed in upon them, and they knew there was no refuge from God except in Him. Then the forgiveness came down in the chapter of Repentance, naming the very state of their hearts. *And upon the three who were left behind, until the earth, vast as it was, closed in upon them, and their souls closed in upon them, and they knew there was no refuge from God except toward Him. Then He turned to them in mercy that they might repent. Indeed God is the Accepting of repentance, the Merciful* (al-Tawbah 9:118). Kaʿb said it was the greatest day of his life, the day his name was cleared from heaven. The honesty of the episode is its power, three faithful men who failed, who refused to cover their failure with a lie, and whose long ordeal of being cut off became a verse of mercy in the Book.

At Tabūk no Roman army appeared, the rumored invasion either dissolved or never gathered, and the Prophet ﷺ, having made his show of strength at the frontier, made treaties with the Christian and Jewish border communities of the north, with the people of Aylah on the gulf and the oasis settlements beyond. They agreed to pay a tribute, the jizyah, in exchange for protection and the keeping of their faith, the arrangement the recitation set out for the People of the Book who came under the authority of the believers (al-Tawbah 9:29). The expedition of hardship was the last great campaign the Prophet ﷺ led, and it ended not in battle but in the extension of the new order's protection over the peoples of the frontier. On the return he ordered the destruction of a mosque that a faction of the hypocrites had built not for worship but as a base for division and intrigue, the mosque of harm, the masjid al-ḍirār, which the recitation exposed as a thing built upon falsehood, never to be stood in, against the mosque founded upon piety from the first day (al-Tawbah 9:107-108). Even a place of prayer, the recitation taught, is judged by the intention that raises it.

## The Year of Delegations

With Mecca fallen and Arabia's great confederations broken, the peninsula made its decision, and it made it by sending its tribes to Medina. The ninth year is remembered as the Year of Delegations, the ʿĀm al-Wufūd, in which the tribes of all Arabia sent their representatives to the Prophet ﷺ to accept Islam and enter the new order, delegation following delegation through the year, the long resistance of the peninsula giving way to a flood of submission, exactly as the chapter of Divine Help had foretold, the people entering the religion of God in crowds. The people of Ṭāʾif came, the Thaqīf who had stoned him, submitting at last. The tribes of the center and the east and the south came. And among the most significant was the delegation of the Christians of Najrān, the very community whose martyred ancestors the chapter of the Trench had memorialized, who came to debate the nature of Christ with the Prophet ﷺ. When the argument over whether Jesus (AS) was the son of God reached its limit, the recitation proposed a solemn test, the mubāhalah, that both parties call down the curse of God upon the liars among them, each bringing their sons and their women. *Then whoever argues with you about it after the knowledge has come to you, say, come, let us call our sons and your sons, our women and your women, ourselves and yourselves, then let us pray earnestly and invoke the curse of God upon the liars* (Āl ʿImrān 3:61). The Prophet ﷺ came out for the imprecation with his own family, his daughter Fāṭimah, her husband ʿAlī, and their two sons, and the Christians of Najrān, seeing the gravity with which he staked his own household on the truth, drew back from the curse and made a treaty instead, agreeing to a tribute and keeping their faith and their churches under the protection of the believers. The community that had been burned for monotheism in the chapter of the Trench was now received in treaty by the bearer of the final monotheism, allowed its religion under a covenant of protection.

The same year the Prophet ﷺ sent Abū Bakr to lead the pilgrimage to Mecca, and behind him sent ʿAlī to proclaim the opening verses of the chapter of Repentance to the assembled pilgrims, the verses that ended forever the old order of idolatrous pilgrimage at the House. They declared a disassociation from the treaties with the idolaters who had broken faith, gave them four months of safe passage, and then closed the sanctuary to idolatry, that after that year no idolater would make the pilgrimage and no one would circle the House naked as the pagans had done. *And a proclamation from God and His Messenger to the people on the day of the greater pilgrimage, that God is free of the idolaters, and so is His Messenger* (al-Tawbah 9:3). The House of Abraham (AS), cleansed of its idols at the conquest, was now cleansed of idolatrous pilgrimage, reserved for the worship of the One alone. The whole movement of the chapters of Quraysh and the Elephant, the sanctuary saved that it might be worshipped at, reached here its completion. The House was the House of the One God again, in stone and in rite both.

## The Farewell Pilgrimage

In the tenth year the Prophet ﷺ made the pilgrimage to Mecca that would be his only complete Hajj and his last, and the tradition calls it the Farewell Pilgrimage, the Ḥajjat al-Wadāʿ, for he seemed to know it was a leave-taking. He gathered to himself the greatest assembly of his life, more than a hundred thousand believers from across Arabia, to perform the rites of Abraham (AS) purified and to hear him deliver the summation of all he had brought. On the plain of ʿArafāt, mounted on his camel before the vast gathering, he delivered the sermon that the sources preserve in its substance from many witnesses, and it stands as the charter of the community and the distillation of the message. He declared the blood and the property and the honor of every person sacred and inviolable to every other, as sacred as that day, that month, that city. He placed his foot upon the practices of the age of ignorance and abolished them, the blood-vengeance of the old feuds, beginning with the unavenged blood of his own family, and the usury of the old economy, beginning with the interest owed to his own uncle, taking nothing for himself first so that none could say he spared his own. He charged the believers concerning the rights of women, that they had rights over the men as the men had rights over them, and to treat them with kindness, for they were a trust from God. He declared all believers brothers, no Arab superior to a non-Arab nor a non-Arab to an Arab, no white superior to a black nor a black to a white, except by God-consciousness. And he told them he had left among them that which, if they held fast to it, they would never go astray, the Book of God. Then he asked the great assembly whether he had conveyed the message, and they answered that he had, and he raised his finger to the sky and brought it down upon the people and said, O God, bear witness, three times. The sermon was the message made law for a civilization, the sanctity of life, the equality of souls, the rights of the weak, the end of the old cruelties, and the Book as the abiding guide.

It was on that day, on ʿArafāt, in the midst of the Farewell Pilgrimage, that the verse came down which the community has always held to be the announcement of completion, the seal upon the whole revelation. *This day I have perfected for you your religion, and completed My favor upon you, and approved for you Islam as religion* (al-Māʾidah 5:3). The religion was declared complete. There would be no addition to it, for it wanted nothing. The favor of God upon the believers, begun with the first command to recite in the cave of Ḥirāʾ twenty-three years before, was now full. And the perceptive among the companions wept when they heard it, ʿUmar among them by the reports, for they understood that a thing declared complete and perfect is a thing that is finished, and that the completion of the message meant the nearness of the end of the messenger. The verse of perfection was, to those with ears, also a verse of farewell. The work for which the orphan of Quraysh had been raised up was done.

## The Final Illness

The Prophet ﷺ returned to Medina, and within a short time the final illness took him, a fever that grew heavier by the day. The biographers and the sound reports together preserve the signs of leave-taking that gathered about him in those weeks. He went out to the martyrs of Uḥud and prayed over them as one bidding farewell, and he went to the graveyard of al-Baqīʿ in the night and prayed long for the dead, as though he were soon to join them. He told the believers from the pulpit that God had given one of His servants the choice between the goods of this world and what is with Him, and that the servant had chosen what is with God, and Abū Bakr, understanding that the Prophet ﷺ spoke of himself, wept, for he alone grasped that the Prophet ﷺ was announcing his own departure. As the fever worsened the Prophet ﷺ asked the leave of his wives to be nursed in the chamber of ʿĀʾishah, and they gave it, and there he lay. He distributed the last of what he had, distressed by the reports to find a few coins still in his keeping that he had not yet given away, unwilling to meet his Lord with hoarded wealth, and he freed his servants. And when he grew too weak to lead the prayers himself, he commanded that Abū Bakr lead the people in prayer in his place, and the community read the sign in it, that the man the Prophet ﷺ set to stand in his place at the prayer was the man to stand in his place over them.

In a last interval of strength he came out, supported between two men, his feet dragging, and sat by Abū Bakr at the prayer, and looked upon the ranks of the believers at their worship, the community he had built from nothing, and the reports say his face shone with what looked like joy at the sight, and he smiled to see them in their rows. It was nearly the last the people saw of him. The sound tradition holds that no prophet dies until he is shown his place in Paradise and then given the choice to remain or to go on, and the Prophet ﷺ, by ʿĀʾishah's narration, had been given that choice and had made it.

## The Death

He died in the chamber of ʿĀʾishah, his head resting against her, on a day of the month of Rabīʿ al-Awwal in the eleventh year of the Hijra, at the age of sixty-three. The sound reports preserve the small human details of the end with a tenderness that has never faded, the cup of water by him into which he dipped his hand and wiped his face, saying there is no god but God, that death has its agonies, the fresh tooth-stick that he wanted at the last and that ʿĀʾishah softened for him so that he cleaned his teeth with it in his final hour. And his last words, by her narration, were raised to the choice he had been given, repeating, with the highest companionship, O God, the highest companionship, the rafīq al-aʿlā, choosing the company of the prophets and the truthful and the martyrs over the life of this world, until his hand fell and he was gone.

The community shattered.
