The man who had been the voice of heaven among them, the center of their every day, the leader they had followed out of Mecca and into a hundred dangers, was dead, and many could not bear it or believe it. ʿUmar, the fiercest of them, stood in the mosque and refused to accept it, declaring that the Prophet ﷺ had not died but had gone to his Lord as Moses (AS) had gone, and would return, and threatening any who said he was dead. The faith hung in that hour on a knife's edge, the grief tipping toward a denial that could have broken the community's grip on the truth. And it was Abū Bakr who held them. He came in, and went to the chamber, and uncovered the face of the Prophet ﷺ, and kissed him, and wept, and said that he was as good in death as in life. Then he came out into the mosque, and quieted ʿUmar, and spoke to the people the words that have anchored the community in every dark hour since, that whoever had been worshipping Muhammad, let him know that Muhammad was dead, and whoever had been worshipping God, let him know that God is living and never dies. And then he recited the verse that had been revealed at Uḥud, when the false cry of the Prophet's death had nearly broken the army, the verse now landing with the full weight of its true occasion. *Muhammad is but a messenger. Messengers have passed away before him. So if he died or was killed, would you turn back on your heels. And whoever turns back on his heels will never harm God at all, and God will reward the grateful* (Āl ʿImrān 3:144). And the companions said it was as though they had never heard the verse until Abū Bakr recited it that day, and ʿUmar said his legs would not carry him and he fell to the ground knowing the Prophet ﷺ was truly dead. The faith had been built so that it did not rest on the life of the man who carried it but on the God who sent him, and at the death of the man that foundation held. The community grieved, but it did not break.

## Saqifah and the Succession

The Prophet ﷺ left no son who survived him and, by the understanding that prevailed, no explicit written appointment of a successor read out to the whole community, and so the question of who would lead after him fell upon the companions in the very hours after his death, before he was even buried. The Helpers gathered at a roofed hall, the Saqīfah of the Banū Sāʿidah, to deliberate, and the leading emigrants joined them, and there, after discussion and some tension between the Helpers and the emigrants over where the leadership should rest, the gathering pledged itself to Abū Bakr. He was the companion who had been the Prophet's friend from the first days, the second of the two in the cave, the man he had set to lead the prayers in his illness, and Abū Bakr became the first Khalīfah, the first successor, the head of the community after the Prophet ﷺ. The general pledge of the people followed.

The deeper question of the succession, of whether the leadership ought to have passed to the Prophet's nearest kinsman ʿAlī and to remain in the house of the Prophet ﷺ, became in the generations that followed the central point of difference between the two great streams of the tradition, and a faithful history records the divergence without arbitrating it, for it is a question of interpretation over which sincere believers have differed since. What the record carries plainly is the event, that Abū Bakr was chosen and pledged to in those first hours and led the community through its first and most dangerous crisis, and that ʿAlī, whatever the circumstances and the timing the differing traditions relate, was among the foremost of the companions and would himself in time hold the same office. The Prophet ﷺ was buried where he had died, in the chamber of ʿĀʾishah, beneath the floor of the room, on the principle the companions recalled that a prophet is buried where he passes, and the chamber that had been the place of his death became the place of his rest, and remains so, within the mosque that grew around it.

## The Ridda and the Empire That Swallowed the Giants

The death of the Prophet ﷺ was the signal for much of Arabia to test whether the new order would survive its founder, and a wave of apostasy and rebellion broke across the peninsula, the wars the tradition calls the Riddah. Tribes that had submitted to the Prophet ﷺ refused now to pay the zakāt to his successor or fell away from the faith altogether, and a series of false prophets, who had arisen even in the Prophet's last days, gathered the disaffected, Musaylimah in the center of Arabia, who had claimed prophethood and a revelation of his own, Ṭulayḥah among the northern tribes, Sajāḥ in the east, and al-Aswad al-ʿAnsī in Yemen, the counter-prophets who rose to claim the mantle and divide the inheritance. Abū Bakr, in the most consequential decision of his short rule, refused every compromise and held the community to the whole of what the Prophet ﷺ had established, declaring that he would fight any who tried to separate the prayer from the alms, and he sent the armies of Islam against the rebellions and the false prophets and broke them one after another, Musaylimah falling at the fierce battle of Yamāmah, the peninsula reconsolidated under the faith within little more than a year. The cost of Yamāmah, where many of those who had memorized the recitation by heart were killed, moved the community to its first great act of preservation, the gathering of the scattered written and memorized portions of the Quran into a single collected text under Abū Bakr, a work later standardized and copied under the third successor, ʿUthmān, so that the recitation the Prophet ﷺ had received was secured against loss in one agreed form, the text the believers recite to this day.

With Arabia secured, the armies of the people of the seam turned outward to the two exhausted giants between which this history began, and what followed in a single generation closed the great frame of the whole story. Under Abū Bakr and then under ʿUmar, the second successor, the believers struck into Roman Syria and Sasanian Iraq at once, and the two empires that had bled each other to exhaustion across the decades this work opened with, depopulated by plague and cold and war, had nothing left to stop them. The Roman army was broken at the Yarmūk in the year 636, and Syria fell, and in 638 Jerusalem itself, the holy city the Persians had sacked and Heraclius had walked the Cross back into, surrendered to the believers, and ʿUmar entered it not in conquering splendor but humbly, by the reports, in worn travelling clothes, and guaranteed its people their lives and their churches and their faith. The Persian army was broken at al-Qādisiyyah, also in the year 636, and Ctesiphon, the capital of the king who had torn the Prophet's letter, fell, and the regalia and the treasure of the kings of Persia were carried back to Medina, and among the spoils were the bracelets of Khosrow, and ʿUmar called for the old Bedouin Surāqah ibn Mālik, who had pursued the Prophet ﷺ on the road of the Hijra and been promised those very bracelets by a hunted fugitive with a price on his head, and the bracelets of the king of Persia were placed upon the arms of Surāqah, and the promise made on the road of the migration was kept against the treasure of the fallen empire. By the year 642 the back of Sasanian Persia was broken at Nihāwand, and the empire that had held Jerusalem and seemed eternal passed out of history altogether.

The frame closes here, exactly where it opened. The history began with two giants staring across a frontier, certain the only danger was each other, and with a recitation that foretold, when Persia looked invincible, that Rome would be defeated and then would conquer within a few years, and the believers would rejoice on the day of the help of God. The recitation's prophecy was fulfilled at Nineveh in the Prophet's lifetime. And then, within a few years of his death, the deeper turn came that no one in the chapter of the Romans had needed to spell out, for the power that rose from the ignored seam between the giants absorbed them both, taking Persia entirely and tearing the Roman east away, the people of the barren valley with the cube of stone in it inheriting the world that the watchers had been too tired to watch. The God who had foretold Rome's reversal over Persia had been raising, in the gap the empires despised, the third thing that would outlast them.

## The Brick

What had been completed was not only a religion and a community but an edifice that the prophets before him had been building across the ages, and the Prophet ﷺ described his own place in it in an image the sound collections of Bukhārī and Muslim preserve, the parable of the brick. He likened himself and the prophets who came before him to a man who built a beautiful house and finished it perfectly except for the place of a single brick in one corner, so that people walking around it would marvel at the house and ask only why that one brick had not been set. I am that brick, he said, and I am the Seal of the Prophets. The recitation had named him so, the last of the line, completing what was begun. *Muhammad is not the father of any of your men, but he is the Messenger of God and the Seal of the Prophets, and God is ever, of all things, Knowing* (al-Aḥzāb 33:40). The house of prophecy, raised by Adam and Noah (AS) and Abraham (AS) and Moses (AS) and Jesus (AS) and all the messengers between, stood finished with his coming, the last brick in its place, the favor of God upon mankind completed as the verse on ʿArafāt had said, the religion perfected and approved.

He had come an orphan into a barren valley in the year his people named for an elephant, and he had been the supplication of Abraham (AS) and the glad tidings of Jesus (AS) and the light his mother saw in the dark. He had spent forty years as the Trustworthy and twenty-three as the Messenger ﷺ, and he had been mocked and tortured out of his own city and had returned to it as its master and pardoned it. He had bound feuding tribes into one community by a creed and a covenant, faced the armies of his people at Badr and Uḥud and the Trench and outlasted them, and cleansed the House of Abraham (AS) of its three hundred and sixty idols and returned it to the One for whom it was raised. He had received in the highest heaven the prayer that anchors the believer's day, and recited a Book that overturned the moral order of a civilization, and delivered on a plain before a hundred thousand the charter of the sanctity of every life and the equality of every soul, and heard from heaven that the religion was complete. And he had died an ordinary death, his head against his wife, choosing the highest companionship, and the empire of the giants had fallen to the people of his seam within a generation, and the edifice of prophecy stood sealed.

The chronological life is told. What remains is to see the man himself more closely than the march of events allows, the face and the daily rhythm and the household and the mercy of him, and to set out the law he left and the unseen horizon he opened and the chain of prophets he sealed. To those closer portraits the following Books turn, no longer following the calendar but circling the man and the message from the angles the narrative could not pause upon. The statesman has finished his work. The portrait begins.

# V · THE LIVING PORTRAIT

*The chronicle has shown what he did. This Book shows what he was. The companions who lived beside him preserved his face and his gait and the fragrance of him, the way he ate and dressed and slept, the few things he owned, the jokes he made and the tears he shed, his tenderness with children and animals and the weak, the rhythm of his days, and the worship that ran beneath all of it like a hidden spring. They preserved these because they loved him, and because the recitation had told them that in him was the pattern they were to follow. This is the portrait the lovers drew, set down detail by detail, so that a reader fourteen centuries away might see, as nearly as words allow, the human being through whom the Book came.*

## The Face and the Form

Those who saw him strained to describe him, and the descriptions they left agree in their portrait of a man of arresting and gentle presence. They were gathered above all in the collection of Tirmidhī known as the Shamāʾil and scattered through the sound books. He was of middle height, neither tall nor short, yet the companions said that when a tall man walked beside him the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ seemed the taller of the two. His complexion was radiant, neither stark white nor brown but luminous, and the sound reports say his face shone like the moon, and that when he was glad his face brightened until it was as though a piece of the moon shone from it. His hair fell between his ears and his shoulders, black, neither tightly curled nor wholly straight. His eyes were large and dark, the lashes long, with a natural appearance of kohl about them. His brow was broad, his beard full, his teeth slightly spaced, and when he smiled, which was often, the light caught them. Between his shoulders was the Seal of Prophethood, a raised mark of flesh the size of a small egg or a clustered mole, which the companions Jābir ibn Samurah and others reported in the sound collection of Muslim. It was the sign the People of the Book were said to look for, and the boy who would become a companion was lifted as a child to see it.

He walked, the reports say, as though he were descending a slope, leaning forward with purpose, covering the ground swiftly, so that those with him hurried to keep pace. He turned his whole body to face the one who addressed him rather than only his head, giving each person the sense of his full attention. His voice carried. His silences were long and thoughtful. And a particular detail the companions never tired of recording was his fragrance, for they said the scent of him was sweeter than any perfume, that his sweat was like pearls and smelled of musk, and that a street he had passed through kept his fragrance after him so that people knew the Prophet ﷺ had walked there. Anas, who served him as a boy for ten years, said he had never touched silk or brocade softer than the palm of the Prophet ﷺ, nor smelled any scent finer than his.

Among the most beloved of all the descriptions is one given not by a companion but by a Bedouin woman during the Hijra itself, Umm Maʿbad, at whose tent the Prophet ﷺ and Abū Bakr stopped on the road to Medina, and whose account her husband recorded, a portrait carried in the biographical and traditional sources at the weight of its transmission. She described to her husband a man of conspicuous beauty and radiant face, neither thin nor heavy, with dark eyes and long lashes, a resonant voice, a long neck, a full beard. When silent he was dignified, and when speaking he was crowned with light, the sweetest and most striking of men from a distance and the finest and most beautiful up close. His words were clear and measured, neither too few nor too many, falling like a string of pearls, neither overbearing nor weak, and his companions surrounded him, hanging on his word and rushing to his command. A stranger meeting him for an hour on the road had seen what those who loved him saw across a lifetime.

## The Character

Of all that can be said of him the recitation said the highest thing first, before the companions ever drew their portraits, for God Himself testified to his character in a single verse. *And indeed you are of a great character* (al-Qalam 68:4). His character was the foundation of his prophethood, and when his widow ʿĀʾishah, who knew him as no one else did, was asked to describe that character, she gave the answer the community has treasured above all descriptions, recorded in the sound tradition, that his character was the Quran. He was the recitation walking, the living embodiment of what the Book commanded, and the recitation made him for that reason the pattern every believer was to follow. *There has certainly been for you in the Messenger of God an excellent example, for anyone who hopes in God and the Last Day and remembers God often* (al-Aḥzāb 33:21).

The portrait the companions drew of that character is consistent across hundreds of reports. He was the most humble of men despite being the most honored. He sat among his companions so that a stranger entering could not tell which of them was the Prophet ﷺ, and he disliked men standing for him in deference. He mended his own sandals, patched his own garment, helped with the work of the house, and served himself. He never, the sound reports say, struck a servant or a woman or any creature with his hand, except in the cause of God in battle. He answered the invitation of the poor and the slave, accepted the gift however small, and would not refuse a request he could grant. Anas, who entered his service at the age of about ten and stayed ten years, testified in the sound books that in all that time the Prophet ﷺ never once said to him the smallest word of rebuke, never said why did you do this or why did you not do that, never said even uff, the lightest expression of annoyance in the Arabic tongue. He was more modest, the companions said, than a virgin secluded in her chamber, and when something displeased him they read it only in his face, for he would not shame a man to his face. He kept every promise. He never broke faith, the very trait by which Heraclius recognized him. He was the most truthful of men and would not lie even in jest. And the recitation drew his tenderness toward his community in terms that almost ache, naming his grief at their suffering and his yearning for their wellbeing. *There has certainly come to you a messenger from among yourselves. Grievous to him is what you suffer. Concerned is he over you. To the believers he is kind and merciful* (al-Tawbah 9:128).

## The Day and the Night

His day was built upon the prayer, and the prayer threaded it from before dawn to the depth of the night. He rose for the night prayer in the last third of the dark, and after it slept a little, and woke for the dawn prayer, going out to the mosque to lead the believers while the sky was still grey. After the dawn prayer he would sit in the mosque in the remembrance of God until the sun rose, and then turn to the affairs of the community, the judgments, the delegations, the teaching, the gathering of companions who sat in a circle around him drinking in his words. He took the midday rest the Arabs called the qaylūlah, and led the noon and afternoon prayers, and gave his afternoons and evenings to his family, going among the chambers of his wives. After the sunset and night prayers he disliked idle talk and turned early to sleep, that he might rise for the night. He slept on his right side, his hand under his cheek, and had taught a litany for the lying down and the waking, beginning the night with the Name of God and the verses of refuge and ending it with praise, so that even his sleep was framed in remembrance.

The hidden engine of his days was the night prayer, the tahajjud, which the recitation had made a special charge upon him, *and from the night, pray with it as additional worship for you, that your Lord may raise you to a praised station* (al-Isrāʾ 17:79). He stood in it so long, the sound reports of Bukhārī and Muslim record, that his feet would swell and crack, and when ʿĀʾishah asked him why he burdened himself so when God had forgiven him all his sins past and to come, he gave the answer that has shamed and inspired the worshippers of every generation since, asking should he not then be a grateful servant. The forgiven one worshipped not from fear of punishment but from gratitude, and his standing through the night was the thanksgiving of a heart that owed everything to its Lord. Through the day his tongue was never still of the remembrance of God, and he sought forgiveness, by the sound reports, seventy times in a day, and by another a hundred times, the sinless one asking pardon more than any sinner, teaching by his own practice that nearness to God is not a possession to rest in but a longing that grows the nearer it comes.

## The Table

He ate as a man eats who has set his heart on the next world and not on this one, and the simplicity of his table is among the most striking things the companions preserved, for he was the master of Arabia and lived as its poorest. ʿĀʾishah reported in the sound books that whole months would pass, two and three new moons together, without a fire being lit in the houses of the Prophet ﷺ for cooking, and that they lived in those stretches on the two black things, dates and water, with sometimes a little milk from a neighbor's gift. His family never ate wheat bread to fullness three days running until he died. He never in his life ate at a table laid high, nor from fine dishes, but on the ground, as the poor ate. And he never once, the sound reports of Abū Hurayrah record, found fault with a food set before him. If he had an appetite for it he ate it, and if he disliked it he simply left it, and never said a word of complaint, for to disparage what God had provided was a thing his gratitude would not allow.

He ate with the right hand and with three fingers, and licked them clean when he finished, saying that one does not know in which part of the food the blessing lies. He began with the Name of God and ended with praise of Him, and he taught that the food of one suffices two and the food of two suffices four, urging the sharing of the table. He forbade eating while reclining, eating as a free man sitting upright and not as a king sprawled at his ease, and he taught moderation against the gluttony he saw as a sickness of the soul, saying, in a report the collections carry, that the son of Adam fills no vessel worse than his stomach, and that a few morsels to keep his back straight are enough, and if he must eat more, then a third for his food, a third for his drink, and a third for his breath. He said that the believer eats in one stomach and the ungrateful in seven, the contentment of the grateful heart asking little where the appetite of the heedless never fills. He had foods he loved within that simplicity, the sweetness of honey and the sweetness of dates, and he was fond of the gourd, picking it out from around the dish, and he praised honey as a healing, the recitation itself having named it so, *there comes from within the bee a drink of varying colors, in which is healing for people* (al-Naḥl 16:69). His was not the asceticism of one who despised God's gifts, for he received them with thanks and enjoyed the good among them, but the discipline of one who would not be owned by them, who could feast at a wedding and fast for days, and whose heart was anchored past the reach of the table.

## The Cloak and the Possessions

His dress was as plain as his table. He wore what was available, wool and cotton and the rough cloth of the Yemen, and he loved white garments above all, saying the believers should wear white and shroud their dead in it. He wore a lower wrap and an upper cloak, a mantle thrown over the shoulders, and a turban whose end he sometimes let fall between his shoulders, and simple sandals with two straps. He kept himself clean and combed and fragrant, for cleanliness and good scent he loved and counted part of faith, but of finery and display he wanted nothing. He wore a single silver ring, engraved in three lines with the words Muhammad, the Messenger, of God, which he used as a seal upon his letters to the kings, and he forbade gold and silk to the men of his community while permitting them to the women.

What he owned in the world could be carried by a few hands. He had his weapons, kept for the defense of the faith, several swords among them the one the tradition names Dhū al-Fiqār, taken at Badr, and bows and shields and a coat of mail, and his war banner, black, that the companions called the Eagle. He had his mounts, horses that he loved and named, the camel Qaṣwāʾ that knelt at Ḥudaybiyah and bore him on the Farewell Pilgrimage, a white mule given him by a foreign king that he called Duldul, and a donkey. He had the household goods of a poor family, a few vessels, a leather water-skin, a mat to sleep on that left its marks pressed into his side, a mattress of palm fiber. And the most eloquent fact of all about his relation to the world is the state in which he left it, for the sound report of ʿĀʾishah in Bukhārī records that when the Prophet ﷺ died, the master of all Arabia, to whom the treasure of the conquered would soon be flowing, his armor was pawned with a Jewish neighbor of Medina as security for a debt of barley he had taken to feed his family, and was not yet redeemed. He had said of himself, in a report the collections carry, that his relation to this world was as a traveler who shelters an hour in the shade of a tree and then rises and leaves it. He died owing barley with his shield in pawn, and left no wealth, only what he gave away.

## The Healer's Knowledge

The tradition preserved, alongside his teaching of the soul, a body of guidance for the care of the body, the prophetic medicine the scholars call the ṭibb al-nabawī, and it should be received for what it is. It is the counsel the Prophet ﷺ gave his people on health and healing, part general wisdom of moderation and cleanliness, part specific remedy, and held at the weight the tradition assigns it. He set its first principle as hope, teaching in the sound collection of Muslim that God had sent down no disease without sending down for it a cure, known to those who know it and hidden from those who do not, save one disease for which there is no cure, old age, and so he urged his people to seek treatment and not to despair. He commended particular remedies the Arabs knew, honey above all, which the recitation had named a healing, and he is reported in Bukhārī to have directed a man whose brother suffered in the belly to give him honey, and when the man returned saying it had not helped, to repeat it, saying that God had spoken truly and the man's brother's belly had lied, until the cure took. He praised the small black seed, the ḥabbat al-sawdāʾ, saying in the sound books that in it was a cure for every disease but death. He practiced and recommended cupping, the ḥijāmah, and named days he favored for it. And the spine of all his health counsel was the same moderation he taught at the table, the warning against filling the stomach, the third for food and third for drink and third for breath, for he saw overeating as the root of bodily sickness as he saw heedlessness as the root of the soul's. Alongside the remedies of the body he taught the healing of word and prayer, the recitation of the chapters of refuge over the sick, the ruqyah, breathing the verses upon the ailing, so that the believer met illness with both the means God had placed in creation and the turning of the heart to the One who placed them. His was a medicine that joined the cure to the Curer, that took the remedy and trusted the Lord, and that met the plague, as the first Book of this work recorded, with quarantine and patience rather than terror and flight.

## The Laughter and the Tears

The gravity of his mission did not make him grim, and the companions were careful to preserve his lightness, for they had known a man who smiled more than he laughed and who carried a gentle humor that never once crossed into falsehood. His laughter was mostly a smile that showed his teeth, and he joked with his companions and his family, but when they asked whether he jested with them he answered that he did, but that he spoke only truth even in his jest. So when an old woman asked him to pray that she enter Paradise and he told her, in a report the Shamāʾil tradition carries, that no old woman would enter Paradise, and she turned away weeping, he explained gently that the women of Paradise are made young, that none enters it old, the jest true beneath its surprise. When a man asked him for a mount to carry him, he answered that he would carry him on the child of a she-camel, and when the man protested that a camel's child could not bear him, he laughed and asked what else but a she-camel bears a camel, every full-grown mount being the child of one. He called a companion, in play, the father of two ears. And his tenderness with children was without limit. He would prolong his prostration in prayer when his grandsons Ḥasan and Ḥusayn climbed upon his back, not rising until the child climbed down, and he carried them on his shoulders and called them his sweet basil. He noticed the small griefs of children, asking the little brother of Anas, whose pet bird had died, in a phrase the sound books preserve in its very rhyme, Abū ʿUmayr, what happened to the little sparrow, comforting a child's loss as gravely as a man's.

And he wept. The same eyes that lit when he smiled overflowed easily, in the night prayer and at the recitation of the Quran and at the death of those he loved. When he asked Ibn Masʿūd to recite the Quran to him and Ibn Masʿūd protested that it had been revealed to the Prophet ﷺ and not to him, the Prophet ﷺ said he loved to hear it from another, and as Ibn Masʿūd recited the chapter of the Women and reached the verse asking how it will be when a witness is brought from every nation and the Prophet ﷺ is brought as a witness against them, he said enough, and Ibn Masʿūd looked and his eyes were overflowing. When his infant son Ibrāhīm, born to him in his old age of Māriyah, lay dying in his arms, the tears fell from the Prophet's eyes, and a companion, surprised, asked whether even he wept, and he gave the answer that has taught the believers how to grieve, that the eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, but that we say nothing except what pleases our Lord, and that he was sorrowful at the parting. He grieved as a man and submitted as a prophet, and the two were not at war in him, the tears and the surrender held together in one heart.

## The Mercy to All Creatures

The recitation had defined his whole purpose in seven words, *and We have not sent you except as a mercy to the worlds* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:107), and the worlds his mercy reached included the beasts, in an age and a place that gave animals no thought at all. He taught that there was a reward in every living thing, and the sound books of Bukhārī and Muslim carry his teaching of both edges of it. There was the woman who was punished in Hell on account of a cat she had imprisoned until it starved, neither feeding it nor freeing it to feed itself, and there was the man whose every sin was forgiven because, finding a dog dying of thirst at a well, he climbed down and filled his shoe with water and gave it drink, the Prophet ﷺ saying that in every creature with a living liver there is a reward. He forbade the overloading of beasts of burden and the leaving of them hungry, and rebuked an owner whose camel, the report in Abū Dāwūd records, complained to the Prophet ﷺ of its hunger and overwork, weeping when the Prophet ﷺ stroked it, so that he turned to the man and asked whether he did not fear God in this animal that God had placed in his hand. He forbade the use of any living thing as a target for sport, the setting of beasts to fight, the branding of an animal on the face, and the killing of any creature beyond need. He told of a prophet before him who burned an anthill in anger over a single ant's bite and was rebuked by God for destroying a whole nation of creatures that glorified Him. He returned the chicks to a distressed bird that fluttered over the companions who had taken them, asking who had grieved this bird for its young. When he commanded the slaughter of animals for food he commanded it be done with mercy, the blade sharpened and hidden from the animal's sight, the killing swift, no beast slaughtered before another.

And his mercy to the weak among people was the same mercy extended, the orphan and the widow and the slave and the poor and the woman, all of whom the world of his birth had counted as nothing. Of the orphan whose burying alive the recitation had condemned, he made a charge of the highest honor, raising two fingers together and saying that he and the one who cares for an orphan would be in Paradise like these two. Of the slaves the markets traded as goods, he said in the sound books that they are your brothers whom God has placed under your hand, so feed them from what you eat and clothe them from what you wear and do not burden them beyond their strength, and if you burden them then help them, and he himself freed his servants and married the freed to the free. He commanded the kindness to women that his civilization had never imagined, charging the men with their good treatment as a trust from God, and made the care of daughters, the very daughters the pagans had buried, a path to Paradise, saying that whoever is tested with daughters and treats them well, they will be a shield for him from the Fire. The mercy to the worlds was not a phrase. It was a man who would not let an ant be burned or a cat be starved or a dog die of thirst or a daughter be despised, in a world that thought none of these worth a thought.

## The Households

His domestic life has been examined and misrepresented more than any part of him, and it deserves to be set out plainly and with reverence both. For the first twenty-five years of his marriage he had one wife only, Khadījah, and took no other while she lived, and she was some fifteen years his senior. The plural marriages all came after her death, in the Medinan years, and the great majority of them were marriages of alliance, protection, and instruction, not of appetite. They bound the tribes of Arabia to him through kinship, gave shelter and honor to the widows of fallen companions, and raised up among his wives teachers of the religion who would carry the intimate knowledge of the Prophet's practice to the whole community after him. Through ʿĀʾishah alone, the young wife of his later years who outlived him by decades, came a vast portion of the law and the example, for she had seen him in the privacy of the home as no man could, and the community learned its prayer and its conduct and its fasting in part from what she transmitted. His wives, the Mothers of the Believers as the recitation named them, were Sawdah and ʿĀʾishah and Ḥafṣah, Zaynab bint Khuzaymah who was called the mother of the poor for her charity and died soon after the marriage, Umm Salamah, Zaynab bint Jaḥsh, Juwayriyah and Umm Ḥabībah and Ṣafiyyah and Maymūnah, and the Coptic Māriyah who bore him Ibrāhīm, several of these the widows of companions or captives freed and honored by marriage, several the daughters of chiefs whose tribes were drawn to the faith by the bond.

Within the house he was, by ʿĀʾishah's own report in the sound books, in the service of his family, helping with their work until the call to prayer drew him out, and he was gentle and playful and just among them. The recitation legislated his household as it legislated the community, and two episodes within it became scripture. When his wives, in a season of difficulty, pressed him for more of the comforts of the world than his ascetic house provided, he was commanded to set before them a clear choice, the verse of the choosing. *O Prophet, say to your wives, if you desire the life of this world and its adornment, then come, I will provide for you and release you in a handsome manner. But if you desire God and His Messenger and the home of the Hereafter, then indeed God has prepared for the doers of good among you a great reward* (al-Aḥzāb 33:28-29). He offered them their freedom and the world if they wanted it, and every one of them, beginning with ʿĀʾishah, chose God and His Messenger ﷺ and the poverty of his house over the comfort he offered to give them. And the marriage that the enemies of the faith have most attacked, his marriage to Zaynab bint Jaḥsh, the divorced wife of his freedman and adopted son Zayd, the recitation itself addressed directly and turned into the abolition of a false custom, for the Arabs held an adopted son's wife forbidden as a true son's would be, and God broke that fiction by commanding the very marriage, that the believers might know the wives of their adopted sons were not forbidden to them, adoption conferring love and care but not the lineage and the prohibitions of blood. *So when Zayd had no longer any need of her, We married her to you, in order that there be no discomfort upon the believers concerning the wives of their adopted sons when they no longer have need of them. And the command of God is to be fulfilled* (al-Aḥzāb 33:37). The marriage that the slanderers cast as desire was, in the recitation's own framing, a legislation against a falsehood, the dismantling of a custom by the Prophet ﷺ submitting to bear the discomfort of it himself.

The gravest trial of his household was the slander cast at ʿĀʾishah herself, the affair the tradition calls the ifk, the lie. She was left behind by accident on the return from an expedition and brought back to the army by a companion who found her, and the hypocrites of Medina seized on it to whisper a vile accusation against her chastity, and the whisper spread until it tormented the Prophet ﷺ and his family for a month. ʿĀʾishah wept and fell ill, not knowing the talk at first and then crushed by it, until she could only say, as the prophet Jacob (AS) had said in his grief, that beautiful patience was hers and God's help was sought against what they described. And her vindication came not from a man's word but from heaven, the recitation descending to clear her name for all time and to scourge the slanderers, in the chapter of Light. *Indeed those who brought forth the lie are a group among you. Do not think it bad for you, rather it is good for you. For every person among them is what he has earned of the sin, and he who took upon himself the greater part of it will have a great punishment* (al-Nūr 24:11). The recitation declared the slander itself a manifest lie, rebuked the believers who had repeated it without bringing witnesses, and laid down the law of slander against chaste women to protect every woman after her, turning the agony of his own wife into a shield for the honor of all the believing women to the end of time. ʿĀʾishah said ever after that she had never imagined God would reveal concerning her affair words of the Quran to be recited, so small did she count herself, and that her innocence came down from the seventh heaven.

His children were born almost all to Khadījah, the sons al-Qāsim and ʿAbd Allāh who died in infancy, and the four daughters Zaynab, Ruqayyah, Umm Kulthūm, and Fāṭimah, and his last child Ibrāhīm born of Māriyah in his old age and dying as an infant. He buried, in the course of his life, every one of his children save Fāṭimah, who survived him by only months. Of Fāṭimah, the youngest and dearest, he said that she was a part of him, that whatever angered her angered him, and the love between them was a tenderness the whole community remembered. He who had begun life an orphan, fatherless and then motherless, ended it having buried his wife of a quarter century and six of his seven children, and bore each grief with the submission he had shown at the deathbed of the infant Ibrāhīm, the eye in tears and the heart grieving and the tongue saying only what pleased its Lord.

## The Worshipper

Beneath the statesman and the commander and the lawgiver, beneath the husband and the father and the friend, the foundation of the man was the worshipper, and unless this is seen the portrait is not complete, for everything else he was rested upon his relation to his Lord. He worshipped as no one before or since, and he worshipped despite the assurance, which he alone among men possessed, that he was already forgiven. The night prayer that swelled his feet, the gratitude that answered ʿĀʾishah's question, was the center of his hidden life, the long standing in the dark when the world slept, the recitation and the bowing and the prostration drawn out hour upon hour. He fasted often beyond the obligation, the Mondays and the Thursdays, the bright days of the middle of each month, much of the month of Shaʿbān, and the day of ʿĀshūrāʾ, and in the last ten nights of Ramadan he withdrew into the mosque in seclusion, the iʿtikāf, seeking the Night of Power in which the recitation had first descended. His tongue kept the remembrance of God in every state, and he asked forgiveness scores of times a day. And he had said of the prayer itself a thing that reveals the whole orientation of his soul, that the coolness of his eye, the delight and the rest of him, had been placed in the prayer, so that what other men sought in wealth and ease and the comforts of the world he found in standing before his Lord. The Prophet ﷺ who owned nothing and gave away what came to him, who lived on dates and water and died with his shield in pawn, was the richest of men in the only wealth he counted, the nearness to God that he pursued through the night long after he had been promised Paradise.

Seen rightly, his worship was not one province of the man set beside the others, the statesman here and the worshipper there, but the truing of the whole of him to a single measure. The recitation speaks of a balance God set into the very frame of creation. *And the heaven He raised, and He set the balance, that you not transgress within the balance. And establish weight in justice, and do not fall short in the balance* (al-Raḥmān 55:7-9). It is the just proportion by which the heavens are held, and by which a life is held no less. To be God-conscious, to have taqwā, was on this measure to stand true to that order, to bring the inner man and the outer act into one line, so that the prayer in the dark and the justice in the market and the mercy to the beast and the truthfulness in jest were not separate virtues laid side by side but one alignment showing many faces, the single orientation of a self set right with its Lord. And because the measure was God-consciousness and nothing else, the rank it conferred ran by no other currency. The recitation had cut every rival claim away. *O mankind, indeed We created you from a male and a female, and made you peoples and tribes that you may know one another. Indeed the most noble of you in the sight of God is the most God-conscious of you* (al-Ḥujurāt 49:13). The Prophet ﷺ had said the same from the camel on ʿArafāt, that no Arab stood above a non-Arab and no white above a black except by the fear of God. The man whose plainness hid him among his companions held the only height the recitation recognized, and he held it by the same taqwā that bowed him lowest.

This was the man, then, that the lovers drew. The radiant face that shone like the moon and the fragrance that lingered in the street. The character that God called great and that ʿĀʾishah called the Quran, the humility that hid him among his companions, the gentleness that never said uff to a servant in ten years. The day built on prayer and the night given to it, the table of dates and water that he never once disparaged, the single silver ring and the pawned armor, the honey and the black seed and the mercy that met the plague with patience. The smile that showed his teeth and the jest that was always true, the prolonged prostration under a child climbing his back and the tears at an infant's death, the cat and the dog and the thirsting beast and the despised daughter all gathered under his mercy, the household of honored widows and the wife whose innocence came down from heaven, and beneath it all the worshipper standing in the dark in gratitude. He was the excellent example the recitation named, not a distant ideal but a human being the companions could touch and smell and follow, and they followed him, and they set down what they saw, so that he might be followed still. What this character became when it was made into law, the rights it secured and the justice it rendered and the covenants it kept, is the matter of the Book that follows.

# VI · THE COVENANT-KEEPER

*A character becomes a civilization only when it is made into law, and the mercy and justice that the previous Book showed in the man, this Book shows in the order he built. He was not only a prophet and a worshipper but a head of state who wrote a constitution, a judge who decided between disputants, a commander who set the laws of war, a maker of treaties who never once broke faith, and a lawgiver who placed fixed rights in the hands of the women and the orphans and the slaves and the poor whom his world had counted as nothing. The recitation that came down across these years came down as legislation, and the legislation bears everywhere the same signature, the binding of the strong and the protection of the weak, justice rendered without regard to status or creed, and the covenant kept absolutely. This is the Book of the law he left, the outward form of the inward man.*

## The Constitution of Medina

The first act of his statecraft was a written document, and the historians of law have recognized it as a constitution in the full sense, perhaps the first of its kind. It was drawn up in the earliest days of Medina to order a city of feuding tribes and rival faiths into a single polity. The document the sources preserve as the Ṣaḥīfah did several things at once that no Arabian custom had done. It declared the believers, emigrant and helper, a single community, an ummah, distinct from all others, their primary bond now the faith and not the tribe. It brought the Jewish tribes of the city into one defensive confederation with the believers, naming them a community alongside the believers, with their religion to themselves and the believers' religion to the believers, each bearing its own expenses but all sharing in the defense of Medina against any who attacked it. It abolished the old order in which each clan pursued its own blood-vengeance in isolation, binding the parties of the covenant to stand together against aggression and to ransom their captives in kindness and justice among themselves. And it named the supreme arbitration of the new order, that whatever any of the parties disagreed upon in a serious matter was to be referred to God and to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. A city of three faiths and warring tribes was made, by a single written instrument, into one polity with one defense, one supreme court of appeal, and freedom of religion for each community in its own affairs. The reordering was radical, and it held, and it became the template for the relation of the new order to the peoples within it.

## The Judge

He sat in judgment between people daily, and his judicial practice established principles that the law of the believers would build upon for centuries. The first was that he judged as a man judges, by the evidence presented to him, and that his verdict bound the outward case but could not change the inward truth before God. The sound report in Bukhārī and Muslim preserves his own statement of this, that he was only a human being, and that disputants came to him, and that one of them might be more eloquent in his argument than the other so that the judge ruled in his favor according to what he heard, and that whoever was thereby awarded something of his brother's right by a false plea was awarded only a piece of the Fire, and should not take it. The judge decides by the evidence, and the man who wins a false verdict has won his own damnation. He laid down the burden of proof, that the claimant must bring evidence and the one who denies must swear the oath, the principle that has governed the courts of the believers ever since.

The second principle was that the law was no respecter of persons, and he enforced it against his own most beloved kin in words that became the charter of equal justice. When a woman of the noble clan of Makhzūm was caught in theft and her family, unwilling to see one of their rank punished, sought to have the most beloved of the companions intercede with the Prophet ﷺ to set aside the penalty, he rose in anger, the sound books record, and said that the nations before them had been destroyed precisely because when a noble among them stole they let him go and when a weak one stole they applied the penalty, and he swore by God that if Fāṭimah the daughter of Muhammad herself were to steal, he would cut off her hand. The law fell on the high and the low alike, and not even the Prophet's own daughter, were she a thief, would be spared it, and not even the dearest companion's intercession would lift it. And the third principle was that justice was owed to every person regardless of faith, that the believer's court was not a partisan instrument for the believers against others. When a Muslim of Medina committed a theft and tried to cast the blame upon an innocent Jew, and his clan sought to use the Prophet's authority to shield their man and condemn the Jew, the recitation itself came down to forbid it and to clear the innocent, commanding the Prophet ﷺ to judge by the truth God had shown him and not to be an advocate for the treacherous. *Indeed We have revealed to you the Book in truth, that you may judge between people by what God has shown you. And do not be an advocate for the deceitful* (al-Nisāʾ 4:105). The recitation made the standard absolute and named the temptation it forbade, the bending of justice toward one's own. *O you who believe, be persistently standing firm in justice, witnesses for God, even if it be against yourselves or parents and relatives* (al-Nisāʾ 4:135), and it forbade even the hatred of an enemy as a ground for injustice. *And do not let the hatred of a people lead you to be unjust. Be just, that is nearer to righteousness* (al-Māʾidah 5:8). Justice was owed to the relative and the stranger, the friend and the enemy, the believer and the unbeliever, without exception, for it was owed first to God.

## The Sanctity of Blood and Right

The charter he proclaimed on the plain of ʿArafāt at the Farewell Pilgrimage, the inviolability of every person's blood and property and honor, was not a sermon only but the foundation of his law, and it overturned the order of an age in which the strong took the weak's life and goods as a matter of course. He abolished the endless blood-feud of the tribes, in which a single killing could set clans warring for generations, and replaced it with a measured law of justice. The recitation legislated retaliation for murder, the qiṣāṣ, but bounded it strictly against the old excess in which a tribe might kill many for the loss of one, and it opened wide the door to forgiveness and blood-money in place of vengeance, and it named the wisdom of the law in a phrase of compressed brilliance. *And there is for you in legal retribution life, O people of understanding, that you may become righteous* (al-Baqarah 2:179). A known and limited penalty, applied by the community and not by the feud, and openable to mercy, was itself the preservation of life, ending the cycles of escalating vengeance that the old order's lawlessness had fed. And the recitation set the value of the single human life at the value of all life, in words that have no parallel in the codes of the ancient world. *Whoever kills a soul, unless for a soul or for corruption in the land, it is as if he had killed all of mankind. And whoever saves one, it is as if he had saved all of mankind* (al-Māʾidah 5:32). The murder of one was the murder of humanity, and the saving of one was the saving of humanity, and upon that measure the sanctity of blood was built into the law.

## The Treaties and the Pledges

He was a maker of covenants, and the fidelity with which he kept them is among the most documented features of his statecraft, attested even by his enemies, for it was the keeping of his word that Heraclius named as a mark of the true prophet. He bound communities to him by written treaty, the Constitution with the people of Medina, the truce of Ḥudaybiyah with Quraysh whose humiliating terms he honored to the letter though they galled his followers, the covenant with the Christians of Najrān that guaranteed them their faith and their churches and their priests under his protection in exchange for a tribute, the terms with the people of the northern frontier, the settlement with the Jews of Khaybar who remained on their land. And the principle that governed all of them was the absolute keeping of the covenant, a fidelity the recitation made law. *And fulfill the covenant, for the covenant will be questioned* (al-Isrāʾ 17:34), it commanded, and it opened the chapter of the Table with the same charge, *O you who believe, fulfill the contracts* (al-Māʾidah 5:1). Even where he feared a treaty partner might betray him, he was forbidden to break faith secretly, but commanded to renounce the treaty openly and on equal terms first, so that there could be no treachery on his side. *And if you fear betrayal from a people, throw back to them their treaty on equal terms. Indeed God does not love the treacherous* (al-Anfāl 8:58). And he was commanded to honor in full the treaties of those who had kept faith with him, to their term (al-Tawbah 9:4). The covenant extended its protection over the non-Muslim who lived under the authority of the believers, the dhimmī, the protected person, and the Prophet ﷺ set a fearful guard over that protection in a report the collections carry, declaring that whoever wronged a covenanted person, or burdened him beyond his capacity, or took from him anything without his consent, he himself, the Prophet ﷺ, would be that person's adversary on the Day of Resurrection. The man who broke no covenant with a king made himself, in the next world, the prosecutor of any believer who broke covenant with a protected non-believer.

## The Letters and the Wider World

When the truce of Ḥudaybiyah opened a space of peace, he turned his statecraft outward to the powers beyond Arabia, and the letters he sent to Heraclius and Khosrow and the Negus and the governor of Egypt and the Arab kings of the frontiers were instruments at once of religion and of diplomacy, opening formal relations with the empires while calling their rulers to the One God. The letters were sealed with his silver seal, carried by chosen envoys schooled in their delivery, and framed upon the Quranic invitation to the shared ground of monotheism. *Say, O People of the Book, come to a word equal between us and you, that we worship none but God and associate nothing with Him, and that none of us take others as lords besides God* (Āl ʿImrān 3:64). They were the act of a head of state announcing his polity to the world on equal terms, neither cringing before the empires nor needlessly provoking them, and the responses, the emperor's grave recognition and the Persian king's contemptuous tearing, became, as the chronicle has shown, the seeds of the two empires' opposite fates.

## The Law of War and Peace

He made war when war was forced upon him, and in making it he established a discipline of restraint that stands centuries ahead of the practice of his age and of ages long after it. The recitation set the cause and the limit together, permitting the fighting of those who fought the believers while forbidding the transgression of bounds. *And fight in the way of God those who fight you, but do not transgress. Indeed God does not like transgressors* (al-Baqarah 2:190). And it made peace the preferred end, commanding the inclining to peace whenever the enemy inclined to it. *And if they incline to peace, then incline to it, and rely upon God* (al-Anfāl 8:61). Within the fighting itself the Prophet ﷺ and the commanders he instructed laid down prohibitions the sound reports preserve, that the non-combatant was not to be touched, no woman killed, no child, no aged person, no hired laborer, none of the monks and hermits secluded in their cells, that there was to be no mutilation of the dead, no treachery, no breaking of a pledge of safety, no killing of one who had been granted safe-conduct, the amān, that crops and trees and wells were not to be wantonly destroyed, that the enemy's dead were to be left their dignity. And the captive, the recitation taught, was to be fed and clothed, the believers praised for giving their own food to the captive and the orphan and the poor for the love of God, seeking no reward and no thanks (al-Insān 76:8-9). The man who lay among the prisoners of war in the hand of the early Muslims was, by the Prophet's command, to be fed from what his captor ate and clothed from what his captor wore. A law of war that spared the woman and the child and the monk, that forbade mutilation and treachery, that protected the crop and the well, and that fed and clothed the captive, was a thing the ancient world had not seen, and it flowed from the same character that would not let an ant be burned.

## The Economy and the Poor

His legislation reached into the wealth of the community and bound it to justice, and at the center of his economic order stood a war on the two evils he saw devouring the poor, the usury that fed on their need and the hoarding that starved their share. The recitation declared usury, the riba by which the lender multiplied his return upon the borrower's distress, a thing against which God and His Messenger ﷺ declared war, in language used nowhere else in the Book. *O you who believe, fear God and give up what remains of interest, if you are believers. And if you do not, then be informed of a war from God and His Messenger* (al-Baqarah 2:278-279), permitting the lender his principal but forbidding him the increase, that he wrong not and be not wronged. And the Prophet ﷺ abolished the usury of the old order at the Farewell Pilgrimage, beginning with the interest owed to his own uncle, taking nothing for his own family first. Against the hoarding of wealth the recitation pronounced a terrible warning, that those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in the way of God will have it heated in the fire of Hell and their foreheads and sides and backs branded with it (al-Tawbah 9:34-35). And in place of hoarding it established the right of the poor in the wealth of the rich, the zakāt, not a charity bestowed at whim but a fixed due, a known right, that the recitation located within the very definition of the righteous. *And in their wealth was a known right for the petitioner and the deprived* (al-Dhāriyāt 51:19), and it named the categories upon whom the zakāt must be spent, the poor and the needy and the debtors and the wayfarer and the freeing of slaves and the rest (al-Tawbah 9:60). He commanded honest dealing in trade, fair measure and full weight, and the recitation opened a chapter with woe to the defrauders who take in full measure from people and give them short (al-Muṭaffifīn 83:1-3). And he disowned the cheat altogether, declaring in the sound collection of Muslim, when he thrust his hand into a heap of grain for sale and found it wet beneath the dry surface and the seller admitted the rain had touched it, that the seller ought to have put the wet on top for the buyer to see, and that whoever deceives is not of him. The economy he built was an economy of justice, the increase of usury forbidden, the hoard condemned, the poor given a fixed right, the measure made honest, and the cheat cast out of the community.

## The Covenant with the Weak

The deepest and most lasting work of his law was the placing of fixed, enforceable rights into the hands of those whom his world, and most of the world before and long after him, had held as property or as nothing, the women and the orphans and the slaves and the poor. For the women of a society that had buried its daughters and inherited its widows as goods, the recitation legislated a revolution. It gave women a fixed and guaranteed share of inheritance where they had owned nothing and had themselves been inherited, declaring that for men is a share of what parents and relatives leave, and for women is a share of what parents and relatives leave, a portion made obligatory (al-Nisāʾ 4:7), and it set out the shares in detail (4:11-12). It made the marriage-gift, the dower, the woman's own property to keep, not a price paid to her guardian. It required her consent to her marriage. And it forbade outright the old practice of inheriting women against their will and of mistreating them to seize back their property, commanding instead that they be lived with in kindness (al-Nisāʾ 4:19). For the orphan whose property the strong had devoured, it set a fierce guard, commanding the giving of orphans their property and forbidding the exchange of the bad for the good and the consuming of their wealth, and warning that those who consume the property of orphans unjustly consume only fire into their bellies and will burn in a blaze (al-Nisāʾ 4:10). For the slave it opened every door to freedom, making the freeing of a slave an expiation for sins and a supreme act of virtue, the steep path the recitation praised, the freeing of a neck (al-Balad 90:11-13), and it established the contract of self-purchase by which a slave could buy his own freedom, commanding the masters to make the contract with those slaves who sought it and to give them from the wealth of God (al-Nūr 24:33). And it gathered the whole circle of those owed kindness into a single sweeping command that placed the weak beside the worship of God Himself. *Worship God and associate nothing with Him, and to parents do good, and to relatives, and orphans, and the needy, and the near neighbor, and the distant neighbor, and the companion at your side, and the traveler, and those your right hands possess* (al-Nisāʾ 4:36). The law was the institution of the mercy the portrait had shown, the same tenderness that returned the bird its chicks and gave the thirsting dog its water now written into the rights of the widow and the orphan and the slave, made binding and enforceable, secured not by the goodwill of the strong but by the command of God.

This, then, was the order he built, the outward form of the inward man. The constitution that made one polity of warring tribes and rival faiths. The justice that fell on the noble thief and the Prophet's own daughter alike and stood firm for the innocent Jew against the guilty Muslim. The sanctity of blood that valued the single life at the value of all life. The covenants kept so absolutely that an emperor knew the prophet by his faithfulness, and the protection thrown over the non-believer under his rule so completely that the Prophet ﷺ made himself the adversary of any who wronged him. The law of war that spared the woman and the monk and fed the captive. The economy that warred on usury and the hoard and gave the poor a fixed right. And the rights placed in the hands of the women and the orphans and the slaves, made law where they had been air. The character whose name was the Quran had become a polity whose law was justice, and the covenant-keeper kept faith with God by keeping faith with every soul his order touched. Yet the law governs the seen world only, the realm of the courts and the markets and the treaties. The Prophet ﷺ also opened a window upon the unseen, upon the realities beyond the reach of the law and the senses, and to that horizon the next Book turns.

# VII · THE UNSEEN HORIZON

*The law of the previous Book governs the seen world, the courts and the markets and the treaties. But the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was sent to open a window upon the world the senses cannot reach, the realities behind the veil, and the recitation and the sound tradition carry his testimony to them in detail no other founder of a religion has left. He was taken in a single night above the seven heavens to the Lote Tree of the utmost boundary. He carried back the words in which his Lord speaks of Himself. He described the majesty and the light, the Hour and its signs, the breaking open of the graves and the gathering of all who ever lived, the terror of the standing and the mercy of the intercession, the Basin where the believers drink and the Bridge over the Fire that all must cross, and at the summit of it all the felicity beyond which there is no felicity, the sight of the Face of God. This Book follows him to that horizon. It is written in the register the matter demands, and it holds to what the Book and the sound reports transmit, presenting the imagery in its own power as the tradition received it.*

## The Ascension

The Night Journey was set in its chronological place in the second Book, the carrying of the Prophet ﷺ from the Sacred Mosque at Mecca to the Farthest Mosque at Jerusalem and the raising of him through the heavens, and the recitation's opening of it was given there, *Glory to the One who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque, whose surroundings We have blessed, to show him of Our signs* (al-Isrāʾ 17:1). Here the journey is opened to its summit, for it was the single greatest disclosure of the unseen ever granted to a human being, and the chapter of the Star describes its furthest reach in verses of veiled and tremendous restraint. After leading the prophets in prayer at Jerusalem he was raised, by the sound narrations of Bukhārī and Muslim, through the seven heavens, and at each he met a prophet stationed there, Adam in the first and Jesus (AS) and John (AS) in another and Joseph (AS) and Idrīs (AS) and Aaron (AS) and Moses (AS), until at the seventh he came to Abraham (AS) seated against the Frequented House, the Bayt al-Maʿmūr, the heavenly sanctuary that seventy thousand angels enter each day never to return, the celestial pattern of the House on earth that the angels circle as men circle the Kaʿba below.

Beyond the seventh heaven he was brought to the boundary of the created order itself, to the Lote Tree of the utmost limit, the Sidrat al-Muntahā, past which no creature passes, and the chapter of the Star renders what occurred there in language that says everything by withholding everything. *And he certainly saw him in another descent, at the Lote Tree of the Boundary, near it is the Garden of Refuge, when there covered the Lote Tree that which covered it. The sight did not swerve, nor did it transgress. He certainly saw of the greatest signs of his Lord* (al-Najm 53:13-18). Something covered the Lote Tree that the words will not name, a radiance or a host of angels or a glory beyond utterance, and the gaze of the Prophet ﷺ neither turned aside in fear nor strained past its bound in presumption, the perfect courtesy of the seeing, and he saw of the greatest signs of his Lord. The chapter speaks also of a nearness almost beyond the bearing of language, that the Prophet ﷺ drew near and came down, or was drawn near, until he was at the distance of two bow-lengths or nearer, *then he approached and descended, and was at a distance of two bow-lengths or nearer, and He revealed to His servant what He revealed* (al-Najm 53:8-10). What was revealed in that nearness, the recitation leaves sealed.

And it was there, at that summit, that the five daily prayers were ordained, not sent down to the earth by an angel but received by the Prophet ﷺ in the highest place a created being has ever stood, the one ordinance given above all the heavens, fifty at the first that were lightened, on Moses (AS)' counsel and the Prophet's returning, to five in the doing and fifty in the reward. The believer who stands five times a day stands in the gift brought back from the Lote Tree. As to the deepest question the Ascension raises, whether the Prophet ﷺ saw his Lord with the eye of his head on that night, the companions themselves differed, and the difference is an honored one that the tradition has never forced to a single answer. ʿĀʾishah held that what he saw at the Lote Tree was Gabriel in his immense angelic form, filling the horizon, and that no eye in this life encompasses God. Others among the companions, Ibn ʿAbbās foremost, held that the Prophet ﷺ was granted a vision of his Lord, whether with the eye or with the heart. The recitation in the chapter of the Star says only that the heart did not lie about what it saw and that the sight did not swerve, and the tradition has been content to leave the seeing where the Book left it, in a reverent silence that affirms the greatness of what was shown without prying into its manner. What is certain is that a son of Adam was carried to the boundary of creation and shown the greatest signs of his Lord, and brought back the prayer and the certainty, and that no one before or after has stood where he stood.

## The Words of the Lord

The Prophet ﷺ carried back, and continued to receive, a body of sayings in which God speaks in the first person, the Divine sayings the tradition calls the Ḥadīth Qudsī, distinct from the Quran in their wording yet conveying the speech of God, and through them the unseen Lord discloses His nearness and His mercy in terms of overwhelming intimacy. The greatest of them, preserved in the sound collection of Muslim on the authority of Abū Dharr, has God address His servants directly across the whole of creation, declaring that He has forbidden injustice to Himself and made it forbidden among them, so let them not wrong one another, that all of them are astray but those He guides, so let them seek His guidance, all of them hungry but those He feeds, all of them naked but those He clothes, so let them ask Him, and that were the first of them and the last, the human of them and the jinn, to stand as one upon the most God-conscious heart among them, it would add nothing to His dominion, and were they to stand upon the most wicked, it would take nothing from it, for it is only their own deeds that He counts up for them and then repays them in full, so whoever finds good, let him praise God, and whoever finds otherwise, let him blame only himself. The infinite self-sufficiency of God and the utter dependence of the creature are stated in that saying with a force the philosophers never matched.

And against that majesty the same sayings disclose a tenderness that has no equal. God says, in the sound report of Bukhārī and Muslim, that He is as His servant thinks of Him, and that He is with His servant when the servant remembers Him, and that whoever draws near to Him by a hand's span, He draws near to by an arm's length, and whoever draws near by an arm's length, He draws near to by a fathom, and whoever comes to Him walking, He comes to running. He says that His mercy prevails over His wrath, or precedes it, a saying written, the tradition holds, above His Throne. He tells of dividing mercy into a hundred parts, sending down but one to the whole of creation, by which every mother tends her child and every beast its young, and keeping ninety-nine with Himself for the Day of Resurrection. And He descends, in the saying of the night that Bukhārī and Muslim preserve, in the last third of every night to the nearest heaven, and calls, who is calling upon Me that I may answer him, who is asking of Me that I may give him, who is seeking My forgiveness that I may forgive him, until the dawn. The unseen God of the Prophet's disclosure is not a remote first cause but a Lord who descends in the dark to ask His sleeping servants whether any will turn to Him. And in the saying of the friends of God, the awliyāʾ, in Bukhārī, He declares that He makes war on whoever shows enmity to a friend of His, that His servant draws near to Him by nothing more beloved than the duties He has made obligatory, and continues to draw near by the voluntary acts of devotion until He loves him, and that when He loves him He becomes the hearing with which he hears and the sight with which he sees and the hand with which he grasps and the foot with which he walks, and that were such a servant to ask Him, He would give, and were he to seek refuge in Him, He would protect him. The servant beloved of God is so wholly governed by God's will that his very faculties move in obedience, the soul so surrendered that the action of God flows through it. The tradition has received these sayings in their full power, affirming the nearness and the descent and the love as God has stated them.

## The Light and the Majesty

The Prophet ﷺ disclosed the majesty of the God behind the veil, and the recitation set the seal upon it in the verse the believers have held the greatest in the Book, the Verse of the Throne. *God, there is no deity except Him, the Living, the Sustainer of all. Neither drowsiness overtakes Him nor sleep. To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Who is it that can intercede with Him except by His permission. He knows what is before them and what is behind them, and they encompass nothing of His knowledge except what He wills. His Throne extends over the heavens and the earth, and their preservation tires Him not. And He is the Most High, the Most Great* (al-Baqarah 2:255). The Throne over the heavens and the earth, the knowledge that nothing escapes, the sustaining of all things without fatigue, the permission without which not even intercession can be offered, this was the God the Prophet ﷺ proclaimed, named by the most beautiful names, the Merciful, the Sustainer, the Forgiver, the Mighty, the names by which the believer calls upon Him. The recitation set that Throne at the summit of an order made in measured stages, telling that God created the heavens and the earth and all between them in six days and then established Himself over the Throne, the establishing the Book states and does not explain, leaving its manner where it leaves the manner of all His attributes, in a knowledge it does not hand to the creature. *Indeed your Lord is God, who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then established Himself over the Throne. He covers the night with the day, each chasing the other in haste, and the sun and the moon and the stars are subjected by His command. Unquestionably, His is the creation and the command. Blessed is God, the Lord of the worlds* (al-Aʿrāf 7:54). And the recitation marked the deepest difference between this account and the older tellings the Arabs had heard, that the Maker of the six days did not rest upon a seventh, nor tire at all, for weariness belongs to a thing that is itself held up, and He holds all things and is held by none. *And We created the heavens and the earth and what is between them in six days, and no weariness touched Us* (Qāf 50:38). The six days are the making, and the establishment over the Throne is the dominion, and the rest that the older tellings granted their Creator the recitation refuses Him, for the One who holds the heavens and the earth lest they cease has no need to pause. And the Prophet ﷺ described the veil between that God and His creation as a veil of light, saying in the sound collection of Muslim that God does not sleep and that it does not befit Him to sleep, that He lowers the scale and raises it, that the deeds of the night are raised to Him before the deeds of the day and the deeds of the day before the deeds of the night, and that His veil is light, and were He to remove it the glories of His Face would burn away everything of His creation that His sight reached. The Face whose unveiling would consume creation is the Face the believers are promised they will look upon in the Garden, and the distance between those two truths, the burning and the beholding, is the whole mystery of the mercy.

Read to its depth, the Verse of the Throne is not only a proclamation of majesty but the ground of an entire understanding of what it is to exist. The Living, the Sustainer of all, al-Ḥayy al-Qayyūm, is the One who stands by Himself and by whom all else is made to stand. Drowsiness and sleep belong to a thing that is held up from outside itself, that must pause and be renewed, and the verse strips both from God in one breath, for He is sustained by nothing and sustains everything. Every other thing that is, the heavens and the earth and all that lies between, is carried in being from one moment to the next by a hand it does not see, and would slip back into nothing the instant that holding ceased, as the recitation says elsewhere, that God holds the heavens and the earth lest they cease, and that were they to cease, none could hold them after Him (Fāṭir 35:41). Nothing in creation owns its own existence. The sun does not kindle itself, the heart does not beat by its own command, and the recitation states the truth of it without ornament, that everyone upon the earth will perish, and there remains the Face of your Lord, full of majesty and honor (al-Raḥmān 55:26-27; cf. al-Qaṣaṣ 28:88). He alone is the First, before whom there is nothing, and the Last, after whom there is nothing, the Outer and the Inner (al-Ḥadīd 57:3), and beside Him all things are lent and passing, lit by a light not their own. To see this is the root of the worship the Prophet ﷺ called men to, for the one who knows that he is held every instant above nothing by a Lord who need not hold him turns to that Lord with the whole of himself, and finds Him, as the sayings of the night disclose, nearer than the holding itself.

Behind the veil lay an unseen creation the Prophet ﷺ unveiled in part, the angels who do not disobey God in what He commands and who do as they are commanded, the bearers of the Throne, the recording angels at every person's shoulders, the angel of death, Gabriel of the revelation whom the Prophet ﷺ saw twice in his true form filling the horizon with six hundred wings. He disclosed the jinn, the unseen creation of smokeless fire, a company of whom heard him reciting the Quran and believed, the recitation devoting a chapter to their testimony, *say, it has been revealed to me that a company of the jinn listened, and they said, indeed we have heard an amazing recitation that guides to right conduct, so we have believed in it* (al-Jinn 72:1-2). And he disclosed the writing of the decree, telling that the first thing God created was the Pen and that He commanded it to write, and it wrote all that would be until the Day of Resurrection, the whole of what would come inscribed in the Preserved Tablet that the recitation names as the place of the glorious Quran itself. *Rather, it is a glorious Quran, in a Preserved Tablet* (al-Burūj 85:21-22). Behind the seen world the Prophet ﷺ showed a created order of angels and jinn and a written decree, all of it sustained by the Living who neither slumbers nor sleeps.

The written decree raised at once the question that has tried every people, how a man can be answerable for a deed already written before he was made, and the recitation neither hid the difficulty nor undid it by surrendering one of its sides. It held both at their full strength. The decree is total, for not a leaf falls but He knows it, and there is nothing wet or dry but it is in a clear record (al-Anʿām 6:59), and the Pen has written what will be. And the choice is real, for the recitation makes the human deed the very thing the Judgment weighs, lays the turning toward good to God and the turning toward harm to the self, *whatever good befalls you is from God, and whatever evil befalls you is from yourself* (al-Nisāʾ 4:79), and sets the matter before each soul as a road it must walk by its own feet, *say, the truth is from your Lord, so whoever wills, let him believe, and whoever wills, let him disbelieve* (al-Kahf 18:29).
