The two are not balanced against each other as rivals, the one stealing what the other claims. The knowing and the writing are God's, encompassing all, and the doing and the earning are the servant's, real enough to be judged, and the recitation closes the circle by setting the human will within the divine and not against it, *and you do not will except that God wills, the Lord of the worlds* (al-Takwīr 81:29). The Prophet ﷺ taught his companions accordingly to labor as though everything turned on their striving and to trust as though everything turned on their Lord, and forbade them to make the decree a pillow for idleness, telling them to act, for each is eased toward that for which he was made. The dignity the recitation gave the human creature was exactly this, that inside a decree which misses nothing he is still the one who answers, the one whose yes or no is his own to give, the turning that Adam made when he fell and rose, and that the refuser would not.

## The Hour and Its Signs

He spoke much of the Hour, the end of the world and the Day of Resurrection, with a certainty about its coming and a refusal to name its time that the recitation made the model of all knowledge of it. When he was asked when the Hour would be, he answered, in the famous report of the angel Gabriel who came in the form of a man to question him before his companions, that the one asked knew no more of it than the questioner, that its time was known to God alone, and the recitation said the same, that the knowledge of the Hour is with God (Luqmān 31:34), and commanded him, when they pressed him about it, to say only that its knowledge is with his Lord (al-Aʿrāf 7:187). But though its hour was hidden, its signs he named, telling Gabriel in the same report that among its tokens would be that the slave-girl would give birth to her mistress, and that the barefoot, naked, destitute herdsmen of the desert would be seen competing in the raising of tall buildings. And he taught that his own coming was itself a sign that the Hour was near, holding up two fingers side by side and saying, in the sound books, that he and the Hour were sent like these two, the nearness of the one finger to the other the nearness of his age to the end.

He described the greater signs that would precede the Hour, the upheavals that the tradition transmits in detail, the appearance of the great deceiver, the false messiah the Dajjāl, who would claim divinity and try the faith of all the earth with his wonders, and the descent of Jesus (AS) son of Mary, the prophet whose place in the chain the next Book will set, who would return to break the cross and slay the deceiver and rule by the law of the final revelation. He told of the release of Gog and Magog, the corrupting hordes whom Dhū al-Qarnayn had walled away in the first Book of this work and whose barrier the recitation said would be leveled when the promise of God came, pouring forth at the end as the recitation foretold (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:96). He named the rising of the sun from its setting place in the west, after which no faith would profit a soul that had not believed before, and the smoke, and the Beast, and the great sinkings of the earth, and the fire that would drive mankind to their final gathering. And then the blowing of the Trumpet, the recitation describing two blasts, the first at which all in the heavens and the earth would fall dead in terror except whom God willed, and the second at which they would rise and stand, looking on. *And the Trumpet will be blown, and whoever is in the heavens and whoever is on the earth will fall dead, except whom God wills. Then it will be blown again, and at once they will be standing, looking on* (al-Zumar 39:68). The world would end, and then the dead of all the ages would stand. The recitation gave that ending an image of a thing rolled shut and put away, *the Day We will fold the heaven like the folding of a scroll for the records. As We began the first creation, We will repeat it, a promise binding upon Us. Indeed, We will do it* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:104). The sky that had stood spread above every generation as the very figure of permanence would be furled like a written page, and only the One who had spread it would remain.

## The Resurrection and the Gathering

The raising of the dead was the doctrine the idolaters of Mecca had mocked above all others, that crumbled bones could live again, and the recitation answered their mockery by pointing to the One who made them the first time. *And he presents for Us an example and forgets his own creation. He says, who will give life to bones while they are decayed. Say, He will give them life who produced them the first time, and He is, of all creation, Knowing* (Yā Sīn 36:78-79). The God who made a man from nothing could remake him from dust, and the recitation declared He could restore even the fine ridges of his fingertips. *Does man think We will not assemble his bones. Yes, We are able to proportion his fingertips* (al-Qiyāmah 75:3-4). And the Prophet ﷺ described the gathering that would follow the second blast, all who had ever lived raised from their graves and assembled upon one ground, and he described their state in the sound report of ʿĀʾishah, that they would be gathered barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised as they were first created, and when ʿĀʾishah, startled, asked whether the men and women would not then look upon one another's nakedness, he answered that the matter of that Day would be too grave, too overwhelming, for any of them to give a thought to such a thing. He described the sun drawn near above the heads of the assembled, the people sunk in their sweat each according to his deeds, the standing prolonged across an immensity of time, the whole of creation halted in dread before the Judgment, none able to speak except by permission. The length of that Day is past all earthly reckoning, for the recitation measures it not in the days of men but in a scale that dwarfs them, naming a Day whose measure is fifty thousand years (al-Maʿārij 70:4), and teaching that the very counting of time is not the same before God as it is below. *And they urge you to hasten the punishment. But God will never fail in His promise. And indeed a day with your Lord is like a thousand years of those which you count* (al-Ḥajj 22:47). The measures by which a man weighs his brief life are not the measures of the One before whom he will stand. It is from the agony of that standing, the tradition teaches, that the great intercession would be sought.

## The Intercession

The most consoling of all the Prophet's disclosures of the unseen is the great intercession, the Shafāʿa al-Kubrā, and he told it in the sound collections of Bukhārī and Muslim as a scene that places him above all the prophets at the moment of mankind's greatest need. The people, crushed beyond endurance by the length and terror of the standing, would seek someone to intercede with their Lord to begin the Reckoning and relieve them, and they would go first to Adam, the father of mankind, and ask him to intercede, and he would excuse himself and send them to Noah (AS). And they would go to Noah (AS), and he would excuse himself and send them to Abraham (AS), and Abraham (AS) would send them to Moses (AS), and Moses (AS) to Jesus (AS), each of the greatest prophets saying, in the narration, myself, myself, conscious before that majesty of his own station, until they came at last to Muhammad ﷺ, and he would say, I am for it, I am the one. And he would go and fall in prostration beneath the Throne of his Lord, and God would open upon him praises and glorifications of Himself that the Prophet ﷺ had not known before, and he would remain prostrate until it was said to him, raise your head, ask and you will be given, intercede and your intercession will be accepted. This is the Praised Station, the Maqām Maḥmūd, that the recitation had promised him at the end of the verse of the night prayer, *and from the night, pray with it as additional worship for you, that your Lord may raise you to a Praised Station* (al-Isrāʾ 17:79), the station of universal intercession at which the Reckoning of all creation begins by his asking.

His intercession would not end there, for the Prophet ﷺ taught that he would intercede again and again, for the entering of the believers into Paradise, and most movingly for the sinners of his own community. He said in the sound books that every prophet had been given one supplication that God would surely answer, and that each had hastened to use his, but that he had withheld his own, reserving it as an intercession for his community on the Day of Resurrection, so that the dearest gift granted him he had saved not for himself but for them. And he declared, in the report the collections carry, that his intercession was for the people of the grave sins of his community, the very ones who most needed it, that even those of his followers who had fallen into great wrong, so long as they had died upon the faith, would not be abandoned, but reached at the last by the intercession he had saved for them. The hope of the believer rests in no small part upon that withheld supplication, the prayer the Prophet ﷺ kept back through all his life of need, to spend it on his community when nothing else could save them.

## The Basin and the Bridge

He described two things that wait for his community at the crossing into the next world, a mercy and a trial, the Basin and the Bridge. The Basin, the Ḥawḍ, is the great pool granted to the Prophet ﷺ at which his community will drink before the Judgment, and he described it in the sound books in the language of a man who had seen it, its water whiter than milk and sweeter than honey and cooler than snow, its fragrance finer than musk, its cups as many as the stars of the sky, its breadth a month's journey, fed by the heavenly river of abundance, al-Kawthar, the river the recitation gave him in the chapter of that name, *indeed We have granted you al-Kawthar* (al-Kawthar 108:1), and he said that whoever drinks of the Basin a single draught will never thirst again. He told too, with sorrow, that some who claimed to be of his community would be driven away from his Basin for having turned back after him, and that he would say of them, my Lord, they are of my people, and be told that he did not know what they innovated after him.

And he described the Bridge, the Ṣirāṭ, stretched over the back of Hell, that every soul must cross to reach the Garden, finer, in the narrations, than a hair and sharper than the edge of a sword, over which the people would pass according to their deeds, some like the blink of an eye, some like lightning, some like the wind, some like swift horses, some running, some walking, some crawling, and some snatched by hooks and cast into the Fire below, while the messengers stood at its edge calling out, Lord, deliver, deliver. The believers would cross by their own light, the light the recitation says will run before them and on their right (al-Ḥadīd 57:12), each according to the measure of his faith. And above the crossing the Scales would be set, the Mīzān in which the deeds are weighed, the recitation promising that no soul will be wronged in the least, *and We place the scales of justice for the Day of Resurrection, so no soul will be treated unjustly at all. And if there is even the weight of a mustard seed, We will bring it forth, and sufficient are We as accountant* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:47), the one whose scales are heavy with good in a pleasant life and the one whose scales are light in the abyss (al-Qāriʿah 101:6-9). And the books of deeds would be given out, into the right hand of the saved who would call to their companions to come and read their record, and behind the back or into the left hand of the lost (al-Ḥāqqah 69:19-25). The crossing of the next world is, in the Prophet's disclosure, a passage of light and weight and water, the Basin of his mercy before it and the Bridge of the Reckoning across it.

## The Garden and the Fire

At the end of the crossing lay the two final abodes, and the Prophet ﷺ described them as the tradition has always described them, in vivid and unflinching imagery, and this Book presents that imagery as he gave it. Of the Fire he spoke as a reality of terrible heat and depth and grief, the recitation naming it the crusher that mounts to the hearts (al-Humazah 104:4-7), and the tradition transmitting its descent and its punishment, a warning held over the heedless and the cruel and the deniers, dwelt upon not for its own sake but as the alternative the mercy labored to spare them. Over the Fire, the recitation says, are nineteen, and in the same breath it discloses that the number itself was set as a trial, a thing the deniers would seize upon and scoff at while it only deepened the certainty of those who believed, the one sign read two ways by two kinds of heart. *Over it are nineteen. And We have made the keepers of the Fire none but angels, and We have not made their number except as a trial for those who disbelieve, that those given the Scripture may be certain and those who believe may increase in faith, and that those given the Scripture and the believers may not doubt, and that those in whose hearts is disease and the disbelievers may say, what does God intend by this as an example. Thus does God send astray whom He wills and guide whom He wills. And none knows the soldiers of your Lord but He* (al-Muddaththir 74:30-31). The strangeness that hardened the scoffer settled the believer, and the recitation named the number plainly as a thing sent down to divide the two. But it is upon the Garden that the Prophet ﷺ and the recitation lingered, the abode of the merciful named on nearly every page of the Book, the gardens beneath which the rivers flow, the phrase that recurs more than any other promise. He described its levels rising one above another, and named its summit al-Firdaws, the highest Garden beneath the Throne of the Merciful, from which the rivers of all the Garden spring. The recitation described its rivers, *rivers of water unspoiled, and rivers of milk whose taste never changes, and rivers of wine delicious to the drinkers, and rivers of purified honey* (Muhammad 47:15), and its dwellings and its shade and its fruit ever near. And the Prophet ﷺ disclosed a thing about the joy of it that lies past all description, a Divine saying preserved in the sound books in which God declares that He has prepared for His righteous servants what no eye has ever seen, and no ear has ever heard, and what has never crossed the heart of any human being. The deepest pleasure of the Garden is that God removes from the hearts of its people every trace of rancor and grudge they carried, so that they enter it as brothers upon couches facing one another (al-Ḥijr 15:47), and they say, the recitation records, praise be to God who has removed from us all grief (Fāṭir 35:34), and who fulfilled His promise (al-Zumar 39:74).

And above the rivers and the dwellings and the removal of all grief, at the very summit of the felicity of the Garden, the Prophet ﷺ disclosed the gift beside which all the rest is small, the Vision of the Face of God, and the recitation states it plainly, in the chapter of the Resurrection, in words the tradition has affirmed exactly as they stand. *Faces, that Day, will be radiant, looking at their Lord* (al-Qiyāmah 75:22-23). The radiant faces of the saved will look upon their Lord, and the recitation seals the truth of it from the other side by declaring that the wicked will be veiled from their Lord on that Day (al-Muṭaffifīn 83:15), the veiling of the lost the proof of the beholding of the saved, and by promising the doers of good the best reward and something more (Yūnus 10:26), the something more, the ziyādah, that the tradition has always understood as the Vision itself. The Prophet ﷺ described it in the sound collections of Bukhārī and Muslim, telling his companions that they would see their Lord on the Day of Resurrection as they see the moon on the night it is full, with no crowding and no doubt and no difficulty in the seeing, plainly and without obstruction. And he taught, in the sound collection of Muslim, that when the people of the Garden have entered it, God will ask them whether they wish Him to give them anything more, and they, having every delight, will not know what more there could be, until He removes the veil, and they look upon His Face, and the Prophet ﷺ said that they will not have been given anything more beloved to them than the looking upon their Lord. The Face whose unveiling in this world would burn away creation is unveiled in the Garden to the radiant faces of the saved, and the beholding of it is the summit of every felicity, the something more, the reward past the reward. The tradition affirms this Vision as the supreme gift, real and granted, holding to the words of the Book without prying into the manner of a seeing that no likeness can capture.

This is the horizon the Prophet ﷺ opened, the world the law of the previous Book could not reach. The Ascension to the Lote Tree and the prayer brought back from the boundary of creation. The words of the Lord who descends in the dark to ask who will turn to Him and whose mercy prevails over His wrath. The majesty of the Throne and the veil of light, the angels and the jinn and the written decree. The Hour whose time is hidden and whose signs were named, the Trumpet and the rising of the dead and the gathering of all the ages upon one ground. The intercession at the Praised Station where the Reckoning begins by his asking, and the supplication he withheld through all his life to spend upon his community's sinners. The Basin of his mercy and the Bridge of the Reckoning, the Scales and the books of deeds. And at the summit, past the rivers and the dwellings and the removal of every grief, the Vision of the Face, the something more. The Prophet ﷺ who opened this horizon is himself woven through it, the one carried to the Lote Tree, the one who will stand at the Praised Station, the one at whose Basin the believers will drink, the last of the prophets whose stations he passed in his ascent through the heavens. And it is to those prophets, the messengers before him whom he met in the heavens and whose testimony he carried, that the final Book now turns.

# VIII · THE CHAIN OF PROPHETS

*He did not claim to bring a new religion. He claimed to seal an old one. The recitation he carried placed him at the end of a single line of messengers stretching back to the first man, all of them sent by the one God with the one message, all of them brothers in a brotherhood of prophecy, and his own coming the completion of the house they had been building across the ages. He met them in the heavens on the night of his Ascension, and he spoke of them with a familiarity and a love that the previous Books have touched, and he taught his community to believe in every one of them and to make no division between them. This final Book follows his testimony along that chain, from Adam the first to Jesus the last before him, and arrives at the place the whole work has been moving toward, the Seal of the Prophets, the final brick in the wall, the man foretold by Abraham's prayer and Jesus's glad tidings whose coming completed the favor of God upon mankind.*

## The One Message

The deepest claim of the recitation about prophecy is that there has only ever been one of it. Every messenger God sent, from the first to the last, brought the same message at its core, the worship of the one God and the rejection of all besides Him, and the recitation states this as the unvarying content of all revelation. *And We sent not before you any messenger except that We revealed to him that there is no deity except Me, so worship Me* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:25). To every nation a warner was raised with that single summons, *and We certainly sent into every nation a messenger, saying, worship God and shun false gods* (al-Naḥl 16:36). The forms of the law differed from prophet to prophet, the rites and the rulings shaped to each people and each age, but the creed beneath them was one, and the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ expressed the relation of the prophets to one another in an image the sound books preserve, that the prophets are brothers of one father by different mothers, their religion one and their mothers, the differing laws, many. They were a single family carrying a single faith, and so the recitation made belief in all of them a condition of belief in any, the believers professing that they make no distinction between the messengers of God. *The Messenger believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All of them believe in God and His angels and His books and His messengers, saying we make no distinction between any of His messengers* (al-Baqarah 2:285). To honor Muhammad ﷺ was to honor Moses (AS) and Jesus (AS) and Abraham (AS) and all of them, and to reject any one of the messengers was to break the chain that ran through all of them. The Prophet ﷺ did not come to replace the prophets before him but to seal and confirm them, and his community was bound to love them as he loved them.

That the message was always one, beneath all the differing laws, is the surface of a deeper unity the recitation never tires of pressing. The one God is not one among many, nor the greatest of a company, but the One in the sense the chapter of sincerity gives Him. *Say, He is God, One. God, the Eternal Refuge. He neither begets nor is born, and there is none comparable to Him* (al-Ikhlāṣ 112:1-4), the Source on whom all things hang and to whom all things return, beside whom there is no second power and no rival will. The whole of creation is the single work of that single hand, ordered without seam or flaw, and the recitation dares the eye to find a crack in it. *Who created seven heavens in layers. You do not see in the creation of the Most Merciful any inconsistency. So return your gaze. Do you see any rifts. Then return your gaze twice again. Your sight will come back to you humbled and exhausted* (al-Mulk 67:3-4). Because the Maker is one and His work is one, the worship He calls for is the gathering of the whole self toward Him and away from all besides, and the sin the prophets were every one of them sent to break, the setting up of partners with God, is at root not a mistaken opinion but a misdirection of the heart, the turning of a face made for the One toward the many. To worship is to orient, and to worship rightly is to orient toward the highest, and there is nothing higher than the One from whom height itself descends. The prophets differed in their centuries and their tongues and their laws, but they stood, all of them, pointing one way, calling a scattered mankind to face again the single Source it had turned from, and the Seal of them gathered the whole long summons into the word that opens the faith, that there is no god but God.

## Adam, the First

The chain began with Adam, the first man and the first prophet, whom God created with His own hand and into whom He breathed of His spirit, and whom He taught the names of all things, so that Adam knew what the angels did not. *And He taught Adam the names, all of them, then He showed them to the angels and said, inform Me of the names of these, if you are truthful. They said, glory be to You, we have no knowledge except what You have taught us. You are the Knowing, the Wise. He said, O Adam, inform them of their names* (al-Baqarah 2:31-33). God commanded the angels to prostrate before Adam in honor of the knowledge placed in him, and they prostrated, all but Iblīs, who refused out of pride, claiming superiority as a being of fire over a being of clay, and so fell from grace and became the enemy of Adam's children (al-Aʿrāf 7:11-13). Adam dwelt in the Garden and was tried with the single tree, and he and his wife, deceived by the enemy, ate of it and were sent down to the earth, but Adam did not fall as Iblīs fell, for he turned back to his Lord in immediate repentance, and the recitation gives the prayer of his returning, the prayer of all who sin and turn back. *They said, our Lord, we have wronged ourselves, and if You do not forgive us and have mercy upon us, we will surely be among the losers* (al-Aʿrāf 7:23), and God received his repentance, *then Adam received from his Lord words, and He accepted his repentance, for He is the Accepting of repentance, the Merciful* (al-Baqarah 2:37). The first prophet was also the first penitent, and the pattern of the human story was set in him, the fall and the turning back and the mercy that receives it. The Prophet ﷺ met Adam in the nearest heaven on the night of his Ascension, the father of mankind, who looked to his right and smiled at the souls of the blessed among his descendants and looked to his left and wept at the souls of the wretched. And the Prophet ﷺ spoke of Adam's creation in a report the sound books carry and that the tradition has received as it stands, that God created Adam in His form, and tall, sixty cubits in height, the first of a creation made noble and then humbled and then offered the way back.

That offered way back is the deepest dignity the recitation grants the children of Adam, and it is the very thing that set Adam apart from the one who would not bow. The difference between them was not that the one sinned and the other did not. It was that when Adam was shown his wrong he owned it and turned, while the refuser, shown his, defended it and hardened. The prayer of Adam's returning, set down just above, is the prayer of every soul that comes after him, and the turning it carries is the thing. And the recitation throws that door open to every soul that carries Adam's pattern, calling even those who have wronged themselves past all counting not to despair. *Say, O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of God. Indeed God forgives all sins. Indeed it is He who is the Forgiving, the Merciful* (al-Zumar 39:53). And the Prophet ﷺ taught that the door of return stays open while life is in a man, that God stretches out His hand by night to receive the one who sinned by day and stretches it out by day to receive the one who sinned by night, until the sun rises from its setting place. To be a child of Adam is to be the creature who can fall and rise again, and the whole recitation is, in one of its faces, the long calling of that creature home through the door its father was the first to walk, the door that no sin shuts while the heart still beats.

## Noah (AS), Abraham (AS), and the Fathers

After Adam the messengers came in succession, and the recitation tells of Noah (AS), the prophet of the long mission, who called his people to the one God for centuries and was met with mockery and refusal, until God commanded the building of the ark and sent the flood upon the deniers and saved Noah (AS) and those who believed, the recitation naming him a grateful servant, *O descendants of those We carried with Noah. Indeed he was a grateful servant* (al-Isrāʾ 17:3). Noah (AS) was the first of the messengers the tradition calls the people of firm resolve, the ulū al-ʿazm, the greatest of the prophets who bore the heaviest trials, Noah (AS) and Abraham (AS) and Moses (AS) and Jesus (AS) and Muhammad ﷺ, the five upon whom the chain most strongly rests.

Greatest among the fathers was Abraham (AS), whom God took as an intimate friend, the only one the recitation honors with that name, *and God took Abraham as a friend* (al-Nisāʾ 4:125). Abraham (AS) is woven through the whole of this work, for it was his House at Mecca that the first Book described and his prayer at its founding that asked God to raise a messenger from his progeny, the prayer the Prophet ﷺ named as his own origin. Abraham (AS) broke the idols of his people with his own hands and was cast into the fire for it, and God commanded the fire to be cool and safe for him, *We said, O fire, be coolness and safety upon Abraham* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:69), so that the flame that was meant to consume him did not harm a hair of him. He was tried with the command to sacrifice his son, and he submitted, and his son submitted with him, and at the moment of the knife God ransomed the boy with a great sacrifice, the trial that the recitation records in the chapter of the Ranged Ones and that the pilgrimage of the believers commemorates to this day. *And when he reached with him the age of striving, he said, O my son, I have seen in a dream that I am sacrificing you, so see what you think. He said, O my father, do as you are commanded. You will find me, if God wills, of the patient* (al-Ṣāffāt 37:102). He built the House with his son Ishmael (AS) and raised its foundations praying for its acceptance, and he was the pattern of the pure monotheist, the ḥanīf, neither Jew nor Christian, whose religion the recitation commanded the Prophet ﷺ himself to follow. The Prophet ﷺ met Abraham (AS) at the seventh heaven, seated against the Frequented House at the summit of creation, and said that of all mankind he himself most resembled Abraham (AS), the friend of God whose prayer had asked for him across the gulf of two thousand years.

## Moses (AS), the Speaker

Of all the prophets before him, none is mentioned in the recitation as often as Moses (AS), the prophet to whom God spoke directly, without intermediary, the distinction the recitation marks with a special verb. *And God spoke to Moses directly* (al-Nisāʾ 4:164). Moses (AS) was raised in the very house of the tyrant Pharaoh who had ordered the slaughter of his people's sons, and God called him from a fire in the desert night, the burning bush, and spoke to him and gave him the staff that became a serpent and the hand that shone white, and sent him to confront Pharaoh who claimed to be a god, the recitation telling the confrontation across many chapters in greater detail than any other prophet's story. *Has the story of Moses reached you, when his Lord called to him in the sacred valley of Ṭuwā* (al-Nāziʿāt 79:15-16). When Pharaoh pursued the fleeing Israelites to the sea, God parted it for Moses (AS) and his people and drowned Pharaoh and his hosts in it, the great deliverance the recitation recounts again and again as the type of God's rescue of the oppressed. Moses (AS) received the Torah, the scripture of guidance and light, and went apart to his Lord for forty nights, and it was Moses (AS) who, longing to see the One who spoke to him, asked the request that the Ascension of the Prophet ﷺ would answer differently. *And when Moses came at Our appointed time and his Lord spoke to him, he said, my Lord, show me Yourself, that I may look at You. He said, you will not see Me* (al-Aʿrāf 7:143), and the mountain crumbled at the unveiling and Moses (AS) fell senseless. The vision Moses (AS) sought on the mountain and was not granted in this world, the Prophet ﷺ was carried toward at the Lote Tree, and the believers are promised in the Garden. The Prophet ﷺ met Moses (AS) in the heavens on the night of his Ascension and found him weeping, and when he asked why, Moses (AS) said it was because a young man sent after him would bring more of his community into the Garden than Moses (AS) would of his own, the prophet of the Exodus weeping not from envy but from love of the salvation of souls. And it was Moses (AS) who, in his concern for the Prophet's community, sent him back again and again to ask his Lord to lighten the burden of the fifty prayers, until they were five, so that the daily prayer of every believer carries within it the compassion of Moses (AS) for a people not his own.

## David (AS), Solomon (AS), and the Kings

The chain ran on through prophets who were also kings and prophets who were tried in their bodies and their fortunes, and the Prophet ﷺ honored them all. David (AS), to whom God gave the Psalms, the Zabūr, and whom He made a mighty king, and for whom the mountains and the birds were made to echo the praise of God, *and We subjected the mountains to glorify with David, and the birds, and We were doing so* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:79), and whose worship the Prophet ﷺ loved above all others, saying that the most beloved fasting to God was the fasting of David (AS), who fasted one day and broke his fast the next, and the most beloved prayer the prayer of David (AS), who slept half the night and prayed a third and slept a sixth. Solomon (AS), the son of David (AS), to whom God gave a kingdom such as none after him would have, the wind subjected to his command and the jinn to his labor and the speech of the birds and the ants made known to him, the recitation telling of the ant that warned its colony of Solomon's (AS) approaching host and of Solomon (AS) who smiled at her speech, and of the queen of Sheba, the Saba of the first Book of this work, whom Solomon (AS) called to the worship of the one God. Job (AS), Ayyūb, the prophet of patience, tried with the loss of his wealth and his children and his health and never ceasing to praise his Lord, the recitation naming him an excellent servant, ever returning to God (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:83-84). Jonah (AS), Yūnus, who fled his mission in anger and was swallowed by the great fish and called upon his Lord from the threefold darkness of the night and the sea and the belly of the fish, *there is no deity except You, glory be to You, indeed I have been of the wrongdoers* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21:87), and was answered and delivered, the Prophet ﷺ teaching his humility toward the prophets by saying that no one should claim him better than Jonah (AS) son of Matta. And Joseph (AS), Yūsuf, whose story the recitation calls the most beautiful of stories, thrown into the well by his brothers and sold into slavery and raised by God to power in Egypt and tried with temptation and prison and brought at last to forgive the brothers who had wronged him, the very words of his forgiveness the words the Prophet ﷺ would speak over conquered Mecca, no blame upon you this day.

## Jesus (AS), Son of Mary

Among all the prophets before him the recitation gives the fullest and most tender treatment to Jesus (AS), the son of Mary, and his place in the chain must be set out with the care the first Book's account of the Christological wars demands, for the recitation honors Jesus (AS) supremely while refusing him the divinity that the empires around Arabia fought over. He was born of a virgin by the direct creative word of God, the recitation telling of Mary withdrawing in her purity and the angel announcing to her a son though no man had touched her, and of the infant Jesus (AS) speaking from the cradle in defense of his mother, declaring himself the servant of God given the scripture and made a prophet (Maryam 19:30). The recitation names him in terms it gives no other prophet, the Messiah, the Word of God which He bestowed upon Mary, and a spirit from Him, *the Messiah, Jesus son of Mary, was a messenger of God and His word which He conveyed to Mary and a spirit from Him* (al-Nisāʾ 4:171). God gave him miracles by His permission such as no other prophet was given, that he healed the blind and the leper and gave life to the dead and fashioned from clay the form of a bird and breathed into it so that it became a living bird, all by the permission of God, the recitation careful at every miracle to add that it was by God's leave, lest the gift be mistaken for divinity. *And he will be a messenger to the Children of Israel, saying I have come to you with a sign from your Lord, that I design for you from clay the form of a bird, then I breathe into it and it becomes a bird by the permission of God, and I heal the blind and the leper and give life to the dead by the permission of God* (Āl ʿImrān 3:49). He gathered his disciples, the ḥawāriyyūn, the helpers in the cause of God.

On the central matters the empires had divided over, the recitation is clear. Jesus (AS) was not God, nor the son of God, but the servant and messenger and Word and spirit of God, who himself worshipped the One who sent him. And to the wonder upon which the world had built a godhead, the birth from no father, the recitation gave the answer that dissolved the false inference without diminishing the miracle, setting Jesus (AS) beside the first man as twin signs of a single power. For if a birth without a father proved divinity, then Adam, made with neither father nor mother, would carry the greater claim, and the recitation laid the two side by side and named the principle behind both. *Indeed the likeness of Jesus before God is as the likeness of Adam. He created him from dust, then said to him, Be, and he was* (Āl ʿImrān 3:59). The miracle was real and the inference was false, for God creates by His word alone, needing no father and no means, and when He decrees a thing He has only to say to it Be, and it is. Jesus (AS) was called the Word of God not because he was God but because he was brought to be by the word God speaks over everything that comes into being, the same word that raised Adam from dust and that will raise the dead, and to make of the spoken miracle a second god was to mistake the gift for the Giver, which the recitation refused at every turn. And he was not killed, the recitation declaring that the crucifixion the world believed in did not happen as the world believed, but that it was made to appear so to them, and that God raised Jesus (AS) to Himself. *And they did not kill him, nor did they crucify him, but it was made to appear so to them. And those who differ over it are in doubt about it. They have no knowledge of it except the following of assumption, and they did not kill him for certain. Rather, God raised him to Himself, and God is ever Mighty, Wise* (al-Nisāʾ 4:157-158). Jesus (AS) was raised, not slain, and he will return at the end of time, as the seventh Book recounted, to break the cross and slay the deceiver and rule by the truth. And it was Jesus (AS), the recitation insists, who gave the clearest of all the prophetic foretellings of the one who would come after, naming him by name, *and giving glad tidings of a messenger to come after me whose name is Aḥmad* (al-Ṣaff 61:6). The Prophet ﷺ spoke of his own nearness to Jesus (AS) in a way he spoke of no other prophet, saying in the sound collections of Bukhārī and Muslim that he was the nearest of all people to Jesus (AS) son of Mary in this world and the next, that the prophets were brothers of one father by different mothers and there had been no prophet between Jesus (AS) and himself. The chain that had run from Adam through all the messengers reached, in Jesus (AS), the prophet who pointed directly at its end, the brother who announced the coming of the Seal.

## The Seal

And the Seal came. The recitation that had named Muhammad ﷺ the last of the prophets gave him a title that closed the chain forever, the Seal of the Prophets, the one after whom no prophet would come, the completion of the line that began with Adam. *Muhammad is not the father of any of your men, but he is the Messenger of God and the Seal of the Prophets, and God is ever, of all things, Knowing* (al-Aḥzāb 33:40). Where the prophets before him had each been sent to a single people for a single age, he was sent to the whole of mankind for the whole of the time remaining, the universality that no prophet before him had claimed, the recitation commanding him to proclaim it. *Say, O mankind, indeed I am the Messenger of God to you all* (al-Aʿrāf 7:158), and again, *and We have not sent you except comprehensively to mankind, as a bringer of good tidings and a warner, but most people do not know* (Sabaʾ 34:28). His mission was not to a tribe or a nation but to the worlds, the mercy to the worlds the recitation had named, the final messenger of the one God to all who would come after.

The recitation discloses that the coming of this final Prophet ﷺ had been written into the chain from the beginning, that God had taken a covenant from the prophets themselves, every one of them, that when the final Messenger came confirming what they had brought, they would believe in him and support him. *And when God took the covenant of the prophets, saying, whatever I give you of Scripture and wisdom, and then a messenger comes to you confirming what is with you, you must believe in him and support him. He said, do you affirm and take upon that My covenant. They said, we affirm it. He said, then bear witness, and I am with you among the witnesses* (Āl ʿImrān 3:81). The whole chain of prophets, by this covenant, pointed forward to the Seal, every one of them pledged in advance to the one who would complete them, so that Abraham's (AS) prayer for a messenger from his progeny and Jesus's (AS) glad tidings of Aḥmad were not isolated foretellings but the keeping of a covenant the prophets had all sworn. And the Prophet ﷺ gave the relation of himself to the prophets before him its final image, the parable of the brick that the fourth Book of this work recorded, the beautiful house built by all the prophets and finished but for the place of a single brick, and he himself that brick, set in its place, the Seal of the Prophets, the house of prophecy complete.

## The Final Brick

The whole of this work has been the laying of that brick, and at its end the convergence the first Book described comes fully into view. The world into which he was born had been prepared across a century, the empires bled white, the cities thinned by plague and cold, the southern kingdom that named the Merciful and burned its martyrs, the sanctuary defended against the elephant, the seekers pushing up through the pagan crust toward a dawn they could feel but not see. And into that seam between the exhausted giants came the child who was the supplication of Abraham (AS), the glad tidings of Jesus (AS), and the light his mother saw in the dark, the last brick of a house the prophets had been building since Adam. He lived an orphan and rose to be the Trustworthy. He received the Word in the cave and was driven from his city for it and returned to it as its master and pardoned it. He bound the tribes into one community by a creed and a covenant, faced the armies of his people and outlasted them, and cleansed the House of Abraham (AS) of its three hundred and sixty idols and returned it to the One for whom it was raised. He delivered to his people a law of justice that bound the strong and freed the weak, and he opened to them a horizon beyond the seen, the Ascension and the intercession and the Vision of the Face. He died an ordinary human death, his head against his wife, choosing the highest companionship, and the recitation he carried declared on the plain of ʿArafāt before a hundred thousand that the religion was complete and the favor of God upon mankind was full. *This day I have perfected for you your religion, and completed My favor upon you, and approved for you Islam as religion* (al-Māʾidah 5:3).

He was the Seal, the last of the brothers, the prophet whom all the prophets had been pledged to and pointed toward, the brick that finished the house. The chain that began with the first man and ran through Noah (AS) and Abraham (AS) and Moses (AS) and David (AS) and Solomon (AS) and Jonah (AS) and Joseph (AS) and Jesus (AS) and all the messengers between, the single message of the one God carried across the ages by a brotherhood of prophecy, reached its completion in him and was sealed, and after him the house stood finished, wanting nothing, the favor complete and the religion perfected, the mercy to the worlds delivered and the final brick in its place. He had been the supplication of Abraham (AS) and the glad tidings of Jesus (AS), and he became the answer to every prophet and the seal of them all.

The portrait is drawn, the law is set out, the horizon is opened, and the chain is sealed. There remains only to set down, for the reader who would weigh the sources, a brief account of how this life is known and how its reports are graded, and to lay out the calendar of its events in order. To that apparatus the work now turns, the scholarship that stands beneath the reverence, so that what has been told may be both believed and examined. In the Name of the One God, the Lord of the worlds, and peace upon His Messenger, the Seal of the Prophets, Muhammad ﷺ, and upon all the prophets who came before him, and praise be to God, the Lord of the worlds.

# A CHRONOLOGICAL SYNOPSIS

The dates of the early life, before the Hijra, are traditional and approximate, and the reader should hold them with appropriate openness, particularly around the Year of the Elephant. The dates from the Hijra forward are firm. Years are given in the common era, with the year of the Hijra, the AH, added from the migration onward.

The long wars of Rome and Persia, intermittent across the sixth century, reaching their climax between 602 and 628. The Justinianic plague erupting in 541 and recurring in waves thereafter. The dust-veil and the failed summers beginning in 536, opening the Late Antique cold. The massacre of the Christians of Najrān, around 523, the event behind the Companions of the Trench. The Aksumite conquest of Ḥimyar, around 525, and the rise of Abraha thereafter. Abraha's dam inscription around 548 and his northern campaign recorded around 552.

The Year of the Elephant and the birth of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, by tradition around 570. The death of his mother Āminah when he was about six. The death of his grandfather ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib when he was about eight, and the guardianship of his uncle Abū Ṭālib. His marriage to Khadījah, when he was about twenty-five. The rebuilding of the Kaʿba and the arbitration of the Black Stone, when he was about thirty-five.

The first revelation at the cave of Ḥirāʾ, around 610, when he was about forty. The years of the secret call, then the public call from the hill of Ṣafā, around 613. The Persian sack of Jerusalem in 614, the low point of Rome from which the chapter of the Romans foretold its return. The first migration to Abyssinia, around 615. The boycott of the clan of Hāshim in the valley quarter, roughly 616 to 619. The Year of Sorrow, the deaths of Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib, around 619, and the rejection at Ṭāʾif. The Night Journey and the Ascension, in the same season of sorrow by the strong reckoning. The pledges at ʿAqabah, around 620 to 622, and the spread of the faith in Yathrib.

The Hijra to Medina, in 622, the first year of the Islamic calendar, and the writing of the Constitution and the building of the brotherhood. The battle of Nineveh, around 627, at which Heraclius broke Persia and fulfilled the prophecy of the Romans' return. The change of the qibla and the ordaining of the fast of Ramadan, in 2 AH, 624, and in the same year the battle of Badr. The battle of Uḥud, in 3 AH, 625. The battle of the Trench and the reckoning with Banū Qurayẓah, in 5 AH, 627. The treaty of Ḥudaybiyah and the letters to the kings, in 6 AH, 628, and the reduction of Khaybar in 7 AH. The opening of Mecca and the cleansing of the House, in 8 AH, 630, and immediately after it the battle of Ḥunayn and the siege of Ṭāʾif.

The expedition to Tabūk, in 9 AH, 630, and the Year of Delegations in which the tribes of Arabia entered the faith, and the delegation of Najrān and the proposed mutual imprecation, and the pilgrimage led by Abū Bakr at which the House was closed to idolatry. The Farewell Pilgrimage, in 10 AH, 632, and the sermon on the plain of ʿArafāt and the verse of the perfection of the religion. The death of the Prophet ﷺ in the chamber of ʿĀʾishah, in the month of Rabīʿ al-Awwal of 11 AH, 632, at the age of sixty-three, and the gathering at the Saqīfah and the succession of Abū Bakr, and the wars of the apostasy that followed and the first gathering of the Quran into one collected text.

The conquests that closed the frame. The breaking of the Roman army at the Yarmūk and the breaking of the Persian army at al-Qādisiyyah, both in the year 636. The surrender of Jerusalem to the believers in 638. The final breaking of Sasanian Persia at Nihāwand, around 642, and the passing of the empire of Khosrow out of history, and the bracelets of its king placed upon the arm of Surāqah, as the Prophet ﷺ had promised on the road of the Hijra. Within a single generation of the death, the people of the seam between the two exhausted giants had inherited the world the giants had emptied, and the long convergence with which this history opened had reached its end.

# A NOTE ON THE SOURCES AND THEIR GRADING

This life has been told reverently, but it has not been told credulously, and a reader is owed a plain account of how it is known and how its reports have been weighed, for the strength of a biography rests on the honesty with which it grades its own evidence. The material for the life of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ does not arrive at one level of certainty. It arrives in tiers, and this work has tried throughout to signal which tier a given statement stands upon, so that the reader may both believe what is secure and examine what is held more loosely.

The highest tier is the Quran itself. It is, for the believer and as a matter of textual transmission, the verbatim record of the revelation, carried from the first by mass parallel transmission, memorized entire by many in every generation, gathered into one secured text within years of the Prophet's death, and standing today as it stood then. Where this work renders a verse, it does so in the present author's own faithful English rather than reproducing at length any single existing translation, both to respect the rights of the translators whose work fills the libraries and to let the meaning be carried plainly. A rendering is an approximation and not the Arabic, and a reader who would weigh a verse closely should return to the original. The Quran is the firmest ground in the whole account, and where it speaks to an event, its word has been treated as the load-bearing testimony.

Beneath the Quran stands the Hadith, the transmitted record of the Prophet's words and deeds, and the Hadith is itself tiered. The mass-transmitted reports, the mutawātir, carried by so many independent chains that collusion in error is not credible, approach the certainty of the Quran. Below them are the sound reports, the ṣaḥīḥ, authenticated by the rigorous science the early scholars built for examining the chains of transmission, the reports gathered above all in the collections of Bukhārī and Muslim and the other accepted books, and these this work has cited by their collections and treated as strong evidence. Below the sound are the good reports, the ḥasan, weaker in their chains but acceptable, and below those the weak, the ḍaʿīf, which carry little weight and have been used here, where at all, only with their weakness marked. Where this work paraphrases the meaning of a hadith rather than quoting it at length, the same principle governs as with the Quran, the avoidance of reproducing extended copyrighted translations and the carrying of the sense in plain words, with the collection named so the reader may find the original and weigh its chain.

Beneath the Hadith stands the biographical tradition proper, the sīra and the maghāzī, the narrative life assembled by the early biographers, Ibn Isḥāq in the recension of Ibn Hishām, and al-Wāqidī, and the great history of al-Ṭabarī, and the others. This material is the source of much of the connected story, the scenes and the sequences and the human detail that the Hadith collections, organized by legal and devotional topic, do not supply. But it is a lower tier of certainty, for the biographers gathered their reports with a looser standard than the hadith critics applied, and their chains are often weaker or incomplete, and their narratives sometimes carry the shaping and the embellishment of the storyteller's art. This work has drawn on the sīra freely, for without it there is no connected life, but it has tried to mark sīra-grade material as such, with phrases noting that the biographers transmit, or that the report carries the weight of its genre, so that the reader does not mistake a vivid biographical scene for a verse or a sound report. The episode of the monk Baḥīrā, the detailed scene of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib before Abraha, the spider and the doves at the mouth of the cave, the precise figures in several of the battles, these and their like are sīra-grade, valuable and beloved and lower in certainty than the Quran and the sound Hadith, and they have been held at that weight.

Standing apart from all of these, neither confirming the tradition from within nor bound by it, is the external evidence, the inscriptions and the archaeology and the contemporary records of the surrounding civilizations. The Ḥimyarite inscriptions that name the Merciful two centuries before the Hijra, the inscriptions of Abraha at the Marib dam and at Murayghan, the Syriac and Greek records of the Najrān massacre, the chronicles of the Roman and Persian wars, these are evidence of a different kind, and this work has used them both to corroborate the tradition where they support it and to complicate it honestly where they strain against it. The clearest instance of the strain is the chronology of Abraha, whose datable activity in the late 540s and early 550s sits some two decades before the traditional date of the Year of the Elephant and the Prophet's birth, a tension this work laid plainly on the table in its first Book rather than hiding it, holding the secure core, the calamity of the Elephant and the divine defense of the House affirmed by the Quran and the sound report, distinct from the looser content, the exact date and its synchronization with the birth, which remains a matter of careful estimate.

That distinction, between the secure core of an event and the details held more loosely, has been the governing method of this work throughout, applied to the contested episodes by name. The disputed report of the high cranes was set down as existing in the conversation of the sources and judged unsound in its chains, with nothing of substance resting upon it. The numbers and fine details transmitted about the reckoning with Banū Qurayẓah were typed as sīra-grade while the broad event was noted as referenced in the Quran, the severity and the context of wartime treason held together without apology and without inflation. The shared Late Antique literary horizon behind the Quranic account of Dhū al-Qarnayn was acknowledged openly, the existence of a common motif-world distinguished carefully from the metaphysical status of the revelation, which the tracing of a motif cannot adjudicate. In each case the aim was the same, to present what the sources say, to grade them honestly, and to let reverence and rigor stand together rather than forcing the one to silence the other. The life of the Prophet ﷺ does not need to be defended by the suppression of evidence, and a faith that rests on the secure ground of the Quran and the sound Hadith is not threatened by the honest acknowledgment that the biographical details vary in their certainty.

A word, last, on the manner of reading these sources, and the Quran above them all. To grade a report is to ask whether it is sound, and that asking is the exterior discipline this note has described. But the Quran asks of its reader a second labor beside the first, an interior one, that he not pass over its verses as a man passes over a load he carries without grasping what is in it, but that he ponder them and turn them and let them open. It names this labor of itself. *Will they not then ponder the Quran, or are there locks upon their hearts* (Muhammad 47:24). It calls itself *a Book We have sent down to you, blessed, that they may ponder its verses and that those of understanding may be reminded* (Ṣād 38:29). And it points to its own seamlessness as the mark of its origin, *do they not ponder the Quran. Had it been from other than God, they would have found within it much contradiction* (al-Nisāʾ 4:82). The text holds a depth that is not spent on a first reading, and it says as much when it names its Author the First and the Last, the Outer and the Inner (al-Ḥadīd 57:3), the One who is at once the surface of every meaning and the ground beneath it. This work has read the life by the grading of its reports, which is the exterior discipline. Where the matter allowed, it has tried also to read for the depth the Quran asks of its reader, holding the two together, the soundness of the chain and the pondering of the word, so that what is told may be at once examined and entered.

It is for this reason that the work does not close upon the transmitted record. The eight Books and this note have drawn the life from the soundest reports the tradition preserves, graded as honestly as the evidence allows, and the synopsis above orders what those reports establish. Yet every tier beneath the Quran reaches the reader through chains of human memory, strong but not infallible in the sound and the mass-transmitted alike, and weaker still in the biographical record beneath them. One source alone is wholly immune, secured from the first against the slip of transmission by the parallel memorization of multitudes, and standing today as it stood then. The final volume turns to that source by itself. Setting the transmitted report aside, it draws the portrait of the Prophet ﷺ from the Quran alone, by the plain sense of its words and the triliteral roots that carry them and its own usage as the interpreter of itself, and lets the one text that cannot err speak the man. What the graded sources reconstruct from without, the Quran utters from within, and it is fitting that a life woven throughout with the recitation should close in the recitation's own unaided voice.


# IX · SERVANT AND SEAL

*The Quranic portraiture of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, between exaltation and accountability. The volumes before this one drew the life from the soundest transmitted record, the strongest the historian holds, yet no chain of men is wholly immune from the errors of its transmission. One source alone is. This final volume sets the transmitted report aside and draws the portrait of the Prophet ﷺ from the Quran itself, read by its own words and its own usage, and lets the one text that cannot err speak the man. A study in Quranic prophetology.*

## Abstract

The Quran's address to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ moves across a graded register, from the exaltation of his office to the insistence on his created humanity. The same sūra that raises him to a cosmic station will often return him, within a few verses, to the condition of a mortal under judgment. At the summit he is the mercy to the worlds, the seal of prophecy, the one upon whom God and the angels invoke blessing, and the bearer of names that are elsewhere the names of God. At the same time he is told that he is only a human being, that he holds no knowledge of the unseen, that he commands no power over outcomes, and he is reproved for particular acts and commanded to seek forgiveness. This study treats the two reaches as a single motif and argues that the portrait is internally coherent. Two features unify it. The first is that the Quran locates the source of the Prophet's ﷺ authority outside himself at every turn, so that his greatness is a greatness of reception and conveyance rather than of autonomous origin. The point reaches its limit where his own act is named the act of God, the throw that is God's throwing and the hand pledged to him that is the hand of God. The second is that the reproaches and the commands to seek forgiveness do not compete with his rank but constitute it, on the model of the Quran's own Adam, honored above the angels not for impeccability but for the capacity to recognize error and to turn back. A further register sets his endowment beside that of the earlier prophets. Where Moses (AS) was given the staff and the parted sea, and Jesus (AS) the healing breath and the titles of Word and Spirit, the sign of the Prophet ﷺ is the recited Book, and he is presented as the one they foretold and as the seal, in the root sense of the certifying and completing mark, set upon their line. The paper proceeds in three movements, each theme set in a three-part table of elevation, neutral, and warning. Nine registers draw the portrait. Four read it contested, the charge that he fabricated the revelation, the demand that he produce spectacle, and the refusal of the People of the Book who had awaited him, each answered from the same text and traced to one root, the envy that resents a gift given to another. Seven read the servant under trial, the slander and the law awaited and the bounded knowledge and the frown and the firmness and the unseen audience and the hidden enemy and his own vulnerability, and in each of them the resolution descends from God and not from him. Throughout it reads the principal terms at the level of their triliteral roots, illuminated where a sense is not plain by the same root's use elsewhere, taking the Quran as its governing source and interpreting it by its own usage. It concludes that veneration and accountability in the Quranic portrait of the Prophet ﷺ are two faces of one theology of the servant-prophet. Read to its end, that same servanthood turns the portrait outward. The seal upon the prophets, taken at its root as the mark that ratifies and completes rather than the mark that merely ends, closes the office of revelation while leaving open the road of the servant the prophets exemplify and the trials make concrete, the road the Prophet ﷺ himself walked by turning to God in every crisis. Its very protection, the refuge of the closing sūras, is a word given to every believer alike, and the study closes by reading that road as the text leaves it, a path held open to whoever will walk it and an invitation rather than a wall.

## 1. Introduction

The Quran does not address the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in a single voice. Across the text he is named, described, and spoken to along a spectrum that runs from the exaltation of his office to the affirmation of his created humanity. At one pole stand the titles that gather the highest honor. He is the mercy sent to all the worlds, the seal who closes the line of prophecy, a lamp that gives light, the one upon whom God and the angels themselves invoke blessing, and he is named with words that the Quran elsewhere reserves for its Lord. At the other pole stand the verses that strip every such elevation back to its created ground. He is told to announce that he is only a human being, that he holds no knowledge of the unseen, that he commands no power to benefit or harm even himself, that he will die as those before him died. Between these poles the Quran also speaks of him in a plain functional register, as a warner and a bringer of good news, a man among the people to whom he is sent.

The upper reach of this spectrum became, across the centuries, the material of devotion, elaborated in praise. The lower reach is no less present in the text. The emphasis on the Prophet's ﷺ humanity, his bashariyya, recurs throughout, and the verses that reprove him are direct and numerous enough that the tradition gathered them under a name, the reproaches of the Prophet, ʿitāb al-nabī. The two reaches are usually read apart, the one as honor and the other as a difficulty to be managed. This study reads them together, as one motif, and it reads the Quran by the Quran. The text supplies its own frame for holding exaltation and accountability in a single hand, and that frame is followed here rather than any imported one.

The thesis is that the portrait coheres, and that it coheres through two features of the text. The first is that the Quran locates the source of the Prophet's ﷺ authority outside himself at every turn. His very name, Muḥammad, is a passive form from the root ḥ-m-d, to praise: he is the one who is praised, not the one who praises, and the praise flows to him from a higher source that bears the same root. He is rasūl, from r-s-l, the one sent, dispatched by a sender. He is ʿabd, from ʿ-b-d, the servant, the title the Quran chooses for him at the height of his ascent. His greatness, read at the root, is everywhere a greatness of reception and conveyance, never of an autonomous origin he carries in himself. The point reaches its sharpest expression, examined in Sections 6 and 23, in the verses where his own act is named the act of God: of the throw at the battle of Badr, "you did not throw when you threw, but God threw" (8:17), and of the pledge of allegiance sworn to him, "the hand of God is over their hands" (48:10).

The second feature concerns the reproaches. The Quran's commands to the Prophet ﷺ to seek forgiveness, and its direct corrections of his conduct, do not sit in tension with his standing. They instantiate it. The controlling paradigm is the Quran's own account of Adam, honored above the angels not because he cannot err but because, having erred, he recognizes the error and turns back to his Lord, where the refusing party errs and justifies itself rather than returning. On this logic the Prophet's ﷺ corrigibility is not a subtraction from his rank but its mechanism, and the Quran marks the point by issuing the command to seek forgiveness at the very summit of his victory.

To this point the portrait is the one the Quran draws of him. The text also preserves the portrait as it was contested, and the second movement of the study reads that record. His hearers could not agree what he was, calling him poet and sorcerer and madman and forger. They prescribed the spectacle a messenger must bring, a visible God, a book from the sky, an angel at his side. And the People of the Book, who had awaited him and knew him, refused him when he came because the grace had fallen outside their lineage. The Quran answers each contest from the same text and then names its single root. The rejection of the prophets, the demand for wonders, the envy of the chosen, and at the last the fracturing of the one religion into sects, are traced to one cause, baghy, the envy that resents a gift given to another, and the diagnosis is turned, in the end, upon the believers themselves.

The text met him not only as drawn and as contested but as tried. The third movement reads the crises of his life: the slander against his house, the woman whose plea he could not answer, the questions that probed the limit of his knowledge, the frown he was reproved for and the firmness he was held by, the unseen audience and the staked confrontation, the hidden enemy and the law of the struggle, and his own vulnerability to harm. In every one the resolution descends from God and is not produced by him, and the trials return, under fire, the finding the portrait had drawn at rest, that his greatness is a greatness of reception, and that what he is given he does not generate.

These features, traced through the registers of all three movements and gathered in the synthesis, open of themselves onto a further question, and the study takes it up in Section 24 before the conclusion. The highest of the Prophet's ﷺ titles is khātam, the seal, and once the seal is read at its root as the mark that ratifies and completes rather than the mark that merely ends, the portrait raises of itself the question of what that seal closes and what it leaves open, and of what a portrait of the servant-prophet, so read, holds out to the one who comes to it. The portrait and the contest brought against it are set out first. The question they raise is answered last, from the same text and by the same method.

A word on method and sources. This study foregrounds the Quranic text in its plain semantic and lexical sense, reading the triliteral roots that carry the meaning directly from the Arabic, following a key root through its other occurrences where its sense in a given verse is not plain, and interpreting the Quran by its own usage. Where the text attributes a fault or issues a correction, the fault and the correction are presented as the text gives them, without recourse to the later doctrinal harmonizations that sought to soften them. The evidence is the Quran itself, cited by sūra and verse. The English wording has been checked against the standard translations listed in the References, and the renderings given here are the author's own. The historical occasions reported in the biographical literature are noted only where the text itself requires a setting, and the analysis does not depend on them.

The body falls into three movements, and every table reads its theme under the same three registers, how it appears under elevation, under a neutral or functional treatment, and under warning, accountability, and criticism. The first proceeds through nine thematic registers of the portrait as the Quran draws it. The second proceeds through four registers of the portrait contested, the charges his hearers pressed and the answers the recitation returned. The third proceeds through seven registers of the servant under trial, the crises of his life and their resolution from above. A short discussion precedes every table, and each table closes with the principal term and its root. Section 2 defines the three registers before the analysis begins.

## 2. The Three Registers of Address

The columns that organize the tables below name three registers in which the Quran speaks of the Prophet ﷺ. They are analytic categories, drawn to make the motif legible, and the text does not always observe their borders cleanly. A single term can carry more than one charge at once, as the discussion of ʿabd in Section 3 and of the throw at Badr in Section 6 will show. With that caution the three registers may be defined as follows.

The register of elevation gathers the titles and statements that raise the Prophet's ﷺ office to a cosmic or salvific dimension. It includes the great honorific names, the affirmations of his terminal and universal place, the declarations that bind obedience to him to obedience to God, and the marks of singular divine favor toward his person: the protection from his enemies, the consolation offered in his distress, the oath sworn by his life, the reorientation of the qibla to his unspoken wish, the abundance granted after his loss. These are read here as elevation because they single him out as the object of a care and a station granted to no one else, even though their grammar, examined closely, keeps the favor on the side of the Giver.

The register of neutral or functional address gathers the designations that state the Prophet's ﷺ role without exalting or diminishing it, together with the plainly descriptive statements of his humanity, the regulation of his daily and domestic life, and the ethical instruction directed to him as it would be to any believer. Here he is the warner, the bringer of good news, the reciter of what is sent down, a man from among the people. The dominant grammatical form in this register is the imperative qul, "say," which introduces his speech hundreds of times and frames his office as the conveyance of a received word.

The register of warning, accountability, and criticism gathers the verses that fix the Prophet ﷺ as a created and accountable mortal. It includes the categorical statements of his humanity, the limits set on his power and his knowledge and his agency, the exposure of his fear and his grief, the direct reproaches, the explicit attribution of fault with the commands to seek forgiveness, the conditional threats of the severest kind, the warnings of betrayal he cannot himself detect, and the deficits noted in comparison with earlier prophets. This is the register in which the Quran most plainly portrays the Prophet ﷺ in a manner that is corrective or limiting, and the present study reports those portrayals directly rather than dissolving them.

## 3. Theme One: Naming and Designation

The Quran's names for the Prophet ﷺ array themselves across all three registers, and the array is itself instructive. At the summit are the cosmic titles. He is Khātam al-Nabiyyīn, the Seal of the Prophets, the one in whom the prophetic succession is confirmed and brought to its completion (33:40), a title examined at its root in Section 10. He is raḥmatan lil-ʿālamīn, a mercy to the worlds, sent as mercy rather than merely bringing it (21:107). He is a radiant lamp, sirājan munīran (33:46). The honor rises higher still. God Himself, with the angels, invokes blessing upon him, "God and His angels send blessings upon the Prophet" (33:56), and his very renown is lifted, "and We raised high for you your remembrance" (94:4). He is named, moreover, with words the Quran elsewhere reserves for its Lord, "to the believers most kind and most merciful," bil-muʾminīna raʾūfun raḥīm (9:128), where raʾūf and raḥīm are two of the divine attributes, here predicated of the Prophet ﷺ. The two proper names sit on a single root. Muḥammad and Aḥmad both derive from ḥ-m-d, to praise, the first an intensive passive participle, the one repeatedly praised, the second an elative, most worthy of praise, placed in the Quran in the mouth of Jesus (AS) as a prophecy (61:6). Read at the root, the names are receptive. They mark him as the recipient of praise, not its origin.

In the neutral register stand the functional designations that name what he does. He is al-Nabī, the Prophet, and al-Rasūl, the Messenger, the sent one carrying a message. He is bashīr wa nadhīr, a bringer of good news and a warner (2:119; 34:28). One structural feature of the address belongs here and is easily missed. Every other prophet named in the Quran is at some point called by his personal name in direct address: Adam, Noah (AS), Abraham (AS), Moses (AS), Jesus (AS), David (AS). The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is never once addressed as "O Muhammad." To his face the Quran uses only the office, "O Prophet," "O Messenger." His personal name appears only in the third person, in statements about him. The veiling of the name behind the title is itself a form of honor, and it sits quietly within the otherwise plain functional register.

In the register of warning and grounding stand the designations that return him to his created condition. The most categorical is the command to declare his humanity, "Say: I am only a human being like you, to whom it is revealed" (18:110). The differentiating clause is the revelation, not the substance of the man, who is basharun mithlukum, human like his hearers. The same register includes the verse that spends his one proper name on a reminder of his mortality and his replaceability in function, "Muhammad is no more than a messenger; messengers have passed away before him" (3:144). And it includes the title ʿabd, servant, which the text deploys at the very height of his experience, in the opening of the Night Journey, "Glory be to the One who took His servant by night" (17:1). Here the analytic borders blur, for ʿabd is at once the mark of his nearness, the servant carried to the highest place, and the deepest grounding, the one who is owned and who serves. The ascent is named by the servitude. One further root binds the registers together. The word for his humanity, bashar, and the word for his function as herald, bashīr, share the root b-sh-r. The flesh that can be addressed as mortal and the office of bringing good news are spelled from the same three consonants.

| Field | Elevation | Neutral / Functional | Warning, Accountability, Criticism |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Description | Titles raising his office to a cosmic and salvific dimension, including names elsewhere reserved for God. | Designations naming his prophetic function without exalting or diminishing it. | Names and declarations fixing him as a created, owned, and accountable mortal. |
| Context | Chiefly Medinan affirmations of his terminal and universal status, and of the honor God shows him. | Recurrent statements defining the mechanics of the mission. | Grounding statements, several set against demands to deify him. |

| Snippet | “the Messenger of God and the Seal of the Prophets” (33:40); “to the believers most kind and most merciful” (9:128); “God and His angels send blessings upon the Prophet” (33:56) | “We have sent you to all mankind only as a bringer of good news and a warner” (34:28) | “Say: I am only a human being like you, to whom it is revealed” (18:110); “Muhammad is no more than a messenger” (3:144) |
| Key term (root) | khātam (kh-t-m: to seal, certify, complete); raʾūf (r-ʾ-f: tender kindness); raḥīm (r-ḥ-m: womb, mercy) | rasūl (r-s-l: to send); nadhīr (n-dh-r: to warn) | bashar (b-sh-r: skin, flesh, humanity); ʿabd (ʿ-b-d: to serve, worship) |

## 4. Theme Two: Authority and Obedience

The elevation of the Prophet's ﷺ authority is among the strongest in the Quran. Obedience to him is identified with obedience to God, "Whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed God" (4:80). His judgment is made the test of faith itself, in an oath sworn by God on God's own name, "But no, by your Lord, they will not believe until they make you judge in what is disputed between them, and then find no resistance within themselves to what you decide, and submit fully" (4:65). Once he and God have decided a matter, the believer retains no choice in it (33:36). What he gives is to be taken and what he forbids is to be left (59:7). And the claim runs deeper than command, "The Prophet has a greater claim on the believers than they have on their own selves" (33:6).

Even at this height, one feature of the grammar holds. The authority is always conjoined and always named second. The formula is "God and His Messenger," never the Messenger standing alone as the ultimate source, and the decree of 33:36 is "once God and His Messenger have decided," with God first. The binding force is delegated. This is consistent with the wider pattern of the portrait, in which the Prophet ﷺ is the channel of an authority whose origin lies beyond him.

The neutral register defines the limit of his mandate at the point of delivery. He is a reminder and no more, "So remind; you are only a reminder" (88:21). His responsibility ends at conveyance, "The Messenger's duty is only to convey" (5:99). The register of warning then caps the authority explicitly. He is not a tyrant or compeller over the people, and he is not their controller, "You are not a controller over them" (88:22). He is not their guardian or keeper. And the Quran corrects specific exercises of his judgment. It reproves a decision taken over captives of war (8:67) and notes that only a prior decree spared him a severe consequence for it (8:68). It warns him, in a matter of judgment, "do not be an advocate for the treacherous" (4:105). The authority is real and binding, and it is also bounded, delegated, and subject to correction.

| Context | Establishing his juridical authority and the duty of inward submission. | Marking the limit of his mandate at delivery. | Restraining overreach; correcting a ransom decision and a near-miscarriage of judgment. |
| Principal verses | al-Nisāʾ 4:80; al-Nisāʾ 4:65; al-Aḥzāb 33:6 | al-Ghāshiya 88:21; al-Māʾida 5:99 | al-Ghāshiya 88:22; al-Anfāl 8:67-68; al-Nisāʾ 4:105 |
