## 23. Synthesis: The Coherence of the Portrait

The registers return the same finding from three sides. The nine of the portrait return it directly. The four of the contest return it in the negative, the charge that he originated the word answered by his own disclaimer, the demand for spectacle answered by the servant who holds no marvel in his hand, the rejection of the awaited one explained by the envy that sunders every community. And the seven of the trial return it under fire, the slander answered by revelation and not by his defense, the law given and not authored, the knowledge bounded and the firmness granted, the unseen reached by the Word and the confrontation handed to God, the hidden enemy exposed from above and the very refuge from harm a word he is told to say. The Quran raises the Prophet ﷺ to the limit of created standing and, at every turn, withholds from him the one thing that would make him an independent source rather than a maximal recipient. The motif is not a contest between exaltation and grounding that the text fails to resolve. It is a single theology of the servant-prophet, and two observations make its coherence plain.

The first observation concerns the grammar of his elevation, which is receptive throughout. His names are receptive. Muḥammad is the one praised, by another, from the root ḥ-m-d that also names the praising God. His office is receptive. He is rasūl, the one sent, by a sender. His highest title is receptive in the strongest sense. He is ʿabd, servant, the word the Quran chooses at the summit of his ascent. Even the marks of favor are framed as the action of the Giver, not the possession of the favored. God protects him, God suffices him, God expands his breast and lifts his burden, all in the divine first person. The receptivity reaches its limit in the verses where his own act is named the act of God. At Badr, of the gesture that scattered the enemy, the text says "you did not throw when you threw, but God threw" (8:17). Of the pledge sworn to him, "the hand of God is over their hands" (48:10). These are at once the highest exaltation, his hand and his throw made the very vehicle of divine action, and the most complete grounding, for the act is credited not to him but to God. The same permeability runs in the other direction in the matter of the qibla. God saw his face turning to the sky in silent longing and turned the worship of the community to the direction he desired (2:144). Between the throw that is God's and the qibla that bends to his wish, the Prophet ﷺ stands as the place where the human will and the divine meet, the locus through which God acts and to whose longing God answers, never an independent power set beside Him.

The comparison with the earlier prophets confirms the same reading from another side. His distinction among them is not that a greater wonder was placed in his hand, for the staff, the healing breath, and the clay made to breathe are precisely the signs he is not given. His sign is the recited Word, and his scope and his office of confirming and sealing the line are what set him apart. Even this supremacy is receptive. He is the answer to Abraham's (AS) prayer and the one Jesus (AS) names, foretold by the line he completes. He confirms what was given to others rather than originating it, and the seal he sets, in the full sense of its root, certifies and consummates a prophecy whose author is God. To seal the prophets, on the Quran's own usage of the word, is to ratify and complete them, not to stand merely as their terminus.

The second observation concerns the corrective verses, and it is here that the portrait's deepest coherence lies. The reproaches and the commands to repent are not a weight that drags against his rank. They are the substance of it, and the Quran supplies its own paradigm for reading them. The text honors Adam above the angels not for an impeccability the angels themselves possess but for a different capacity altogether. The angels do not deviate, and for that very reason they neither fall nor rise by returning. Adam deviates, recognizes the deviation, and turns back, receiving words of return from his Lord. The refusing party deviates and justifies itself, claiming an inherent superiority, and does not return. The dignity that raises the human above the angel is thus the capacity to err, to recognize the error, and to turn. On this paradigm the Prophet's ﷺ corrigibility is not the floor beneath his station. It is the station's own mechanism. He is the one in whom this human capacity operates at its fullest, and the Quran marks the point unmistakably by issuing the command to seek forgiveness not in a moment of failure but at the summit of his victory, when the conquest is complete, "exalt your Lord with praise and seek His forgiveness" (110:3). The crown of his success is an act of turning.

Read together, the observations explain why the apparent tensions in the portrait are not tensions at all. The same root that grounds him as flesh, b-sh-r, furnishes the word for his commission as herald, bashīr. The human being who can err is the very one who is sent. The character commended as magnificent (68:4) is corrected in its particular acts (80:1; 33:37; 66:1), because the perfected human is governed and not self-governing. The favor that bends the qibla to his wish and the abundance that answers his loss single him out, yet originate wholly in the Giver. The betrayals he cannot detect and the reproaches within his household ground him as a man among men, under God's correction as under God's protection. The bearer of two of the divine names, raʾūf and raḥīm, is the same servant who is told he holds no power to benefit himself, because the names are bestowed and not owned. And the comparison with the earlier prophets resolves on the same principle. The favoring of one prophet above another is God's to assign, and the Prophet's ﷺ terminal and universal place is a place within the divine economy, not a rank he occupies apart from it.

This reading does not soften the reproaches to protect the Prophet's ﷺ dignity, and it has no need to. It locates the dignity in the very capacity the reproaches presuppose. A prophet who could not be addressed in correction would be, on the Quran's own Adamic logic, lower and not higher, nearer to the condition the refusing party claimed for itself than to the condition for which Adam was raised. The most exalted of messengers is held to the most exacting standard, warned that fabrication against God would cost him the aorta and that association would void his works, precisely because the standard rises with the station. The honor and the accountability are not opposed values held in uneasy balance. In the Quranic portrait they are forged from a single material, and the text says as much in its own grammar, when it makes the servant the one it praises, the throw of his hand the throwing of God, and the seeking of forgiveness the crown of his victory.
## 24. The Seal and the Open Path

The portrait, its contesting, the trials that proved it, and their synthesis settle the matter: a servant-prophet whose greatness is the greatness of reception, raised to the limit of created standing and withheld at every turn from being an independent source, accused of originating the word he only carried and cleared by the same text, and met in every crisis by a resolution he received and did not make. One question the themes imply but do not separately resolve remains, and it is the question the title of this study has carried throughout. What does the seal close, and what does it leave open. The word has already been read here at its root, the seal that closes and authenticates and completes, and read by the Quran's own usage rather than as a bare terminus. Read so, it falls in the text upon a defined thing. The single sealing term the Quran sets upon the Prophet ﷺ falls upon the prophets, khātam al-nabiyyīn (33:40). The text sets no sealing term upon the messengers, no seal of the mursalīn and no last of the rusul, and the confession that opens the faith names him rasūl Allāh without naming him the last of them. What the seal closes is the office of prophecy, the descent of a new revelation upon a newly commissioned man. Where the text does not pronounce, it does not pronounce. Its reticence about the office beyond that point is reticence, and not a second seal, and not a license. What the text is nowhere reticent about, and nowhere closes, is guidance.

The reaching of God's guidance to the living the Quran keeps wholly open. The community is charged to call to the good and to forbid the wrong (3:104). The one who was dead and was given a light walks by it among the people (6:122). The living are summoned to answer the One who calls them to what gives them life (8:24). These are offices of guidance, and the text names them without the word rasūl. The recited Word itself, sent down as a guidance for mankind (2:185), is carried and recited and borne from one generation to the next, and the one who bears it bears what is not his own. The Quran frames even its highest bearer in exactly this way. The Prophet ﷺ is told to say that he is only a man to whom it is revealed (18:110), that it is nothing but a revelation revealed (53:4), that upon him lies only the conveyance (5:99; 24:54). The bearer bears a message whose origin is God. The giving of a new revelation is what the seal addresses. The carrying of what has been given is what continues. The seal was set upon the source, never upon the stream.

Behind this openness stands something the Quran places before every prophet. It speaks of an original disposition upon which God shaped mankind, the fiṭra (30:30), and of a covenant taken before time, when He drew from the children of Adam the acknowledgment of their Lord, "Am I not your Lord? They said, Yes" (7:172). The pattern toward which the prophets call is thus prior to them. They are not its authors but its clearest bearers, and the text is explicit that they are set to be followed and not admired from a distance. In the Messenger ﷺ there is a beautiful example for whoever hopes in God and the Last Day (33:21), as there was a beautiful example in Abraham (AS) and those with him (60:4). An example stands at the head of a road. He does not lie across it as a wall. And because, as the synthesis has shown, the summit of this prophet is servanthood, the imitation the text invites is the imitation of a servant, a station withheld from no one. The seal closes the office of revelation. It does not close the road of resemblance, the labor of the servant to grow toward the pattern the prophets carried. To revere the Prophet ﷺ and to walk toward him are not two motions but one.

The road the prophets walked ran through trial, and the Quran does not conceal that they walked it as men. It says plainly that there was no messenger and no prophet before whom, when he recited, Satan did not cast something into his recitation, and that God then annuls what Satan casts and makes His verses firm (22:52). The suggestion was real. The purifying was God's. The servant who reads this is not taught that nearness is the absence of the whisper but that it is the whisper overcome, and that the strength to overcome is granted from above. The prophets are exemplary not because temptation never reached them but because they were carried through it, and the same help is the hope held out to the one who follows.

And the trials read in the third movement give that following its shape. In each of them the Prophet ﷺ met his crisis not by a power in himself but by a turning to God for what he could not supply. Slandered, he awaited the vindication and did not force it. Questioned past the edge of his knowledge, he confessed the limit. Tempted to bend the word, he was held firm from above. Harmed as other men are harmed, he was given a refuge to recite. To walk the road of the servant is to learn that turning, not a power to wield but a dependence to keep. And the path is shown open at its plainest in the last of those trials, for the refuge that was the Prophet's ﷺ own protection was placed in his mouth and in every believer's alike, the two closing sūras a word given to all who will say it. The same recitation that was his armor is the reader's, and there is no plainer proof that the road of the servant was left open than that its very protection is a verse anyone may recite.

Read in this way, the resistance the second movement diagnosed loses its ground. The envy that refused the prophet at every turn, the angel's pride and the clansman's and the heir's, and that sundered the one community into sects, wears two faces in the single heart. One denies the prophet outright. The other sets him so far beyond reach that to follow him is quietly excused, and an idol is raised where an example was meant to stand. Both are answered at once when the prophet is received as what he was sent to be, a servant whose nearness is, by God's design, a road and not a fence. Envy finds no footing against a door held open. It turns toward love, and the love refuses every tribal narrowing, for the text refuses it first. Of the messengers the believers say, "we make no distinction between any of them" (2:285). Of mankind God says that the most honored before Him is the most mindful of Him, and not the nearest by birth or banner (49:13). This is the healing of the sundering, the one community restored not by erasing its prophets but by walking toward the pattern they set, the same undivided line to which the People of the Book were called. The Prophet ﷺ is honored most not by walling his station but by walking toward it.

And the road is to be walked with understanding, not borne in the dark. The Quran reproaches those who were entrusted with the Book and then carried it without grasping it, likening them to a donkey that carries volumes (62:5), and it commands the contrary, asking whether they will not ponder the Quran (47:24) and naming itself a blessed Book sent down that its verses might be pondered (38:29). It names its own depth, "He is the First and the Last, the Outer and the Inner" (57:3), and the way inward is open to reverent reflection rather than reserved to a class. Belief received from others is a beginning. It was never meant to be the end of the road.

So the seal, read with the same care the rest of this study has used, gives not a smaller Prophet ﷺ but a nearer one. The office of revelation is closed and the line of the prophets sealed and ratified. The carriage of God's guidance continues under names that are not rasūl. The road of the servant stays open to whoever will walk it, the prophets foremost upon it and beckoning rather than barring. And what lies past the seen remains where the text leaves it, with God, who holds the keys of the unseen and discloses them to none but a messenger He has approved (6:59; 72:26-27). The hope this leaves the reader is the truest the portrait affords: not a distant figure to be guarded from a safe remove, but the first of the servants on a road that was never closed, and a calling, open to all, to walk it.
## 25. Conclusion

The Quran's portrait of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ holds exaltation and accountability together by design. He is named with the highest titles the text affords, the seal of the prophets and the mercy to the worlds, blessed by God and the angels and called by names that are God's own, and he is addressed, in the same scripture and often in the same passage, as a mortal who will die, who does not know the unseen, who holds no power over outcomes, who is reproved for particular acts within his own household, and who is commanded to seek forgiveness. Set beside the earlier prophets, he is given no staff and no clay made to breathe, but the recited Word, a universal scope, and the office of the seal that confirms and completes their line. Read across the registers of his portrait, the contest brought against it, and the trials that met him, from his naming to the refuge he was given to recite, the portrait does not fracture. It coheres around a single conception.

That conception has two faces, which are finally one. The Prophet ﷺ is the maximal locus of divine action and never its autonomous source. His greatness is the greatness of perfect reception and faithful conveyance, and it reaches its height where his very act is named the act of God, where his wish is answered by the turning of the qibla, and where the prophets before him point toward him as the seal that ratifies and completes their succession. And his accountability, far from competing with his rank, is the mechanism of it, on the model of the Adam whom the Quran honors above the angels for the capacity to recognize error and to turn back. The most-praised servant is the one whose every excellence points beyond himself to its Giver, and who, at the height of his triumph, turns to seek forgiveness. In the Quranic portraiture, the honor and the dependence are the same fact, named from every side the text commands. And because that greatness is a servant's greatness, the portrait closes not as a wall but as an invitation. The seal that ends the descent of revelation leaves open the road of the servant the prophets walked, sets them at its head as the example the believer is bidden to follow, and offers the reader not a figure to be admired from afar but the first upon a path that was never closed. And that the path was never closed the text seals at its plainest in the end, when the Prophet's ﷺ own refuge against the harms that reach all men was given to every believer alike as a word to recite.

## Appendix A. Note on Transliteration and Conventions

Transliteration follows a light academic scheme. Long vowels are marked ā, ī, ū. The emphatic and other distinguished consonants are marked with a dot below: ḥ, ṣ, ṭ, ḍ, ẓ. The letter ʿayn is represented by ʿ and the hamza by ʾ. Roots are given as their bare consonants separated by hyphens, for example ḥ-m-d, and are read directly from the Arabic. Where the sense of a term in a given verse is not plain, the same root is followed through its other occurrences in the text to recover the meaning, a procedure used most fully for kh-t-m in Section 10. Quranic citations give the sūra number followed by the verse number or range, as 33:40 or 80:1-10. Sūra names are given in transliteration at first mention within each table. The honorific ﷺ follows the name and titles of the Prophet Muhammad in keeping with established usage, and rests on the Quran's own statement that God and the angels invoke blessing upon him (33:56). English renderings of the verses are the author's own, prepared directly from the Arabic text and checked for phrasing against the standard translations listed in the References.
## Appendix B. Glossary of Principal Roots

The roots below carry the principal terms used across this work, the books of the life and the study of the portrait that closes it. Each entry gives first the sense the word bears in current and Quranic usage, and then, where the skeleton preserves it, the raw image buried in the three consonants, the concrete and often bodily thing the root meant before it was lifted into abstraction. The method is excavation rather than loose etymology: the one consonantal skeleton is followed across its derived words to recover the single picture beneath them, read directly from the Arabic and without the later doctrinal smoothing. The order is the Arabic alphabet.

ʾ-j-r: a wage, a reward; ajr, the recompense a messenger does not ask (26:109). Beneath it, to set and splint a broken bone (the ajr of a fracture); the wage is the mending that restores, payment as repair.

ʾ-kh-w: brotherhood, a brother; muʾākhāh, the pact of brotherhood by which each Helper of Medina took an emigrant into his house, and akh, brother. The skeleton is the sharing of one source, two who come of the same womb or the same bond; the brothering of Medina is a kinship made by covenant where blood gave none, the tie of the womb extended by the word.

ʾ-dh-n: to permit, to give leave; idhn, the divine leave by which the prophets' signs are worked, "by God's leave" (3:49). The skeleton is the ear (udhun); to give leave is to incline the ear and let a thing through, permission as a hearing granted; and the adhān, the call to prayer, is the same root turned outward, the announcement proclaimed for every ear.

ʾ-f-k: to lie, to slander; ifk, the great slander against the household (24:11). The raw sense is to turn a thing over and invert it; the muʾtafikāt are the overturned cities (53:53), and the lie is truth tipped upside down, the world stood on its head.

ʾ-m-m: to head toward, to lead, a community; ummah, the single community made of emigrant and Helper, imām, the one who leads from the front, and umm, the mother. The skeleton is the point one faces and makes for (amma, to repair to); a community is a body turned to one aim, the leader the one at its head, the mother the source it issues from.

ʾ-m-n: to be safe, to trust, to believe; amān, the safe-conduct granted to an enemy, Amīn, the trustworthy, the Prophet's ﷺ name among his people before the call, and īmān, faith. The skeleton is security itself, the settling of fear (amina, to be safe); to believe is to entrust oneself, to find in God the safety the heart stops fearing for, and the trustworthy is the one in whose keeping a thing is safe.

ʾ-w-l: priority, the first, the greater claim; awlā, "the Prophet ﷺ is closer to the believers than themselves" (33:6). The root is return, the coming back to an origin (awl); what is first is what one returns to, and the greater claim is the claim of the source.

ʾ-y-y: a sign, a mark, a token; āya, at once a miracle and a verse of scripture (2:118). The skeleton is the bare mark left for recognition, the trace by which a thing is known; wonder and verse are named alike as marks set for the eye that reads.

b-d-ʿ: to originate without precedent; bidʿ, "I am no new thing among the messengers" (46:9), and al-Badīʿ, the Originator. The raw image is the first sinking of a well never dug before, to bring forth where nothing of the kind has stood; origination is the breaking of new ground.

b-d-l: to change, to exchange; baddala, the altering of the word entrusted (2:59). The skeleton is substitution, one thing set in the place of another, the swap that leaves the slot filled but the content replaced.

b-r-ʾ: to be clear of, to absolve, to create by separating; baraʾa, "they are absolved of what they say" (24:26), and al-Bāriʾ, the Maker. The raw image is recovery from sickness (burʾ) and the cutting-free of one thing from another; to absolve is to be made clean of a thing, and to create is to part the made from the unmade.

b-sh-r: skin, flesh, the human, and to bring good news; bashar, the human being (18:110), and bashīr, the herald of glad tidings. The buried picture is the outer skin itself (basharah); good news is named from the face, the skin that brightens and shows the tidings before the mouth speaks.

b-gh-y: to seek wrongfully, to overstep, insolent envy; baghy, read in this study as the single root of rejection and schism (2:213; 42:14). Beneath it lies the wound that swells and festers past its bound (baghā l-jurḥ) and the one who sells the body (baghiyy); envy here is a swelling that breaks its own skin, a seeking that will not keep its bound.

b-l-gh: to reach, to convey, to attain; balāgh, "upon the messenger lies only the conveyance" (5:99). The skeleton is arrival at a destination and the coming of age (bulūgh); the bearer's whole task is to carry the thing the full distance and set it down intact.

b-h-l: to invoke earnestly, to imprecate; ibtihāl, the mutual cursing proposed at Najran (3:61). The raw sense is to leave unrestrained, the she-camel turned loose with her udder unbound (bahl); to imprecate is to loose the matter wholly to God, tether cut, and let His judgment fall.

b-y-n: to be clear, to make plain, to separate; bayyina, the clear proof (2:118), and mubīn, the manifest. The skeleton is the gap between two things (bayn), the distance that lets each be seen apart; to make plain is to open that space so the eye can tell one from another.

t-b-ʿ: to follow; ittabaʿa, "I follow only what is revealed to me" (46:9). The raw sense is to walk in another's tracks, to come behind in the same prints; to follow the revelation is to set one's feet where it has stepped.

t-l-w: to recite, and to follow; tilāwa, the recitation of the signs (2:252). The skeleton is one thing close on the heel of another; reciting is following the text word after word, each syllable trailing the last, so that to recite and to follow are one motion.

t-w-b: to turn back, to return; tawba, the repentance the Prophet ﷺ is commanded toward (110:3), and al-Tawwāb, the ever-relenting. The raw image is the bodily turn, the swing of the whole frame back toward what it left; repentance is a reversal of the feet, and God's relenting is His turning back toward the one who turns.

th-b-t: to be fixed, to stand firm; tathbīt, "had We not made you firm, you would have nearly inclined" (17:74). The skeleton is the rooted and the planted, a stake driven and held; firmness is not the absence of the pull but the root that does not give when pulled.

j-d-l: to dispute, to argue; jidāl, and al-Mujādila, the sūra of the woman who argued (58:1). The raw sense is the tight twisting of a rope (jadīl), strand wound hard against strand; argument is a wrestling of twisted cords, each pulling the braid its own way.

j-n-n: the hidden, the covered; jinn, the concealed beings (72:1). The root is concealment itself. The same three letters give janna, to veil in darkness; jannah, the garden whose leaves hide its floor; janīn, the embryo curtained in the womb; and majnūn, the reason screened from itself. One picture under them all: what is covered from sight.

j-h-r: to be open, loud, manifest; jahra, "show us God openly" (2:55). The skeleton is the plain and the exposed, the thing said aloud and seen in full light; to demand God jahratan is to demand Him brought out into the open eye.

ḥ-d-th: to be new, to occur, to narrate; hadith, the report of what the Prophet ﷺ said and did, the second source woven through this life. The skeleton is the coming-to-be of what was not (ḥadatha, to happen newly); a narration is the bringing of an event into speech, news of a thing that occurred, the new told against the foregoing.

ḥ-r-m: to forbid, the sacred, the inviolable; ḥaram, the sanctuary at Mecca, iḥrām, the pilgrim's consecrated state, and ḥarām, the forbidden. The skeleton is the set-apart that may not be touched (ḥaruma, to be inviolable); the sacred and the forbidden are one image, a boundary drawn around a thing that hands must not cross, the precinct and the prohibition named alike.

ḥ-s-d: to envy; ḥasad, the envier's evil sought refuge from (113:5). The raw sense is a gnawing, an itch or mange that eats inward; envy in the skeleton is a thing that consumes the one who holds it from within.

ḥ-k-m: to judge, to govern, to be wise; ḥukm, the judgment that is God's in the Torah (5:43), and ḥakīm, the wise. The raw image is the bridle (ḥakama) that curbs the horse; to judge is to rein in, and wisdom is the restraint that holds chaos to a course.

ḥ-m-d: to praise, to commend; Muḥammad, the much-praised, and Aḥmad, and al-Ḥamīd. The skeleton is praise rendered as due, not flattery but the acknowledgment owed; the Prophet's ﷺ name is its passive form, the one praise comes to, never its source.

ḥ-n-f: to incline away, the upright primordial faith; ḥanīf, the seeker of the pure original religion of Abraham (AS), turned from the idols toward the one God (3:67). The skeleton is the deliberate leaning, a foot turned off the broad road; the ḥanīf has inclined away from the worship of the many to the worship of the One, upright by a turning.

ḥ-w-r: whiteness, to return, a disciple; ḥawāriyyūn, the disciples of Jesus (AS), the devoted white-garbed helpers (3:52). The skeleton is a bright whiteness and the turning back of a thing (ḥāra, to return); the disciple is named for a purity that is also a constancy, one who whitens and returns, the close helper at the prophet's side.

kh-t-m: to seal, to stamp, to finish; khātam, set upon the Prophet ﷺ in 33:40. The Quran's own uses fix the range: a seal pressed on hearts and hearing that fixes a state (2:7), the sealing of mouths in final testimony (36:65), and the sealed wine "whose seal is musk," the certifying finish (83:25-26). The raw image is the signet pressed into clay or wax, the ring that at once closes a thing and stamps it as genuine; the seal ends, authenticates, and completes in one act.

kh-d-d: the cheek, a furrow, a trench; ukhdūd, the dug pit in which the People of the Ditch were burned for their faith (85:4). The skeleton is the long groove cut in a surface, the furrow of a cheek and the trench in the ground; the image is precise, a fire fed in a cleft dug for the killing.

kh-ṣ-m: to contend at law, an adversary; khaṣīm, "he is a manifest adversary" (16:4). The skeleton is the litigant, the one who stands against you in dispute; the same word names the disputant and the dispute, the contention and the one who carries it.

kh-l-q: to create, and disposition, nature; khuluq, the "magnificent character" of 68:4, and ikhtilāq, the fabrication charged against the revelation (38:7). The buried image is the measuring and cutting of leather to a proportion (khalaqa l-adīm); to create is to fashion by exact measure, and to fabricate a lie is the same craft turned to a false cut.

d-ʿ-w: to call, to summon, to claim; daʿiyy, the adopted son, the one called to a father not his own (33:5), and the offices of guidance that "call to the good." The raw sense is the raised voice that summons; the adopted son is a sonship by calling-out rather than by blood, a name conferred by the voice.

dh-k-r: to remember, to mention; dhikr, the reminder the bearers carry. The skeleton holds also the male (dhakar) and the sharp point; remembrance is the keen edge of the mind that pierces back to what was, and mention the bringing of it again to the tongue.

dh-n-b: an offense, a fault; dhanb, the fault the Prophet ﷺ is told to seek covering for (40:55). The raw image is the tail (dhanab), the part that trails behind; a fault is what drags after the deed, the consequence following at the heel like a tail.

r-ʾ-f: tender kindness, pity; raʾūf, a divine name applied to the Prophet ﷺ (9:128). The skeleton is the softest pity, the tenderness that flinches at another's pain; it is the gentlest grade of mercy, compassion at its most yielding.

r-j-l: a man, the adult male; rajul, "two men of those who feared God" (5:23). The raw image is the foot and the leg (rijl); the man is named the one who stands and goes on his own legs, manhood as the upright gait.

r-ḥ-m: the womb, mercy; raḥma and raḥīm, and al-Raḥmān and al-Raḥīm. The buried picture is the womb (raḥim) itself; mercy in the skeleton is womb-feeling, the sheltering and feeding of the utterly dependent, named from the organ that holds and nourishes the unformed.

r-s-l: to send, to dispatch, to let flow; rasūl, the sent one. The skeleton is the loosing of a thing to go, a herd sent to water or hair let down loose (irsāl); the messenger is one released toward a destination by a sender, the sending never his own.

r-sh-d: right direction, sound judgment; rushd, the "right conduct" the jinn heard the recitation guide to (72:2). The raw image is holding to the true road and the maturity that can hold it (rushd, the coming of age); guidance here is the path kept and the judgment grown firm enough to keep it.

r-ḍ-w: to be content, to approve; irtaḍā, the messenger God "has approved" to whom alone the unseen is disclosed (72:27). The skeleton is satisfaction, the settling of the self in consent; approval is the will at rest in a thing, wanting it as it is.

r-f-d: to support, to provision; rifāda, the hereditary office of feeding the pilgrims at Mecca, a charge of the Prophet's ﷺ clan. The skeleton is the propping of a thing and the giving of aid (rafada, to support); the provisioning of pilgrims is a holding-up of the weary traveler, sustenance offered as a prop under one who would otherwise fall.

r-f-q: gentleness, ease, a companion; rafīq, the companion, and the rafīq al-aʿlā, the highest company of the prophets the Prophet ﷺ chose at his death. The skeleton is the easy and the kind, the elbow (rifq) one leans on; a companion is one whose nearness is a gentleness, the soft handling that makes a hard road bearable.

r-m-y: to throw, to cast; ramā, "you did not throw when you threw, but God threw" (8:17). The raw sense is the bodily hurl, the arm's release of a stone or shaft; the verse takes the most physical of acts and names its force as God's.

r-w-ḥ: spirit, breath, wind; rūḥ, named of Jesus (AS), "a Spirit from Him" (4:171), of the Holy Spirit, and of the revelation, "a Spirit of Our command" (42:52). The skeleton is moving air, breath and wind and the cool of relief (rāḥa); spirit in the root is the unseen breath that animates, known only by its motion.

z-k-w: to grow, to be pure, almsgiving; zakāt, the purifying due paid from wealth, and tazkiyah, the cleansing of the soul. The skeleton joins growth and purity on one frame (zakā, to thrive and to be clean); the alms is named for both, a giving that purifies what remains and a pruning that makes the wealth and the giver grow.

z-w-j: a pair, a spouse, a yoke; zawj, the wife kept or released in the trial of the household (33:37). The raw image is the yoking of two, a pair coupled to pull together; a spouse is one of a matched two, joined to a single draught.

z-y-d: to increase, to add; ziyādah, an addition, the increase God gives beyond the measure earned. The skeleton is the surplus laid onto a thing, the growth past the original sum; the root names the more that is added, the increment that exceeds, the giving that goes beyond the due.

s-ḥ-r: sorcery, enchantment; siḥr, the label "a manifest sorcerer" (10:2) and the bewitchment marked as the contested occasion of the refuge sūras. The raw sense is to turn a thing from its true nature to a false seeming, kin to saḥar, the turning-edge of night into dawn; magic in the skeleton is a diverting, the real bent into a counterfeit of itself.

s-r-y: to travel by night; Isrāʾ, the night journey of the Prophet ﷺ from Mecca to Jerusalem (17:1). The skeleton is the journeying done in darkness, movement under cover of night while the world sleeps; the night journey is named for its hour, a passage made in the dark and known only after.

s-ṭ-r: to rule lines, to write in rows, to dominate; muṣayṭir, "you are not over them a controller" (88:22). The raw image is the drawn line and the written row (saṭr); to dominate is to rule lines over another, to set them within margins one has drawn.

s-q-y: to give to drink, to water; siqāya, the hereditary office of providing water to the pilgrims at Mecca. The skeleton is the giving of water to the thirsty, the irrigation of a dry field; the office is named for the most basic mercy in a waterless land, the cup held out to the parched.

s-l-m: peace, safety, surrender; salm, the peace to be inclined to "if they incline to it" (8:61), and islām and salām. The buried image is wholeness and soundness (salima, to be unhurt, entire); peace is the body intact, and to surrender is to hand oneself over whole into safekeeping.

s-y-r: to journey, to go, conduct; sīra, the path of a life and so the record of the Prophet's ﷺ life, and sayr, travel. The skeleton is the going of a traveler along a road (sāra, to move along); a life's sīra is its course and its conduct, the way a man walked through his days, the journey that, once told, becomes the life-story.

sh-r-ḥ: to open, to expand, to lay open; sharaḥa, "did We not expand for you your breast" (94:1). The raw sense is to slit and splay wide, to lay a thing open as one opens meat to the bone; the expansion of the breast is a cutting-wide of the inner space to receive.

sh-ʿ-b: a mountain pass, a people, a branch; shiʿb, the valley quarter of Abū Ṭālib in which the clan was penned during the boycott, and shaʿb, a people. The skeleton holds both gathering and dividing on one frame, the pass that draws a road into one narrow throat and the branching of a people into clans; the shiʿb of the boycott is the narrow place the clan was driven into and hemmed.

sh-ʿ-r: to perceive finely, hair, poetry; shāʿir, the "poet" the Prophet ﷺ was called (21:5). The skeleton is the hair (shaʿr) and the fine sensing it stands for (shaʿara, to feel acutely); the poet is named the one of hair-fine perception, and the charge of poetry sets mere fine feeling against revealed knowledge.

sh-f-ʿ: to pair, to make even, to intercede; shafāʿa, the intercession that holds only "by His leave" (2:255). The raw image is the even number (shafʿ) against the odd, the adding of a second to a one; to intercede is to come alongside as a second, pairing one's plea to another's need.

sh-y-ʾ: to will, to want; mashīʾa, the will of God excepted into every plan, "except that God wills" (18:24). The skeleton is bare volition, the wanting that brings a thing toward being; the correction of the Prophet's ﷺ promise binds his every "I will" beneath this larger willing.

sh-y-ʿ: to spread, to follow in a party; shiyaʿ, the "sects" into which the one religion was cut (6:65). The raw image is diffusion and accompaniment, a thing spread abroad and a band that walks together; a sect is a following spread off on its own line, splinter and company at once.

ṣ-d-q: to be true, to confirm; muṣaddiq, the Quran "confirming what was before it" (3:3). The skeleton joins truth and hardness; the true is the solid that bears weight, and to confirm is to stand under what came before and hold it up; and the ṣiddīq, the wholly truthful, the title borne by Abū Bakr, is the one in whom truth has set hard.

ḍ-r-r: to harm, injury; ḍirār, the mosque raised to do harm and split the believers (9:107), and ḍarar, hurt. The skeleton is the bare infliction of damage, a thing pressed against another to its hurt; the root names both the harm done and the intent to do it, the wound and the will behind it.

ḍ-ʿ-f: weakness, and the doubling of a thing; ḍaʿīf, the weak, the believers without clan to shield them. The skeleton holds two senses on one frame, frailty (ḍaʿufa, to be weak) and multiplication (ḍiʿf, a double); the weak is the one easily bent, and the reward promised the patient is the same root turned to increase, the deed doubled.

ḍ-y-q: to be narrow, constricted; ḍāqa, the breast that "tightens" under denial (11:12). The raw image is the strait and the pinched, space closing in until it grips; distress in the skeleton is room running out, the chest narrowed to a clench.

ṭ-ʿ-m: to taste, to eat, food; ṭaʿām, the objection that the messenger "eats food and walks the markets" (25:7). The skeleton is the bare act of tasting and the food tasted; the adversaries press it as proof of mere creatureliness, the mouth that must be fed.

ẓ-h-r: the back, to appear, midday; ẓihār, the old repudiation that likened the wife to "the back of the mother" (58:2). The raw image is the back (ẓahr), the surface turned and shown and the strength that bears a load; the same root gives the manifest (ẓāhir) and the noon (ẓuhr), the outer face fully lit.

ʿ-b-d: to serve, to worship; ʿabd, the servant, the title chosen for the Prophet ﷺ at the summit of the ascent (17:1). The raw image is the smoothed and trodden path (ṭarīq muʿabbad), ground made firm by being walked; the servant is one shaped to a road, level under the foot of his Lord.

ʿ-b-s: to frown, to scowl; ʿabasa, "he frowned and turned away" (80:1). The skeleton is the knit brow itself, the face drawn tight in displeasure; the sūra opens on the bare physical act, the muscles of the face indicted before the deed is named.

ʿ-r-j: to ascend, to mount, to limp; Miʿrāj, the ascension of the Prophet ﷺ through the heavens, and maʿārij, the ways of ascent (70:3). The skeleton is the going-up by stages and the unevenness of a limping gait (ʿaraja, to climb and to limp); the ascent is a mounting through ranks, a rising by steps from the earth toward the throne.

ʿ-ṣ-m: to grasp, to hold fast, to protect; the verb in "God will protect you from the people" (5:67). The buried picture is the handhold seized and the rope clung to (iʿtaṣama bi-, to grip fast); protection in the skeleton is not a wall but a grip, the firm hold that keeps one from falling.

ʿ-k-f: to cleave to, to devote, to seclude; iʿtikāf, the withdrawal into the mosque in seclusion to seek the Night of Power, the Prophet's ﷺ retreat in the last ten days of the fast. The skeleton is the staying-fixed beside a thing, a constant bending over it (ʿakafa, to keep to); the retreat is a fastening of the self to one place and one purpose, the world shut out and the devotion held.

ʿ-m-r: to live long, to inhabit, to build; ʿumr, the lifespan by which God swears and against which a life is weighed. The raw image is the filling of a place with life (ʿimāra, to cultivate and people a land); a lifespan is a span inhabited, the years filled as one fills a house.

ʿ-w-dh: to seek refuge, to take shelter; istiʿādha, and the muʿawwidhatān, the two sūras of refuge (113-114). The raw sense is to cling to a thing for safety, to fasten oneself to a protector; to seek refuge is to grip God as a frightened creature grips what shelters it.

gh-z-w: to raid, to strive toward, a campaign; maghāzī, the military expeditions of the Prophet ﷺ, the part of the sira that records them. The skeleton is the setting-out against a thing with intent (ghazā, to raid); a campaign is a purposed going-forth, the march toward an aim that must be met in the field.

gh-f-r: to cover, to forgive; istighfār, the seeking of forgiveness commanded at the summit (110:3), and al-Ghafūr. The raw image is the covering-over, kin to the mighfar, the helmet that covers the head; forgiveness in the skeleton is a covering laid over the fault, hidden from sight rather than unmade.

gh-y-b: the hidden, the absent; ghayb, the unseen whose keys are God's alone (6:59). The skeleton is absence from sight, what has sunk below the horizon (ghābat al-shams, the sun set); the unseen is the withdrawn, present but past the edge of the eye.

f-t-ḥ: to open, to seek victory; istiftāḥ, the People of the Book who "had prayed for victory" by him (2:89), and al-Fattāḥ. The raw image is the opening of a closed door and the taking of a closed city; victory in the skeleton is an opening, the shut thing forced wide.

f-t-n: trial, temptation; fitna, the danger the firmness guarded against (17:73). The buried picture is the smelting of gold in fire to part it from its dross (fatana l-dhahab); a trial is an assaying by burning, the heat that proves what a thing is made of.

f-r-q: to split, to part, to distinguish; furqa, the "division" of the one community (42:14), and al-Furqān, the criterion. The raw image is the cleaving of a thing in two, the parting of the sea and the parting-line of the hair (farq); to distinguish is to cut between, and schism is the cut turned upon the body it should have served.

f-r-y: to fabricate, to invent falsely; iftirāʾ, the charge of forging a lie against God (69:44). The raw sense is the cutting of leather to shape (farā l-adīm); fabrication is a real craft of cutting and joining, but a thing cut to a false pattern and passed as given.

f-ḍ-l: to exceed, to favor, to grant surplus; faḍḍala, "We favored some of the prophets above others" (2:253). The skeleton is the overplus, the remainder above the measure (faḍl, what exceeds need); favor is a surplus poured beyond the due, more given than was owed.

f-ṭ-r: to split open, to originate, the primordial nature; fiṭra, the disposition upon which God shaped mankind, the innate turning toward Him (30:30), and Fāṭir, the Originator. The skeleton is the first splitting, the cleft from which a thing issues (faṭara, to cleave); creation is a breaking-open, and the fiṭra is the original cut, the native shape pressed into a thing at its making.

q-b-l: to face, to turn toward, to accept; qibla, the direction of prayer turned for him (2:144). The raw image is the front and the facing (qubul); to accept is to turn the face toward, and the qibla is the chosen face the whole body fronts in prayer.

q-r-b: to be near; qurbā, kinship (42:23), qurbān, the offering brought near (5:27), and qāba, the measure of nearness in the vision (53:9). The skeleton is approach, the closing of distance; kinship is nearness of blood, an offering a nearness sought by gift, and the vision's "two bows' length" the nearest a creature is brought.

q-ṭ-ʿ: to cut, to sever; taqaṭṭuʿ, the religion "cut into pieces" among them (21:93). The raw sense is the clean severing, a thing chopped through; the schism is named the dismemberment of one body, cut into parts that no longer hold.

q-w-m: to stand, to rise; maqām, the "praised station" of the Prophet ﷺ (17:79). The raw image is the upright posture, the rising to one's feet; a station is a standing-place, a rank named for where one stands and how high.

q-y-l: the midday rest; qaylūlah, the noonday sleep of the Arabs in the heat, the hour the Prophet ﷺ kept. The skeleton is the stilling of the day at its height, the pause when the sun stands and motion stops; the root names the rest taken in the white heat of noon, the body laid down while the light blazes.

k-th-r: to be much, to abound; kawthar, the "abundance" granted against the taunt of being cut off (108:1). The skeleton is sheer multitude, number past counting; the gift is named the overflowing, set exactly against the enemy's charge of barrenness.

k-f-l: to take charge of, a portion, a double share; kifl, "a portion" of the burden or the reward (4:85). The raw image is the part folded back on itself, a double fold (kifl); a portion is a fold of the whole assigned to one, charge and share at once.

k-l-m: to speak, a word, a wound; kalima, the Word predicated of Jesus (AS) (4:171), and kalām, the speech of God. The buried picture is the wound (kalm) that breaks a surface; a word in the skeleton is a thing that cuts and marks, speech that leaves an incision on the air and the ear.

m-w-t: death, stillness; mawt, "you will die and they will die" (39:30). The skeleton is the going-still, the cessation of motion and breath; death in the root is the stop, the frame fallen quiet, and the Prophet ﷺ is held under it with all that breathe.

n-b-ʾ: to announce, to bring weighty news; nabī, the prophet, and by the alternate root n-b-w, to be raised high. The raw image is the report of consequence carried from afar (nabaʾ, grave tidings); the prophet is the bearer of the weighty announcement, or the one raised up to bear it.

n-dh-r: to warn, to vow; nadhīr, the warner (35:24). The skeleton is the cry of alarm raised before a coming harm and the binding of a vow; to warn is to give notice of what advances, the voice thrown ahead of the danger.

n-ṣ-r: to help against an enemy, to defend, to give victory; intiṣār, the believing poets who "defend themselves after they were wronged" (26:227), naṣr, and Anṣār, the Helpers of Medina who took the emigrants into their homes. The raw image holds also the rain that revives a dead land; help in the skeleton is a rescue sent down, aid that comes to the hard-pressed from beyond their own strength.

n-f-q: to dissemble, to spend, a tunnel; munāfiq, the hypocrite hidden from the Prophet's ﷺ sight (9:101), and nifāq. The buried image is the jerboa's burrow with a second hidden mouth (nāfiqāʾ), the escape-hole kept covered until it bolts; the hypocrite is the creature that shows one entrance and keeps another to flee through.

n-w-r: light; nūr, the light by which God guides, named of the revelation and of God Himself, light upon light. The skeleton is the bare radiance that makes seeing possible, the fire's gleam and the day's brightness; light in the root is what dispels the dark and shows the road, the first condition of sight, and the book that guides is named by it.

h-j-r: to abandon, to migrate, to shun; hijra, the migration from Mecca to Medina that opens the calendar, and muhājirūn, the emigrants. The skeleton is the cutting of a tie and the leaving of a place (hajara, to forsake); the migration is a severance before it is a journey, the abandonment of home and clan for the sake of the bond that replaces them.

h-d-y: to guide, to lead the way; hadā and hudā, the guidance the Quran keeps wholly open (2:185). The raw image is leading along a road and the gift offered with the right hand (hadiyya); guidance is a leading-forward and a thing given, the way shown and the hand held out on it.

w-ʾ-d: to bury alive; waʾd, the burying alive of infant daughters in the age before the call, waʾd al-banāt, which the recitation drags into the light to condemn (81:8). The skeleton is the pressing-down of a living thing under earth, a weight laid on the buried; the root names the specific killing, the daughter set in the ground while she still breathes.

w-t-n: the aorta, the life-vein; watīn, the great vessel the false prophet would have severed from him (69:46). The skeleton is the single cord on which life hangs, the artery whose cutting empties the body; the image makes the threatened end exact and bodily.

w-d-d: to love, to feel affection; mawadda, the "affection of kinship" asked as the only wage (42:23), and al-Wadūd. The skeleton is settled tender love, the wishing-well that holds steady; it is affection as a fixed disposition, not the flare but the constant warmth.

w-z-n: to weigh, the balance; mīzān, the Scale on which the deeds are weighed at the crossing (21:47), and wazn, weight. The skeleton is the act of weighing, the pair of pans hung to find the truth of a mass; the Balance is the deed brought to its exact measure, the weight of a life read off against the standard, nothing added and nothing lost.

w-s-w-s: to whisper, to insinuate; waswās, the "slinking whisperer" sought refuge from (114:4). The word is its own sound, a low hidden hiss repeated, the rustle of a garment or the chink of ornaments; temptation in the skeleton is named by its noise, the faint susurrus at the edge of hearing.

w-q-y: to guard, to shield; taqwā, the God-wariness that shields the self by keeping it true, the being God-conscious. The skeleton is the placing of a guard between a thing and what would harm it (waqā, to protect); taqwā is the wariness that sets a shield before the soul, the watchfulness that keeps one from the edge.

y-d: the hand; yad, "the hand of God is over their hands" (48:10). The skeleton is the hand itself and, from it, power and a favor done (a yad, a benefaction given by hand); the hand in the root is agency made visible, the grip by which a thing is held and a gift is passed.

# REFERENCES

## The Primary Documentary Sources

The reconstruction rests on the primary Arabic corpus named below, set down here as the documentary foundation of the work. The Qur'an is the first anchor; the nine canonical hadith collections and the early Sira and Maghāzī literature are the transmitted record from which the connected life is drawn.

**The Qur'an.** The ʿUthmānic codex, al-muṣḥaf al-ʿUthmānī, in the canonical reading of Ḥafṣ ʿan ʿĀṣim.

**The Hadith, the nine books, al-Kutub al-Tisʿa.**

- Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, of Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl al-Bukhārī (d. 256 AH / 870 CE)
- Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, of Muslim ibn al-Ḥajjāj al-Naysābūrī (d. 261 / 875)
- Sunan Abī Dāwūd, of Abū Dāwūd al-Sijistānī (d. 275 / 889)
- Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī, with Al-Shamāʾil al-Muḥammadiyya, of Muḥammad ibn ʿĪsā al-Tirmidhī (d. 279 / 892)
- Sunan al-Nasāʾī, of Aḥmad ibn Shuʿayb al-Nasāʾī (d. 303 / 915)
- Sunan Ibn Mājah, of Muḥammad ibn Yazīd ibn Mājah (d. 273 / 887)
- Musnad Aḥmad, of Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal (d. 241 / 855)
- Muwaṭṭaʾ Mālik, of Mālik ibn Anas (d. 179 / 795)
- Sunan al-Dārimī, of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Dārimī (d. 255 / 869)

**The Sira and Maghāzī, the early biographical and campaign literature.**

- Sīrat Rasūl Allāh, of Ibn Isḥāq (d. 150 / 767), in the recension of Ibn Hishām (d. 218 / 833)
- Kitāb al-Maghāzī, of al-Wāqidī (d. 207 / 823)
- Al-Ṭabaqāt al-Kubrā, of Ibn Saʿd (d. 230 / 845)
- Tārīkh al-Rusul wa'l-Mulūk, of al-Ṭabarī (d. 310 / 923)
- Dalāʾil al-Nubuwwa, of al-Bayhaqī (d. 458 / 1066)

The external witnesses that stand outside this corpus, the Ḥimyarite and Aksumite inscriptions, the texts of Abraha at Marib and Murayghan, and the Syriac, Greek, and Persian records of the surrounding empires, are named in their places in the narrative above.

## The Translations Consulted

English wording was checked against the following standard translations, consulted for phrasing only. The renderings given in this study are the author's own, prepared directly from the Arabic.

Pickthall, Marmaduke. 1930. The Meaning of the Glorious Koran.

Yusuf Ali, Abdullah. 1934-1938. The Holy Qurʾan: Text, Translation and Commentary.

Arberry, A. J. 1955. The Koran Interpreted. London: George Allen and Unwin.

Abdel Haleem, M. A. S. 2004. The Qurʾan: A New Translation. Oxford: Oxford University Press.
